The relativistic Euler equations: ESI notes on their geo-analytic structures and implications for shocks in 1 ​ D 1D and multi-dimensions Thanks: ∗ Vanderbilt University, Nashville, TN, USA. leonardo.abbrescia@vanderbilt.edu Thanks: ∗∗ Vanderbilt University, Nashville, TN, USA. jared.speck@vanderbilt.edu Thanks: † LA gratefully acknowledges support from an NSF Postdoctoral Fellowship. Thanks: †† JS gratefully acknowledges support from NSF grant # DMS-2054184 and NSF CAREER grant # DMS-1914537.
Leonardo Abbrescia ∗† and Jared Speck ∗∗††

Abstract
. In this article, we provide notes that complement the lectures on the relativistic Euler equations and shocks that were given by the second author at the program Mathematical Perspectives of Gravitation Beyond the Vacuum Regime , which was hosted by the Erwin Schrödinger International Institute for Mathematics and Physics in Vienna in February, 2022. We set the stage by introducing a standard first-order formulation of the relativistic Euler equations and providing a brief overview of local well-posedness in Sobolev spaces. Then, using Riemann invariants, we provide the first detailed construction of a localized subset of the maximal globally hyperbolic developments of an open set of initially smooth, shock-forming isentropic solutions in 1 ​ D 1D , with a focus on describing the singular boundary and the Cauchy horizon that emerges from the singularity. Next, we provide an overview of the new second-order formulation of the 3 ​ D 3D relativistic Euler equations derived in [ 41 ] , its rich geometric and analytic structures, their implications for the mathematical theory of shock waves, and their connection to the setup we use in our 1 ​ D 1D analysis of shocks. We then highlight some key prior results on the study of shock formation and related problems. Furthermore, we provide an overview of how the formulation of the flow derived in [ 41 ] can be used to study shock formation in multiple spatial dimensions. Finally, we discuss various open problems tied to shocks. Keywords : acoustical metric, Cauchy horizon, eikonal function, genuinely nonlinear, hyperbolic conservation law, maximal globally hyperbolic development, nonlinear geometric optics, null form, Riemann invariant, shocks, singularities, singular boundary, stable blowup, wave breaking Mathematics Subject Classification (2020) Primary: 35L67; Secondary: 35M11, 35L65, 35L81, 76L05, 76Y05



August 8, 2026

1.
Introduction

In this article, we provide notes that complement the lectures on
the relativistic Euler equations and shocks that were given by the second author at
the program
Mathematical Perspectives of Gravitation Beyond the Vacuum Regime
,
which was hosted by the Erwin Schrödinger International Institute for Mathematics and Physics
in Vienna in February, 2022. Broadly speaking, our main goals are the following:

(1)

To briefly introduce the equations.

(2)

To provide an overview of the new formulation of the equations
derived in
[
41
]
.

(3)

To describe the implications of the formulation from
[
41
]

for the study of multi-dimensional shocks.

Although our main interest is solutions in
3D

without
symmetry assumptions,
we highlight Sect.
3
, in which we rigorously study a family of initially smooth, simple isentropic shock-forming

plane-symmetric
solutions and give a complete description of a portion of their maximal (classical) globally hyperbolic developments
(MGHD for short), up to the boundary.
To the best of our knowledge,
this is the first article
on
any
quasilinear hyperbolic system in
1D

that provides the detailed construction
of the MGHD within the vicinity of a shock singularity.
Sect.
3
in particular
provides an introduction to some of the main ideas behind the study of shock formation
in a gentle, semi-explicit
1D
setting, where energy estimates are not needed.
Roughly, the MGHD is the largest possible classical solution + globally hyperbolic
region that is launched by the initial data,
and when it exists and is unique
(for some solutions to some hyperbolic PDEs, it is not unique – see Sect.
5.5
!),
it is the holy grail object, at least from the point of view of classical solution.

We stress outright that, although the relativistic Euler equations are posed on an ambient Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
, from the point of view of the causal structure of the fluid,
the correct notion of “globally hyperbolic”
is not with respect to the spacetime metric
\mathbf{g}
, but rather
with respect to the acoustical metric
\mathbf{h}
, introduced in
Def.
2.4
. To avoid confusion, we will refer to this as
\mathbf{h}
-global hyperbolicity and the corresponding
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD.

Many of the techniques and geometric insights behind the modern approach to studying
shocks have roots in mathematical General Relativity,
notably the celebrated proof by Christodoulou–Klainerman

[
28
]
of the nonlinear stability of Minkowski spacetime as a solution to Einstein’s equations.
A key unifying theme between the global existence result
[
28
]
and the results on shocks that we discuss here is that

nonlinear geometric optics
, implemented via an
eikonal function

U
, plays a central role in the study of the flow.
We will discuss eikonal functions in detail
in Sects.
3
and
6
.

We highlight a key advantage of the formulation of the flow derived in
[
41
]
.

For general solutions (in
3D
, i.e., three spatial dimensions),
it allows one to implement a sharp version of nonlinear geometric optics.
Nonlinear geometric optics is crucial for the study of multi-dimensional shocks,
and it has other applications, such as low-regularity well-posedness
(see Sect.
2.3.5
).

In line with points (1)–(3) above, this article is meant to give an introduction to the mathematical techniques and methods needed for the PDE analysis through the lens of nonlinear geometric optics. A comprehensive topical review of the rich history of shock singularities in the framework of hyperbolic conservation laws would encompass a work of strictly larger order of magnitude. We refer the reader to

[
39
,
18
,
49
]
and the references therein for a good start.

1.1.
Motivation, context, and the structure of the article

1.1.1.
Mathematical and physical motivations

The relativistic Euler equations are an important matter model in mathematical General Relativity.
For example, relativistic fluids are often used in cosmology to model the average energy-matter
content of spacetime, and they in particular play a central role in the Standard Model of cosmology.

For a non-exhaustive yet comprehensive overview of these physical applications, we refer the reader to the texts

[
78
,
97
,
100
]
, and the references therein. There has also been exciting rigorous mathematical progress on the
global structure of solutions when the spacetime is expanding

[
84
,
77
,
44
,
43
,
91
]
. We also highlight

[
33
]
and
[
34
]
*Chapter 1 for a modern introduction to
the relativistic Euler equations and their connections to the laws of mechanics and thermodynamics.

Despite the above remarks,
the rigorous mathematical theory of multi-dimensional solutions is far from complete. Even if one considers the relativistic Euler equations
on a given spacetime background (i.e., without coupling to Einstein’s equations),
there are many mathematically rich phenomena that have not been fully understood.
Chief among these is the fundamental issue,
going back to Riemann’s foundational work
[
83
]

on non-relativistic compressible fluids in
1D
,
that initially smooth solutions can develop shock singularities in finite time.
Shocks are singularities such that various fluid variables’ gradients blow up in finite time
(in a precise, controlled fashion, as it turns out), though the fluid variables themselves (such as the velocity and density)
remain bounded. This phenomenon is also known as
wave breaking
.
The relatively mild nature of the singularity gives rise to the hope that one might
-- at least for short times --
be able to uniquely continue the solution weakly
past the ‘‘first singularity,
1
1

1

In multi-dimensions, the correct notion of
“first singularity” is not a point in spacetime, but rather a co-dimension
2
submanifold of points;
see Sect.
5.5
.
”
subject to suitable selection criteria, typically
in the form of Rankine–Hugoniot-type jump conditions across a shock hypersurface
(which is not known in advance) and an entropy-type condition.
This weak continuation problem is known as the
shock development problem
, and in full generality, it remains open, for both the
3D

relativistic Euler equations and their non-relativistic analog, i.e., the
3D
compressible Euler equations;
see Sect.
5.6
for further discussion. The long-term goal is certainly the following:

Develop a rigorous
global-in-time-and-space

theory of existence and uniqueness for open sets of multi-dimensional solutions
that are allowed to transition from classical to weak due to shock formation,
and describe the interactions of different
shock hypersurfaces, Cauchy horizons (see Sect.
3.8
),
and other crucial qualitative features of the flow.

This goal is far out of reach as of present. If one could accomplish it even in a single perturbative regime,
that would represent truly remarkable progress.

While there have been many works on shock formation for the multi-dimensional non-relativistic compressible
Euler equations,
such as
[
30
,
92
,
87
,
65
,
67
,
24
,
23
,
22
,
21
,
4
,
3
,
1
]
,
there have been relatively few works on multi-dimensional shock formation for the relativistic Euler equations.
The most notable work in the relativistic case is Christodoulou’s breakthrough monograph
[
34
]
,
in which he proved shock formation and studied some aspects of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD for
open sets of irrotational and isentropic solutions
in
3D
that are compactly supported perturbations of non-vacuum constant fluid states.
Unlike the non-relativistic case, there are currently no rigorous results that prove multi-dimensional shock formation
for the relativistic Euler equations in the presence of vorticity and entropy.
However, in Sect.
6
, we provide a blueprint for how one can use the equations of

[
41
]
and the analytic framework of
[
34
,
92
,
87
,
65
,
67
,
4
,
1
]

to prove stable shock formation in the
3D
relativistic case for open sets of solutions with vorticity and entropy.

The remainder of the article is organized as follows:

•

In Sect.
2
, we set up the study of the relativistic Euler equations and provide an overview
of basic ingredients that play a role in the proof of local well-posedness in Sobolev spaces.
In particular, we introduce the
acoustical metric

\mathbf{h}
, which is the solution-dependent
Lorentzian metric that drives the propagation of sound waves;
the acoustical metric is fundamental for implementing nonlinear geometric optics and studying multi-dimensional shocks.
While we state the equations in the case of an arbitrary spacetime background
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
,
the vast majority of the article concerns the case in which
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{1+3}

and
\mathbf{g}=\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
is the Minkowski metric on
\mathcal{M}
.
We again point out that nearly all notions of hyperbolicity will refer to the acoustical metric
\mathbf{h}
and not
\mathbf{g}
or
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
;
see also Sect.
4.2.5
and Remark
6.1
.

•

In Sect.
3
, to help readers gain intuition for the subsequent discussion,
we study the flow in one spatial dimension, which is equivalent to studying plane-symmetric solutions in
3D
.
In particular, in Theorem
3.1
,
we provide a detailed analysis of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD for a large set of shock-forming relativistic Euler solutions in
1D
.
Some aspects of the theorem, notably the results concerning the formation of Cauchy horizons,
have not been proved in detail elsewhere in the literature. Although
Theorem
3.1
specifically concerns the relativistic Euler equations,
the techniques could be applied to a wide variety of strictly hyperbolic genuinely nonlinear
PDEs in one spatial dimension.

•

In Sect.
4
, we provide an overview of
the new formulation of the
3D
relativistic Euler equations derived in
[
41
]
.

•

In Sect.
5
, we describe some previous works on shock formation and related problems.

•

In Sect.
6
,
based in part on our prior experience
[
92
,
87
,
65
,
67
,
4
,
1
]

in studying shock formation in multi-dimensional non-relativistic compressible fluids,
we provide an overview of how the new formulation of the flow from
[
41
]

can be used to study shock formation for open sets of solutions to the
3D

relativistic Euler equations.
As of present, rigorous fluid shock formation
results in multi-dimensions with vorticity and entropy
have been proved only for the non-relativistic compressible Euler equations.
Nonetheless, from the perspective of shock formation, the relativistic Euler equations and
the non-relativistic compressible Euler equations enjoy many structural commonalities,
and we expect that the overview we provide in Sect.
6
could be turned
(with substantial effort) into a complete proof.

•

In Sect.
7
, we describe various open problems.

2.
Standard formulations of the relativistic Euler equations and local well-posedness

In this section, we introduce some standard first-order formulations of the relativistic Euler equations.
We also introduce the acoustical metric
\mathbf{h}
and set up a corresponding
geometric version of the energy method that applies to a first-order formulation of the flow.
Finally, we state Prop.
2.3
, which is a standard result on local well-posedness,
and briefly discuss how its proof is connected to the energy method.

2.1.
The basic setup

We start by discussing standard formulations of the relativistic Euler equations on
an arbitrary four-dimensional Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
,
where
\mathbf{g}
is the spacetime metric of signature
(-,+,+,+)
.
Our discussion in this section is motivated by Christodoulou’s
presentation in
[
34
]
*Chapter 1.
We are somewhat terse in our presentation here, and thus we refer readers to
[
34
]
*Chapter 1 for additional details.

Convention 2.1

(Moving indices with
\mathbf{g}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
)
.

In this section, we will lower and raise indices with the spacetime metric
\mathbf{g}

and its inverse
\mathbf{g}^{-1}

respectively, i.e.,
\mathbf{u}_{\alpha}:=\mathbf{g}_{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{u}^{\beta}

and
\upxi^{\alpha}:=(\mathbf{g}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}\upxi_{\beta}
. Similar comments apply to the bulk of the article,
once we fix
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{1+3}
and
\mathbf{g}=\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
as the Minkowski metric on
\mathbb{R}^{1+3}
;
see Sect.
2.2.1
.
This will become especially important later on, when we introduce the acoustical metric
\mathbf{h}
, i.e.,
we do
not
raise or lower indices with
\mathbf{h}
or
\mathbf{h}^{-1}
.

2.1.1.
The basic fluid variables and relations

We now introduce the basic fluid variables that will play a role in our study of the relativistic Euler equations:

\uprho:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
denotes the fluid’s (proper) energy density,

p:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
denotes the fluid pressure,

s:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
denotes the entropy per particle (“entropy” for short from now on),

n:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
denotes the proper number density,

\uptheta:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
denotes the temperature,

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle:=\frac{\uprho+p}{n}

(1)

denotes the enthalpy per particle,
and
\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}
denotes the fluid’s four-velocity,
which is a future-directed vectorfield on
\mathcal{M}
normalized by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=-1.

(2)

In the rest of the paper,

\displaystyle\overline{H}

\displaystyle>0

(3)

denotes an arbitrary fixed, positive value of
H
; we find it convenient to use
H
to normalize
various constructions.

We find it convenient to work with the natural log of the enthalpy.

Definition 2.1

(Logarithmic enthalpy)
.

We define
the (dimensionless) logarithmic enthalpy
h
as follows:

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle:=\ln\left(H/\overline{H}\right).

(4)

Definition 2.2

(The quantity
q
)
.

We define the quantity
q
as follows:

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle:=\frac{\uptheta}{H}.

(5)

2.1.2.
A first statement of the relativistic Euler equations

Relative to arbitrary coordinates,
the relativistic Euler equations on
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
can be expressed as
the following system of
quasilinear hyperbolic conservation laws
:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\kappa}\mathbf{T}^{\alpha\kappa}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle(\alpha

\displaystyle=0,1,2,3),

(6a)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\kappa}(n\mathbf{u}^{\kappa})

\displaystyle=0,

(6b)

where
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of the spacetime metric
\mathbf{g}
and:

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle:=(\uprho+p)\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}\mathbf{u}^{\beta}+p(\mathbf{g}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta

\displaystyle=0,1,2,3),

(7)

is the energy-momentum tensor of the fluid.
It turns out that under additional assumptions discussed in Sect.
2.1.3
,
equation (
2
) can be viewed as a constraint that is preserved by
the flow of the PDEs (
6a
); see
equation (
15
) and the discussion surrounding it.
We also emphasize that later on, we will discuss several other formulations of the flow.

2.1.3.
Equation of state and thermodynamic relations

The system (
2
)
+
(
6a
)–(
6b
)
+
(
7
)
is not
closed because there are too many fluid variables and not enough equations.
The standard path to closing the system
begins with an
equation of state
, that is, an assumed
functional relationship of the form
p=p(\uprho,s)
.
The basic hyperbolicity of the equations will require that

speed of sound

c
,
defined by:

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{\frac{\partial p}{\partial\uprho}\left|\right._{s}},

(8)

should be real and non-negative,
where
\frac{\partial p}{\partial\uprho}\left|\right._{s}

denotes the partial derivative of
p
(i.e., of the equation of state)
with respect to
\uprho
at fixed
s
.
The vanishing of
c
causes a severe degeneracy in the system,
and we will therefore restrict our attention to
solution regimes in which:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<c\leq 1,

(9)

where we have normalized our setup so that the speed of light is unity
(i.e., the second inequality in (
9
) implies that the speed
of sound is less than or equal to the speed of light).
We also restrict our attention to solutions such that:

\displaystyle\uprho

\displaystyle>0,\,p>0,\,n>0,\,\uptheta>0,\,H>0.

(10)

The laws of thermodynamics demand that the fluid variables satisfy the following functional relations:

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\uprho}{\partial n}\left|{}_{s}\right.,

\displaystyle\uptheta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial\uprho}{\partial s}\left|\right._{n},

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}H

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{n}+\uptheta\mathrm{d}s,

(11)

where

\frac{\partial}{\partial n}\left|{}_{s}\right.

denotes partial differentiation with respect to
n
at fixed
s

and

\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\left|\right._{n}

denotes partial differentiation with respect to
s
at fixed
n
.

2.2.
Minkowski metric assumption and Minkowski-rectangular coordinates

In the rest of the paper, unless we explicitly state otherwise,
it should be understood that the spacetime manifold
\mathcal{M}
is equal to
\mathbb{R}^{1+3}

and that
\mathbf{g}
is equal to the Minkowski metric, which for clarity we denote by
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
.
We fix a standard global Minkowski-rectangular coordinate system
\{x^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=0,1,2,3}
on
\mathcal{M}
,
relative to which
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}=\mbox{diag}(-1,1,1,1)
.
We use the notation
\{\partial_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=0,1,2,3}
to denote the partial derivative vectorfields in this
coordinate system. We also use the alternate notation
t:=x^{0}
and
\partial_{t}:=\partial_{0}
.

2.2.1.
Index conventions

From now until the end of the paper, we use the following conventions for indices.

•

(
Lowercase Greek index conventions
) Lowercase Greek spacetime indices

\alpha
,
\beta
, etc. correspond to the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates
and vary over
0,1,2,3
. All lowercase Greek indices are lowered and raised with the Minkowski metric
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}

and its inverse
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}
.
Throughout the article, if
\upxi
is a type
\binom{m}{n}
spacetime tensorfield,
then unless we indicate otherwise, in our identities and estimates,

\left\{\upxi_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}}\right\}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots\alpha_{m},\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n}=0,1,2,3}
denotes its
components with respect to the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates
.

•

(
Lowercase Latin index conventions
)
Lowercase Latin spatial indices
a
,
b
, etc. correspond to the Minkowski-rectangular spatial coordinates and vary over
1,2,3
.
Much like in the previous point,
if
\upxi
is a type
\binom{m}{n}

\Sigma_{t}
-tangent tensorfield,
then
\left\{\upxi_{b_{1}\cdots b_{n}}^{a_{1}\cdots a_{m}}\right\}_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{m},b_{1},\cdots,b_{n}=1,2,3}

denotes its components with respect to the Minkowski-rectangular spatial coordinates
.

•

(
Uppercase Latin index conventions
)
Uppercase Latin spatial indices
A,B
, etc. correspond to the spatial coordinates

(x^{2},x^{3})
and vary over
2,3
.
This will be important in Sect.
6
, where

(x^{2},x^{3})
will correspond to perturbations away from plane-symmetry.

•

(
Einstein summation
)
We use Einstein’s summation convention in that repeated indices are summed,
e.g.,
L^{A}X^{A}:=L^{2}X^{2}+L^{3}X^{3}
.

2.3.
A well-posed first-order formulation

In this section, we provide a first-order formulation of the flow in
which the unknowns are taken to be

\left(h,\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\mathbf{u}^{3},s\right)
.
Thanks to the assumptions state in Sect.
2.1.2
,
the remaining fluid variables can be expressed
2
2

2

By (
2
),
one can also express
\mathbf{u}^{0}=\sqrt{1+\mathbf{u}_{a}\mathbf{u}^{a}}
.

as functions of the unknowns.

Definition 2.3

(Derivative of a scalar function with respect to a vectorfield)
.

Throughout the paper, if
\mathbf{Z}
is a vectorfield and
f
is a scalar function,
then
\mathbf{Z}f:=\mathbf{Z}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}f
denotes the derivative of
f

in the direction of
\mathbf{Z}
.

With the help of (
11
),
one can compute that for
C^{1}
solutions,
(
6a
)–(
6b
)
is equivalent to the following first-order formulation of the flow:

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h+c^{2}\partial_{\kappa}\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=0,

(12a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}+\Pi^{\alpha\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h-q(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1})^{\alpha\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}s

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle(\alpha

\displaystyle=0,1,2,3),

(12b)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}s

\displaystyle=0,

(12c)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=-1,

(12d)

where:

\displaystyle\Pi^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle:=(\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}+\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}\mathbf{u}^{\beta},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta

\displaystyle=0,1,2,3),

(13)

denotes projection onto the
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
-orthogonal complement of
\mathbf{u}
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{u}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle(\alpha

\displaystyle=0,1,2,3).

(14)

Equations (
12b
)–(
12d
)
+
(
13
)
are a first-order quasilinear hyperbolic system and are locally well-posed in suitable Sobolev spaces;
see Prop.
2.3
.
using (
12c
)
We also note by contracting (
12b
) against
\mathbf{u}_{\beta}
and
using (
12c
), we deduce – without using (
12d
) –
the following identity:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\left\{\bm{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})+1\right\}&=\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}(\partial_{\kappa}\mathbf{u}_{\lambda})\mathbf{u}^{\lambda}\\
&=-\left\{\bm{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})+1\right\}\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h.\end{split}

(15)

Using (
15
), one can easily show via Grönwall’s inequality
that if
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})+1
vanishes at
t=0
, then

\bm{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})+1
vanishes on any slab
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}

of classical existence on which the solution is
C^{1}
.
Hence, (
12d
) can be viewed as a constraint on the initial data that
is preserved by the flow.

Remark 2.1

(Additional terms in the context of curved spacetime backgrounds)
.

When posed on a general smooth spacetime background
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
,
there are additional terms on
RHS (
12a
)–(
12b
)
of the schematic form
\bf{\Gamma}\cdot\mathrm{f}(\mathbf{V})
,
where
\bf{\Gamma}
schematically denotes Christoffel symbols of

\mathbf{g}
,

\mathbf{V}
denotes the array of fluid variable unknowns (see definition
23
),
and
\mathrm{f}
schematically denotes a smooth function.
In the context of fluid shock formation, the dominant terms in the flow
are Riccati-type nonlinearities in the fluid obtained after differentiating the equations one time,
i.e., they are quadratic in the first derivatives of the fluid;
see Remark
3.5
.
Note that upon differentiating the term

\bf{\Gamma}\cdot\mathrm{f}(\mathbf{V})
,
one obtains a term that is
linear
in the derivatives of the fluid variables.
This means, in particular, that the for large-gradient solutions,
the differentiated terms
\partial_{\alpha}\left(\bf{\Gamma}\cdot\mathrm{f}(\mathbf{V})\right)

will be small relative to the shock-driving Riccati-type fluid terms,
i.e., the “new terms” coming from the non-flat ambient geometry are
relatively
small. This observation is a key reason behind our expectation that one can use the methods
described later in this article to prove shock formation
for the relativistic Euler equations on a general spacetime

(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
,
at least for initial data with suitable large gradients.

2.3.1.
Equations of variation

As a preliminary step in studying the well-posedness of
(
12b
)–(
12d
),
we will discuss the equations satisfied by the derivatives of solutions. This is important in the
sense that proofs of local well-posedness rely on differentiating the equations to obtain energy estimates
for the solution’s higher derivatives.
Specifically, by differentiating the equations, it is straightforward to see that
given a solution
(h,\mathbf{u}^{\alpha},s)
to (
12a
)–(
12d
),
any derivative (of any order), which we denote by
(\dot{h},\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\alpha},\dot{s})
,
satisfies an inhomogeneous system, known as the
equations of variation
, of the following form:

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\dot{h}+c^{2}\partial_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\dot{\mathfrak{F}},

(16a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\alpha}+\Pi^{\alpha\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\dot{h}-q(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1})^{\alpha\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\dot{s}

\displaystyle=\dot{\mathfrak{G}}^{\alpha},

\displaystyle(\alpha

\displaystyle=0,1,2,3),

(16b)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\dot{s}

\displaystyle=\dot{\mathfrak{H}},

(16c)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{u},\dot{\mathbf{u}})

\displaystyle=\dot{\mathfrak{I}}.

(16d)

Although here we do not provide a detailed formula for the structure of
the inhomogeneous terms
\dot{\mathfrak{F}}
,
\cdots
,
\dot{\mathfrak{I}}

on RHSs (
16a
)–(
16d
),
it is easy to see that they feature terms involving
\leq
the number of derivatives represented by the
variations
(\dot{h},\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\alpha},\dot{s})
,
i.e., fewer derivatives than the principal terms on LHSs (
16a
)–(
16d
).

2.3.2.
The acoustical metric

A basic question about the equations of variation is: what kinds of energy estimates are available for solutions?
It turns out that this question is connected to the intrinsic geometry of the flow via the
acoustical metric
,
which plays a fundamental role throughout the article.

Definition 2.4

(The acoustical metric and its inverse)
.

We define:

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{n}\left\{c^{-2}\bm{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha\beta}+(c^{-2}-1)\mathbf{u}_{\alpha}\mathbf{u}_{\beta}\right\},

(17a)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{n}^{-1}\left\{c^{2}(\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}+(c^{2}-1)\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}\mathbf{u}^{\beta}\right\},

(17b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}

\displaystyle:=c^{2}+(1-c^{2})(\mathbf{u}^{0})^{2}\geq 1.

(17c)

We refer to
\mathbf{h}
as the
acoustical metric
.

With the help of (
12d
), one can compute that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\gamma}(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\gamma\beta}

\displaystyle=\updelta_{\alpha}^{\beta},

(18)

where
\updelta_{\alpha}^{\beta}
is the Kronecker delta. That is,
(
18
) shows that
\mathbf{h}^{-1}
is indeed the inverse of
\mathbf{h}
.
Moreover, by construction, we have:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{00}

\displaystyle=-1.

(19)

Remark 2.2

(Normalization condition for
\mathbf{h}
)
.

(
19
) is a convenient normalization condition.
Other works (e.g.
[
34
]
) rely on a different normalization condition for the acoustical metric.
That represents a minor difference that typically has no important implications for the study of solutions.

Remark 2.3

(Subluminal speeds)
.

Note that by (
9
),
(
17a
),
and (
17c
),

\mathbf{h}(\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z})<0\implies\bm{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z})<0
,
i.e., the
\mathbf{h}
-null cones are inside the
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
-null cones.
Note also that by (
12d
),
(
17a
),
and (
17c
),
we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}_{\kappa\lambda}\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\mathbf{u}^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{n}<0.

(20)

In particular,
\mathbf{u}
is
\mathbf{h}
-timelike (and thus
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
-timelike, as one can explicitly see
via (
12d
)).

Remark 2.4

(Acoustical metric in a general ambient spacetime
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
)
.

When studying the relativistic Euler equations in a general ambient spacetime
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})

equipped with coordinates
(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
,
where
t:=x^{0}
is a smooth time function
(see Sect.
4.2.5
),
the acoustical metric is (modulo the comments of Remark
2.2
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\beta}:=\mathfrak{n}\left\{c^{-2}\mathbf{g}_{\alpha\beta}+(c^{-2}-1)\mathbf{u}_{\alpha}\mathbf{u}_{\beta}\right\},

(21)

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}

\displaystyle:=-(\mathbf{g}^{-1})^{00}c^{2}+(1-c^{2})(\mathbf{u}^{0})^{2}>0,

(22)

where the positivity on RHS (
22
)
stems from (
9
) and the assumption that
t
is a time function for
\mathbf{g}
,
which in particular implies that relative to the coordinates
(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
,
we have

(\mathbf{g}^{-1})^{00}=(\mathbf{g}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}(\partial_{\alpha}t)\partial_{\beta}t<0
.

2.3.3.
The arrays
\mathbf{V}
and
\dot{\mathbf{V}}

For notational convenience, we define the
solution array

\mathbf{V}
and the
variation array

\dot{\mathbf{V}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}

\displaystyle:=\left(h,\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\mathbf{u}^{3},s\right),

(23)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{V}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\dot{h},\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{0},\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{1},\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{2},\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{3},\dot{s}\right).

(24)

2.3.4.
Energy currents and energy identity in differential form

We now introduce energy currents, which form the basis for energy identities for the equations of variation.

Definition 2.5

(Energy currents)
.

Given a solution
\mathbf{V}
to
(
12b
)–(
12d
)
+
(
13
),
variations
\dot{\mathbf{V}}=(\dot{h},\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\alpha},\dot{s})

that satisfy (
16a
)–(
16d
),
and scalar functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
,
we define the corresponding
energy current
vectorfield

\dot{\mathbf{J}}_{(\mathbf{V},f_{1},f_{2})}

as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{\mathbf{J}}_{(\mathbf{V},f_{1},f_{2})}^{\alpha}[\dot{\mathbf{V}},\dot{\mathbf{V}}]&:=f_{1}\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}\dot{s}^{2}-2c^{2}q\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\alpha}\dot{s}-2q\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}\dot{s}\dot{h}+\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}\dot{h}^{2}+2c^{2}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\alpha}\dot{h}+c^{2}\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}\dot{\mathbf{u}}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}\\
&\ \ +2c^{2}\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}\dot{h}+f_{2}\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa})^{2}.\end{split}

(25)

Note that the components of
\dot{\mathbf{J}}
can be viewed as quadratic forms in

\dot{\mathbf{V}}
with coefficients that depend on the solution
\mathbf{V}
.
Related energy currents were used by Christodoulou in
[
34
]
. A general framework for
constructing energy currents in the context of
regularly hyperbolic
PDEs was
developed by Christodoulou in
[
32
]
.

The following lemma provides a differential version of the energy identities
afforded by the energy current. The proof is a tedious but straightforward computation, omitted here
(c.f.
[
34
]
*Equation (1.41)).

Lemma 2.1

(Energy identity in differential form)
.

Under the assumptions of Def.
2.5
, with

\dot{\mathbf{J}}_{(\mathbf{V},f_{1},f_{2})}^{\alpha}[\dot{\mathbf{V}},\dot{\mathbf{V}}]

denoting the energy current defined in (
25
),
the following identity holds relative to the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{J}}_{(\mathbf{V},f_{1},f_{2})}^{\kappa}[\dot{\mathbf{V}},\dot{\mathbf{V}}]&=2f_{1}\dot{s}\dot{\mathfrak{H}}-2q\dot{h}\dot{\mathfrak{H}}-2q\dot{s}\dot{\mathfrak{F}}+2\dot{h}\dot{\mathfrak{F}}+2c^{2}\dot{\mathbf{u}}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathfrak{G}}^{\kappa}+2c^{2}\dot{h}\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathfrak{G}}^{\kappa}\\
&\ \ +2f_{2}q\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}\dot{\mathfrak{H}}+2f_{2}\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}\mathbf{u}_{\lambda}\dot{\mathfrak{G}}^{\lambda}+\mathscr{Q}_{(\mathbf{V},f_{1},f_{2},\bm{\partial}\mathbf{V},\bm{\partial}f_{1},\bm{\partial}f_{2})}[\dot{\mathbf{V}},\dot{\mathbf{V}}],\end{split}

(26)

where

\mathscr{Q}_{(\mathbf{V},f_{1},f_{2},\bm{\partial}\mathbf{V},\bm{\partial}f_{1},\bm{\partial}f_{2})}[\dot{\mathbf{V}},\dot{\mathbf{V}}]

is a quadratic form in
\dot{\mathbf{V}}
with coefficients that depend on

\mathbf{V}
,
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
, and their first-order partial derivatives;
it is straightforward to compute
\mathscr{Q}

in detail, though we do not do so here.

By integrating (
26
) over a spacetime region and applying the divergence theorem,
one obtains an energy identity, where the energies are defined along the boundary of the region. A fundamental question is:
when are the energies obtained in this fashion positive definite
3
3

3

In general, energies are not useful if they are indefinite.

?
The answer to this question, provided by Lemma
2.2
,
is tied to the acoustic geometry of the system, captured by the acoustical metric.
In short, the energies are positive definite whenever the boundary of the region
has
\mathbf{h}
-timelike normals, i.e., whenever the boundary is spacelike with respect to
\mathbf{h}
.

Before proving the lemma, we first introduce a subset of state-space in which the
relativistic Euler equations are hyperbolic.

Definition 2.6

(Regime of hyperbolicity)
.

We define the
regime of hyperbolicity

\mathcal{H}
to be the following subset of solution variable space:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left(h,\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\mathbf{u}^{3},s\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{6}\ |\ 0<c(h,s)\leq 1\right\}.

(27)

Lemma 2.2

(Positive definiteness of
\dot{\mathbf{J}}_{(\mathbf{V},f_{1},f_{2})}[\dot{\mathbf{V}},\dot{\mathbf{V}}]
)
.

Let
\upxi
be a past-directed
\mathbf{h}
-timelike one-form,
i.e., assume that
(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}\upxi_{\kappa}\upxi_{\lambda}<0
and
\upxi_{0}:=\upxi(\partial_{t})>0
.
Let
\mathfrak{K}
be a compact subset of the set
\mathcal{H}
defined in (
27
),
and assume that the solution array
\mathbf{V}
defined in (
23
) satisfies

\mathbf{V}\in\mathfrak{K}
.
Then if
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are sufficiently large (in a manner that depends on
\upxi
and
\mathfrak{K}
),
there exists a
C=C(\upxi,\mathfrak{K},f_{1},f_{2})>0
such that
the quadratic form
\upxi_{\alpha}\dot{\mathbf{J}}_{(\mathbf{V},f_{1},f_{2})}^{\alpha}[\dot{\mathbf{V}},\dot{\mathbf{V}}]

is positive definite in the following sense:

\displaystyle\upxi_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{J}}_{(\mathbf{V},f_{1},f_{2})}^{\kappa}[\dot{\mathbf{V}},\dot{\mathbf{V}}]

\displaystyle\geq C|\dot{\mathbf{V}}|^{2}.

(28)

Remark 2.5

(Motivation for Lemmas
2.1
–
2.2
)
.

Although Lemmas
2.1
–
2.2
might seem at first glance like unmotivated technical results, they sit within the general framework of
multiplier methods
for hyperbolic equations. To give a rough idea of the multiplier method, consider a Schwartz-class solution
\phi
to the linear wave equation

-\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\Delta_{x}\phi=0
on Minkowski spacetime
(\mathbb{R}^{1+3},\bm{\mathfrak{m}})
.
We define the
energy current
vectorfield
\mathbf{J}
to be the vectorfield with the following components relative
the standard Minkowski-rectangular coordinates:

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}^{0}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2},

(29a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}^{i}

\displaystyle:=-(\partial_{i}\phi)\partial_{t}\phi,

\displaystyle(i=1,2,3).

(29b)

In (
29a
),
\nabla_{x}\phi:=(\partial_{1}\phi,\phi_{2},\phi_{3})
denotes the spatial gradient of
\phi

and
|\cdot|
is the standard Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Then straightforward calculations yield that
\partial_{\alpha}\mathbf{J}^{\alpha}=0
and that
(\partial_{t})_{\alpha}\mathbf{J}^{\alpha}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}
. These two identities are analogs of (
26
) and
(
28
) respectively.
The upshot is that by integrating
\partial_{\alpha}\mathbf{J}^{\alpha}=0

over
[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, one obtains the celebrated conservation of energy formula for solutions
to the linear wave equation:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{t\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\{(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\right\}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\{(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\right\}\mathrm{d}x.

(30)

The approach is called the “multiplier method” because it is a geometric version of the more standard proof of
(
30
),
in which one multiplies the wave equation by
\partial_{t}\phi
and integrates by parts over
[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Proof.

Throughout,
C=C(\upxi,\mathfrak{K},f_{1},f_{2})

denotes a positive constant (often smaller than
1
) that can vary from line to line.

We start by defining the scalar function
\upxi_{\parallel}
by:

\displaystyle\upxi^{\parallel}

\displaystyle:=\upxi_{\kappa}\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}.

(31)

Then, using (
12d
), we decompose:

\displaystyle\upxi_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\upxi^{\parallel}\mathbf{u}_{\alpha}+\upxi_{\alpha}^{\perp},

(32)

where the one-form
\upxi_{\alpha}^{\perp}
satisfies

\upxi_{\kappa}^{\perp}\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}=0
.
In particular,
\upxi_{\alpha}^{\perp}
is
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
-orthogonal to the
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
-timelike vectorfield

\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}
and thus
\upxi_{\alpha}^{\perp}
is
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
-spacelike whenever it is non-zero,
i.e.,
(\bm{\mathfrak{m}})^{-1}\upxi_{\kappa}^{\perp}\upxi_{\lambda}^{\perp}\geq 0
.
Since
(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}\upxi_{\kappa}\upxi_{\lambda}<0
and

\mathbf{h}_{\kappa\lambda}\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\mathbf{u}^{\lambda}<0

(see (
20
)),
and since
\upxi_{\alpha}
is past-directed while
\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}
is future-directed,
it follows that there is a
C
with
0<C<1
such that:

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\leq\upxi^{\parallel}\leq\frac{1}{C}.

(33)

We can similarly decompose
\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\alpha}
into a variation that is parallel to

\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}
and one that vanishes upon contraction with
\mathbf{u}_{\alpha}
.
We claim that prove (
28
),
it suffices to show that when
f_{1}=f_{2}=0
,
\dot{s}=0
,
and
\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}=0
,
we have:

\displaystyle\upxi_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{J}}^{\kappa}[\dot{\mathbf{V}},\dot{\mathbf{V}}]

\displaystyle\geq C(\dot{h}^{2}+\dot{\mathbf{u}}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}).

(34)

The estimate (
34
) would yield control over
\dot{h}^{2}
and variations such that

\dot{s}=\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}=0

(note that
\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\alpha}
is
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
-spacelike for such variations and thus the
term
\dot{\mathbf{u}}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}
on RHS (
34
)
is positive-definite in such variations).
Then
to handle general variations,
since the first and last
terms on RHS (
25
)
yield the two coercive (in view of (
33
)) terms

f_{1}\upxi^{\parallel}\dot{s}^{2}
and
f_{2}\upxi^{\parallel}(\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa})^{2}
,
we can choose
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
to be sufficiently large
(for example, we can choose them to be large, positive constants)
to obtain control over

\dot{s}^{2}
and
(\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa})^{2}
, and large enough so that these
two coercive terms
and the coercivity (
34
)
can collectively be used to absorb (via Young’s inequality)
all the remaining terms on RHS (
25
).
In total, this would yield (
28
).

Hence, to complete the proof of (
28
), we can
assume that
f_{1}=f_{2}=0
,
\dot{s}=0
,
and
\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}=0
.
This implies that
\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\alpha}
is
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
-spacelike.
We use (
12d
),
(
25
),
(
31
),
and
(
32
)
to compute that:

\displaystyle\upxi_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{J}}^{\kappa}[\dot{\mathbf{V}},\dot{\mathbf{V}}]

\displaystyle=\upxi^{\parallel}\dot{h}^{2}+2c^{2}\upxi_{\kappa}^{\perp}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}\dot{h}+c^{2}\upxi^{\parallel}\dot{\mathbf{u}}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}.

(35)

Since
(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}\upxi_{\kappa}\upxi_{\lambda}<-C<0
,

C<\mathfrak{n}<\frac{1}{C}
,
and
\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}=0
,
we compute using
(
12d
),
(
17b
),
(
31
),
and (
32
)
that there is a
C^{\prime}>0
such that:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}\upxi_{\kappa}\upxi_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-(\upxi^{\parallel})^{2}+c^{2}(\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}\upxi_{\kappa}^{\perp}\upxi_{\lambda}^{\perp}<-C^{\prime}.

(36)

We are denoting the positive constant on RHS (
36
) by “
C^{\prime}
” (rather than “
C
”) for clarity.
Since
\upxi_{\alpha}^{\perp}
is
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
-spacelike, we have

c^{2}(\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}\upxi_{\kappa}^{\perp}\upxi_{\lambda}^{\perp}\geq 0

and thus
C^{\prime}<(\upxi^{\parallel})^{2}
.
Since
\upxi_{\alpha}^{\perp}
and
\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\alpha}
are

\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
-spacelike,
for any
\upsigma>0
,
we can use
(
36
),
the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality with respect to
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
,
and Young’s inequality
to bound the cross term on RHS (
35
) as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}|2c^{2}\upxi_{\kappa}^{\perp}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}\dot{h}|&\leq\frac{1}{\upsigma}c^{4}(\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}\upxi_{\alpha}^{\perp}\upxi_{\beta}^{\perp}\dot{\mathbf{u}}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}+\upsigma\dot{h}^{2}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\upsigma}c^{2}\left\{(\upxi^{\parallel})^{2}-C^{\prime}\right\}\dot{\mathbf{u}}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}+\upsigma\dot{h}^{2}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\upsigma}c^{2}(\upxi^{\parallel})^{2}\left\{1-C^{\prime}C^{2}\right\}\dot{\mathbf{u}}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}+\upsigma\dot{h}^{2},\end{split}

(37)

where the last
C
on RHS (
37
) is from (
33
).
We now set
\upsigma:=\upxi^{\parallel}\left\{1-C^{\prime}C^{2}\right\}^{1/2}
. It follows that:

\displaystyle|2c^{2}\upxi_{\kappa}^{\perp}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}\dot{h}|

\displaystyle\leq c^{2}\upxi^{\parallel}\left\{1-C^{\prime}C^{2}\right\}^{1/2}\dot{\mathbf{u}}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}+\upxi^{\parallel}\left\{1-C^{\prime}C^{2}\right\}^{1/2}\dot{h}^{2}.

(38)

Thus,
since
1-C^{\prime}C^{2}<1
,
we deduce from (
38
)
that the cross term on RHS (
35
) can be absorbed
into the two positive definite terms with room to spare.
We have therefore proved (
34
)
assuming
f_{1}=f_{2}=0
,
\dot{s}=0
,
and

\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}\dot{\mathbf{u}}^{\kappa}=0
,
which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

2.3.5.
Local well-posedness and continuation principle in Sobolev spaces

In Prop.
2.3
, we discuss local well-posedness for the relativistic Euler equations in Sobolev spaces.

Proposition 2.3

(Local well-posedness and continuation principle in Sobolev spaces)
.

Recall the notation
\mathbf{V}
introduced in (
23
).
Let

\mathring{\mathbf{V}}=(\mathring{h},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}^{0},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}^{1},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}^{2},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}^{3},\mathring{s}):=\mathbf{V}|_{t=0}

be initial data for the relativistic Euler equations (
12a
)–(
12d
)
(in particular, assume that
\mathring{\mathbf{u}}
satisfies (
12d
)).
Assume that there is an integer
N\geq 3
such that

\mathring{h},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}^{1},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}^{2},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}^{3},\mathring{s}\in H^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

(and thus (
12d
) and the standard Sobolev–Moser calculus imply that

\mathring{\mathbf{u}}^{0}-1\in H^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
)
and that
\mathring{\mathbf{V}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is contained in a compact subset
of the interior of set
\mathcal{H}
defined in (
27
).
Then there is a maximal
T_{\textnormal{Lifespan}}>0
such that a unique solution to
(
12a
)–(
12d
)
exists and satisfies

h,\mathbf{u}^{0}-1,\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\mathbf{u}^{3},s\in C^{0}\left([0,T_{\textnormal{Lifespan}}),H^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)\cap C^{1}\left([0,T_{\textnormal{Lifespan}}),H^{N-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)
.
Moreover, either
T_{\textnormal{Lifespan}}=\infty
, or one of the following two breakdown scenarios occurs:

(1)

(
Blowup
). The solution’s
C^{1}
norm blows up:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow T_{\textnormal{Lifespan}}}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{C^{1}([0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{3})}=\infty.

(39)

(2)

(
Exiting the regime of hyperbolicity
).
There exists a sequence of points

\{(t_{m},x_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subset[0,T_{\textnormal{Lifespan}})\times\mathbb{R}^{3}

such that for every compact subset
\mathfrak{K}
of the interior of
\mathcal{H}
,
there exists an
m_{\mathfrak{K}}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathbf{V}(t_{m},x_{m})\notin\mathfrak{K}

whenever
m\geq m_{\mathfrak{K}}
.

We make the following remarks.

•

In Sects.
3
and
6
, we will study the formation
of shock singularities, in which the solution remains in the interior of the regime of hyperbolicity and the breakdown is precisely
due to the scenario (
39
).

•

The techniques of
[
50
]
, which rely only on energy estimates, Sobolev embedding, and the fractional
Sobolev–Moser calculus, can be used to save half a derivative compared to Prop.
2.3
, that is, to
prove local well-posedness for the
3D
relativistic Euler equations whenever

\mathring{h},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}^{1},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}^{2},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}^{3},\mathring{s}\in H^{{5/2}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

•

In
[
102
]
, a low-regularity
local well-posedness result for the
3D
relativistic Euler equations was proved. The low-regularity assumption
was on the “wave-part” of the initial data, which was assumed to belong to
H^{2^{+}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
while the “transport-part” (including the vorticity and entropy) was assumed to satisfy some
additional smoothness assumptions, which were shown to be propagated by the flow;
the terminology “wave-part” and “transport”
is tied to the formulation of the flow provided by Theorem
4.1

and is explained in
[
102
]
.
Lindblad’s work
[
64
]
implies that the assumptions on the wave-part
of the initial data are
optimal
in the scale of Sobolev spaces due to the
phenomenon of instantaneous shock formation for initial data in
H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.
The analysis in
[
102
]

relies on the new formulation of the flow presented in Theorem
4.1
,
which allows one to employ analytical techniques based on nonlinear geometric optics and Strichartz estimates.
The techniques used in
[
102
]
have roots in the works

[
40
,
99
]
(see also
[
105
,
103
,
104
]
)
on low-regularity well-posedness for the non-relativistic compressible Euler equations,
which in turn have roots in the works

[
54
,
56
,
58
,
55
,
57
,
59
,
86
,
98
]

on low-regularity well-posedness for quasilinear wave equations.

Discussion of proof of Prop.
2.3
.

This is a standard result that can be proved by linearizing the equations,
deriving a priori estimates for solutions to the linearized equations, and
then invoking a standard iteration scheme.
We refer to
[
90
,
89
,
88
]
for the main ideas, where an analog of Prop.
2.3

was proved for the coupling of the relativistic Euler equations to
Nordström’s theory of gravity. The main idea behind
the a priori estimates (in
H^{N}
) is to construct energy currents for the linearized systems
such that analogs of (
26
)
and (
28
) hold,
and to control RHS (
26
)
in
L^{2}
using the Sobolev–Moser calculus (which is standard in
3D
when
N\geq 3
).
∎

3.
A study of the maximal development in simple isentropic plane-symmetry

Our goal in this section is to prove Theorem
3.1
, which yields
a complete description of a localized portion of the boundary of the maximal (classical)
\mathbf{h}
-globally hyperbolic development (
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD for short)
for open sets of shock-forming solutions to the relativistic Euler equations (
12a
)–(
12c
)
in simple isentropic plane-symmetry; see Fig.
2
.
Roughly, the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD is the largest possible classical solution +
\mathbf{h}
-globally hyperbolic region that is launched by the initial data.
By a
\mathbf{h}
-globally hyperbolic region
\mathfrak{R}
of classical existence launched by the initial data, we mean that every inextendible

\mathbf{h}
-causal curve in
\mathfrak{R}
intersects
\Sigma_{0}:=\{t=0\}
,
where
\mathbf{h}
is the acoustical metric from Def.
2.4
.
In particular, the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD cannot contain any points lying in the
\mathbf{h}
-causal future of any singularity,
where the
\mathbf{h}
-causal future of a point is effectively determined by integral curves of
the two transversal null vectorfields
L
and
\underline{L}
from
Def.
3.1
.

The portion of the boundary that we study includes a singular boundary, where the fluid’s gradient blows up in a shock singularity,
and a Cauchy horizon, along which the solution remains smooth.
For the solutions under study, the past boundary of the Cauchy horizon intersects the past boundary of the singular boundary
in a distinguished point that we refer to as “the crease;” see Fig.
3
.
The crease plays the role of the true initial singularity in
a shock-forming relativistic Euler solution.

Sect.
3
is intended to provide a gentle introduction to the main ideas behind the study of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD
in a simplified setting in which the geometry is easy to study and one can avoid the burden of energy estimates.
It will also help set the stage for Sect.
6
, in which we
outline some of the main ideas needed to study shock formation in
3D
.

3.1.
A description of the symmetry class

As we mentioned, we will study simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions, which we now describe.
First, by “isentropic solutions,” we mean that
s\equiv 0
;
for any constant
s_{0}
, we could treat the case
s\equiv s_{0}
using the same arguments.
From (
12c
), we infer that for
C^{1}
isentropic solutions,
the condition
s=s_{0}

is preserved by the flow if it is satisfied at
t=0
. By “plane-symmetry,” we mean that

\mathbf{u}^{2}=\mathbf{u}^{3}\equiv 0
and that
h=h(t,x^{1})
,
\mathbf{u}^{1}=\mathbf{u}^{1}(t,x^{1})
, and
\mathbf{u}^{0}=\mathbf{u}^{0}(t,x^{1})
.
Such solutions can be viewed as solutions in
1D
in which
s\equiv 0
, and throughout
the rest of the paper,
we make that identification. By “simple,” we mean that only one Riemann invariant
(see Sect.
3.4
) is non-vanishing.

3.2.
The basic setup and conventions in plane-symmetry

In Sect.
3
, since we are studying isentropic plane-symmetric solutions,
we will slightly abuse notation and use
\mathbf{h}
to denote the restriction of
the acoustical metric (
17a
)–(
17b
),
to the
(t,x^{1})
-plane. That is, in Sect.
3
, relative to the
(t,x^{1})
-coordinates,
we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{n}\left\{c^{-2}\bm{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha\beta}+(c^{-2}-1)\mathbf{u}_{\alpha}\mathbf{u}_{\beta}\right\},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta

\displaystyle=0,1),

(40a)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{n}^{-1}\left\{c^{2}(\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}+(c^{2}-1)\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}\mathbf{u}^{\beta}\right\},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta

\displaystyle=0,1),

(40b)

where
\mathfrak{n}
is defined by (
17c
),
and we recall that (
19
) holds.

For future use, we note that for
C^{1}
isentropic plane-symmetric solutions,
the relativistic Euler equations (
12a
)–(
12c
)
in Minkowski spacetime are equivalent to:

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h+c^{2}\partial_{\kappa}\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=0,

(41a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\mathbf{u}^{1}+\Pi^{1\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h

\displaystyle=0,

(41b)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{0}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1+(\mathbf{u}^{1})^{2}}.

(41c)

For future use, we also note the following algebraic identity, which holds in plane-symmetry due to
(
17c
)
and
(
41c
):

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{u}^{0}+\mathbf{u}^{1}c)(\mathbf{u}^{0}-\mathbf{u}^{1}c).

(42)

To ensure ensure that shocks can form in solutions near constant fluid states with constant enthalpy
\overline{H}>0
,
we assume the following non-degeneracy assumption:

\displaystyle 1-(\overline{c})^{2}+\frac{\overline{c}^{\prime}}{\overline{c}\overline{H}}

\displaystyle\neq 0,

(43)

where in (
43
), we are viewing
c=c(H)
,

c^{\prime}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}H}c
,
and LHS (
43
) is defined to be the constant obtained by evaluating

1-c^{2}+\frac{c^{\prime}}{Hc}
at
H=\overline{H}
.
The condition (
43
) implies in particular that
the isentropic plane-symmetric relativistic Euler equations are genuinely nonlinear near the
constant-state solution
(H,\mathbf{u}^{1})\equiv(\overline{H},0)
;
see also Sect.
3.5
.
Christodoulou showed
[
34
]
*Chapter 1 that irrotational relativistic fluids
(of which isentropic plane-symmetric fluids are a special case)
admit a Lagrangian formulation for a potential function,
and the only equation of state leading to

1-c^{2}+\frac{c^{\prime}}{Hc}\equiv 0

is such that the potential function solves the
timelike minimal surface equation.
4
4

4

For the minimal surface equation,

c\equiv 1
corresponds to the trivial embedding
\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{1+(d+1)}
with respect to the Minkowski metric.

For that equation, which satisfies a nonlinear version of the null condition that is stronger than the
classic null condition identified by Klainerman
[
60
]
,
the first author proved that perturbations of plane-symmetric data
(which are similar to the data we study here in Sect.
3
)
lead to global, nonlinearly stable
C^{2}
solutions in both
\mathbb{R}^{1+1}
and
\mathbb{R}^{1+3}

[
2
,
5
]
.

3.3.
Null frame

In
1D
, it is possible to explicitly write down a pair of transversal null vectorfields that dictate the geometry of sound waves.
As we explain in Sect.
6.3
, in more than one spatial dimension, it is not possible to explicitly write down
null vectorfields in terms of the fluid variables. Rather, in that context, the null vectorfields depend on the gradient
of an eikonal function
U
.

Definition 3.1

(Null vectorfields in
1D
)
.

Relative to the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates
(t,x^{1})
,
we define the vectorfields
L
and
\underline{L}
as follows:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle:=\partial_{t}+\frac{\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\mathbf{u}^{0}}+c}{1+\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\mathbf{u}^{0}}c}\partial_{1},

(44a)

\displaystyle\underline{L}

\displaystyle:=\partial_{t}+\frac{\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\mathbf{u}^{0}}-c}{1-\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\mathbf{u}^{0}}c}\partial_{1}.

(44b)

Straightforward calculations
based on (
40a
)
and (
44a
)–(
44b
)
yield the following identities:

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(L,L)=\mathbf{h}(\underline{L},\underline{L})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(L,\underline{L})

\displaystyle=-2.

(45)

In particular, (
45
) shows that
L
and
\underline{L}
are
\mathbf{h}
-null.
The pair
\{L,\underline{L}\}
is known as a
\mathbf{h}
-
null frame
.

From (
45
) and straightforward calculations,
we deduce the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1

(
\mathbf{h}^{-1}
in terms of
L
and
\underline{L}
)
.

In
1+1
spacetime dimensions,
the inverse acoustical metric
\mathbf{h}^{-1}
from (
40b
) satisfies:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}L^{\alpha}\underline{L}^{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\underline{L}^{\alpha}L^{\beta},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta

\displaystyle=0,1),

(46)

i.e.,
\mathbf{h}^{-1}=-\frac{1}{2}L\otimes\underline{L}-\frac{1}{2}\underline{L}\otimes L
.

3.4.
Riemann invariants

In this section, we introduce the
Riemann invariants
, which are a pair of fluid variables, one of which is
constant along the integral curves of
L
and the other along the integral curves of
\underline{L}
. For
isentropic plane-symmetric solutions to the relativistic Euler equations,
the Riemann invariants can be used as state-space variables for the fluid, i.e., the other fluid variables are determined by them.
Riemann invariants were discovered by Riemann in his proof of shock formation for the non-relativistic
compressible Euler equations in
1D
.
In
[
96
]
, Taub discovered Riemann invariants for the isentropic relativistic Euler equations in
1D
.
Studying the flow with respect to the Riemann invariants allows one to study the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD using
techniques based on transport equations and ODE theory. In particular, energy estimates are not needed in
1D
.
Although Riemann invariants are not available in multi-dimensions, approximate Riemann invariants
are
available, and they
are convenient for studying perturbations of isentropic plane-symmetric solutions; see Sect.
6.2
.

To proceed, we let
F=F(H)
be the solution to
the initial value problem:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}H}F(H)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Hc},

\displaystyle F(\overline{H})=0,

(47)

where
\overline{H}>0
is the constant
(
3
),
and in (
47
),
we are viewing
c
as a function of
H
.

Note that since
c(\overline{H})\neq 0
by assumption,
it follows from (
47
) that the function
F

has a local inverse
F^{-1}
defined near the origin such that
F^{-1}(0)=\overline{H}
.

Definition 3.2

(Riemann invariants)
.

We define the
Riemann invariants

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}

\displaystyle:=F(H)+\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1+\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\mathbf{u}^{0}}}{1-\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\mathbf{u}^{0}}}\right),

(48a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}

\displaystyle:=F(H)-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1+\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\mathbf{u}^{0}}}{1-\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\mathbf{u}^{0}}}\right).

(48b)

In the next proposition, for isentropic plane-symmetric solutions, we express the relativistic Euler equations
in terms of
(\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}},\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}})
, and we provide explicit formulae
for various quantities in terms of
(\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}},\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}})
.

Proposition 3.2

(Isentropic plane-symmetric flow in terms of the Riemann invariants)
.

Let
(h,\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{u}^{1})
be a
C^{1}
solution to (
41a
)–(
41c
),
and let
L
and
\underline{L}
be the vectorfields from Def.
3.1
.
Then the Riemann invariants of Def.
3.2
satisfy the
following quasilinear transport system:

\displaystyle L\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}

\displaystyle=0,

(49a)

\displaystyle\underline{L}\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}

\displaystyle=0.

(49b)

Moreover, the components of the fluid velocity
in the Minkowski-rectangular coordinate system
(t,x^{1})

can be expressed in terms of the Riemann invariants as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{0}

\displaystyle=\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right),\qquad\mathbf{u}^{1}=\sinh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right).

(50a)

In addition, with
F^{-1}
denoting the inverse function of the map
F
from Def.
3.2
,
we have:

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=F^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}+\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\right)\right).

(50b)

Finally, the following identities hold:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}+\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)+c\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)}{\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)+c\sinh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)}\partial_{1},

(51a)

\displaystyle\underline{L}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}+\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)-c\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)}{\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)-c\sinh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)}\partial_{1}.

(51b)

Proof.

Equations (
49a
)–(
49b
) follow from straightforward but tedious calculations involving
Def.
3.2
, equations (
41a
)–(
41c
),
the identity
\mathbf{u}^{0}\partial_{\alpha}\mathbf{u}^{0}=\mathbf{u}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}\mathbf{u}^{1}

(which follows from differentiating (
41c
) with
\partial_{\alpha}
),
and the identity

\partial_{\alpha}F(H)=\frac{1}{c}\partial_{\alpha}h

(which follows from (
4
) and (
47
)).

To prove the identities in (
50a
),
we first subtract
(
48b
)
from
(
48a
)
and use
(
41c
) to compute that

\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\right)=\ln(\mathbf{u}^{0}+\mathbf{u}^{1})
.
Rewriting (
41c
) as
(\mathbf{u}^{0}+\mathbf{u}^{1})(\mathbf{u}^{0}-\mathbf{u}^{1})=1

and carrying out straightforward calculations based on the definitions of
\cosh
and
\sinh
,
we conclude (
50a
).
(
50b
) follows from adding (
48a
) to (
48b
),
dividing by
2
,
and then applying
F^{-1}
to each side of the resulting identity.

Finally, (
51a
)–(
51b
)
follow from definitions (
44a
)–(
44b
)
and the already proven identity (
50a
).
∎

Convention 3.1

(The fluid variables as functions of the Riemann invariants)
.

In view of (
50a
)–(
50b
),
throughout Sect.
3
,
we often view all the fluid variables as smooth functions of the Riemann invariants
(or of only
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
when we are studying simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions),
e.g.,
\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{u}^{1},h,H=\mathrm{f}(\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}},\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}})
,
where throughout the paper,
\mathrm{f}
schematically denotes a smooth function that can vary from
line to line.
For example, we use
(
47
),
(
50b
),
the inverse function theorem,
and the chain rule
to compute the following identity, which holds for isentropic plane-symmetric solutions:

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}c

\displaystyle=\frac{cH}{2}c^{\prime}\left\{\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}-\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\right\},

(52)

where on RHS (
52
),

c^{\prime}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}c}{\mathrm{d}H}
.

3.5.
Brief remarks on Riccati-type blowup and genuine nonlinearity

Consider a simple isentropic plane-symmetric solution
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
to (
49a
) (with
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}=0
).
By differentiating the equation with respect to
\partial_{1}
, one obtains a Riccati-type equation
for
\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
of the form

L\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}L^{1}\cdot(\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})^{2}
,
where here we are viewing
L^{1}
as a function of
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
, which is possible thanks to
Prop.
3.2

and Convention
3.1
.
Hence, as long as the
genuine nonlinearity condition

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}L^{1}\neq 0
holds,
then for solutions
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
launched by smooth compactly supported initial data,
one can easily show that
\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
typically blows up along the integral curves of
L
,
much like in the case of Burgers’ equation
\partial_{t}\Psi+\Psi\partial_{1}\Psi=0
;
we refer to
[
39
]
*Section 7.5 for a detailed discussion of the notion of genuine nonlinearity
in the context of
1D
hyperbolic conservation laws.
One can check that the non-degeneracy assumption (
43
) indeed implies that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}L^{1}|_{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}=0}\neq 0
,
i.e., that equation (
49a
) is genuinely nonlinear
near
(\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}},\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}})=(0,0)
.

Our study of the blowup in Theorem
3.1
does not rely on differentiating
the equations with
\partial_{1}
to obtain a Riccati-type PDE.
Instead, it relies on a much sharper approach tied to nonlinear geometric optics, which we set up in
Sect.
3.6
.
In
3D
, such a sharpened approach seems essential, as
there is no known simple approach for studying gradient-blowup based only on
differentiating the equations with respect to a standard, solution-independent partial derivative operator.
In particular, in our discussion of shock formation in
3D
in Sect.
6
,
nonlinear geometric optics will play a fundamental role.

3.6.
Simple isentropic plane symmetry and nonlinear geometric optics

Our study of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD in Theorem
3.1

intimately relies on nonlinear geometric optics, implemented via an acoustic eikonal function
U
,
and a careful study of the corresponding acoustic geometry. The main idea of the proof of
Theorem
3.1

is to show that the solution remains smooth relative to the “geometric coordinates”
(t,U)
all the way up to the shock
and to recover the formation of the shock as a degeneracy between the geometric coordinates
and the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates
(t,x^{1})
.

In this section, we construct the eikonal function and corresponding acoustic geometry for
the isentropic relativistic Euler equations in
1D
,
i.e., to the following initial value problem:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}L\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}=0,\\
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}|_{t=0}=\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\underline{L}\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}=0,\\
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}|_{t=0}=\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}.\end{cases}

(53)

More precisely, we restrict our attention to
simple
isentropic plane-symmetric solutions,
defined here to be those solutions such that
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\equiv 0
.
From (
53
) and (
44b
),
it follows that such solutions arise from initial data with
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\equiv 0
. In this case,
(
50a
) yields the identity

\mathbf{u}^{0}=\cosh(\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})
.
As we alluded to in Convention
3.1
,
(
50a
)–(
50b
)
also yield similar formulas
for
\mathbf{u}^{1},\,H
as functions of
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
.

We start by defining the acoustic eikonal function, which plays a central role in all of the forthcoming analysis.

Definition 3.3

(The acoustic eikonal function in
1D
and the characteristics)
.

Let
\mathbf{h}^{-1}
be the inverse acoustical metric defined in (
46
). Then we define the acoustic eikonal function (eikonal function for short) to be the solution
U
to the following fully nonlinear hyperbolic PDE with Cauchy data:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}U\partial_{\beta}U

\displaystyle=0,

(54a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}U

\displaystyle>0,

(54b)

\displaystyle U|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=-x^{1}.

(54c)

We refer to the level sets of
U
as “the characteristics.”

Remark 3.1

(Geometric optics is easier in
1D
)
.

Although understanding the fully nonlinear structure of (
54a
) is necessary in multiple space dimensions, in
1D
, (
54a
) is equivalent to the
linear
(in
U
) transport equation of
U
derived in the following lemma. This makes the analysis of the acoustic geometry in this section considerably simpler than in multi-
D
. It is also unique to
1D
that the
\mathbf{h}
-null vectorfield
L
can be explicitly expressed in terms of the fluid via the formula (
44a
). The correct analog of (
44a
) in higher dimensions is precisely
L=\frac{1}{L_{(\textnormal{geo})}^{0}}L_{(\textnormal{geo})}
, where
L_{(\textnormal{geo})}^{\alpha}=-(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}U
.

Lemma 3.3

(Linear transport equation for
U
)
.

Let
L
be the vectorfield defined in (
44a
).
For simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions,
if
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
is sufficiently small,
5
5

5

The solutions from Theorem
3.1

satisfy the needed smallness. The key point is that if
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
is large, then

L
and
\underline{L}
could both be right-pointing or both be left-pointing, in which case the initial
condition (
55b
) would not be sufficient for identifying the “correct root of the eikonal equation,”
namely the “root”
LU=0
.

then
the scalar function
U
solves (
54a
)–(
54c
)

if and only if

it solves the following transport equation initial value problem:

\displaystyle LU

\displaystyle=0,

(55a)

\displaystyle U|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=-x^{1}.

(55b)

Proof.

Suppose
U
solves (
54a
)–(
54b
).
Then by (
46
),
we find that
0=(LU)\cdot\underline{L}U
.
If
LU=0
, there is nothing to prove. Hence, we assume that
LU\neq 0
,
and we will show that this leads to a contradiction.
Then by (
46
), we have
\underline{L}U=0
.
By
(
9
),
(
51b
),
(
54c
),
(
55b
),
and our smallness assumption on
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
,
we have:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}U\big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sinh(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})-c\cosh(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})}{\cosh(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})-c\sinh(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})}\partial_{1}U\big|_{t=0}=\frac{\sinh(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})-c\cosh(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})}{\cosh(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})-c\sinh(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})}<0.

(56)

(
56
) contradicts (
54b
).

Conversely, if (
55a
)–(
55b
) hold,
then since (
46
) implies that

(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}U\partial_{\beta}U=-(LU)\cdot\underline{L}U
,
it immediately follows that (
54a
) and (
54c
) hold.
To prove (
54b
),
we use
(
9
),
(
51a
),
(
55b
),
and the smallness of
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
,
arguing as in the proof of (
56
)
to deduce that
\partial_{t}U>0
, as is desired.
∎

Some remarks are in order.

•

The minus sign in (
55b
) is just a convention imposed for consistency with related results that have
appeared in the literature, such as
[
87
,
65
,
67
,
1
]
.

•

The precise initial condition in (
55b
) is not important; small perturbations of this initial condition
could also be used to study the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD.

•

We have adapted our eikonal function to
L
because in
Theorem
3.1
, we will study initial data that lead to shock-forming solutions
such that the singularity is tied to the infinite density of the
L
-characteristics and the blowup of

\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
. There is nothing special about
L
compared to
\underline{L}
, and it is only for convenience that we have
adapted our constructions to it. That is,
using the methods of this section, one could also construct an eikonal function
\underline{U}
adapted to
\underline{L}

and study different initial data that lead to a singularity
tied to the infinite density of the
\underline{L}
-characteristics and the blowup of

\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
. One could also try to study richer problems in which singularities occur
along both families of characteristics.

We now define
\upmu
, the inverse foliation density of the characteristics.
The name is justified by the fact that
\frac{1}{\upmu}
is a measure of the density of the stacking of the
characteristics relative to the level-sets
\Sigma_{t^{\prime}}:=\{t=t^{\prime}\}
.
Roughly speaking,
\upmu
is initially positive, while shock formation corresponds to
\upmu\downarrow 0
,
which signifies the infinite density of the integral curves of
L

(or equivalently, the level sets of
U
in
(t,x^{1})
-space);
we refer to Fig.
3
, a context in which

\upmu
vanishes along the curve portion denoted by “
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]})
”
(we will explain these issues in much more detail later on).

Definition 3.4

(Inverse foliation density)
.

We define the
inverse foliation density
of the characteristics, denoted by
\upmu
, as follows:

\displaystyle\upmu

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{1}U}.

(57)

In Sect.
6
, which concerns solutions in
3D
,
we will define
\upmu
to be
-\frac{1}{(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}\partial_{\kappa}t\partial_{\lambda}U}
;
see (
157
).
Under the initial condition (
156
) that we will choose for
U
,
that definition of
\upmu
can be shown to agree with the one for plane-symmetric solutions
stated in (
57
).

We also note the following identities, which follow from (
55b
)
and
(
57
):

\displaystyle\upmu|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathfrak{n}}{c}\big|_{t=0},\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu\big|_{t=0}=1.

(58)

We now define a collection of vectorfields associated to the eikonal function.

Definition 3.5

(The vectorfields
X,\,\breve{X},\,\breve{\underline{L}}
)
.

Let
L
and
\underline{L}
be the vectorfields from Def.
3.1

(they are
\mathbf{h}
-null by (
45
)).
We define:

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}(\underline{L}-L),

\displaystyle\breve{X}

\displaystyle:=\upmu X,

\displaystyle\breve{\underline{L}}

\displaystyle:=\upmu\underline{L}.

(59)

Using
(
44a
)–(
44b
),
(
45
),
and (
59
),
it is straightforward to see that
X
is
\Sigma_{t}
-tangent (i.e.,
Xt=0
) and satisfies

\mathbf{h}(X,X)=1
, that
\mathbf{h}(\breve{X},\breve{X})=\upmu^{2}
, and that
\mathbf{h}(\breve{\underline{L}},\breve{\underline{L}})=0
.
We also note that in plane-symmetry,
using Def.
3.1
,
(
42
),
and Def.
3.5
,
one can compute the following identity:

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=-\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{1}.

(60)

Our main results concern solutions in which the factor
\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}

on RHS (
60
) satisfies
\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\approx 1
.

Definition 3.6

(Geometric coordinates in
1D
and the corresponding partial derivative vectorfields)
.

We define
(t,U)
to be the
geometric coordinates
,
and we denote the corresponding geometric coordinate partial derivative vectorfields
by
\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\frac{\partial}{\partial U}\right\}

(which are not to be confused with the partial derivatives
\{\partial_{t},\partial_{1}\}

in the
(t,x^{1})
-coordinate system).

Using (
44a
), (
55a
), (
57
),
and (
60
), it is straightforward to compute that:

\displaystyle Lt

\displaystyle=\breve{X}U=1,

(61a)

\displaystyle LU

\displaystyle=\breve{X}t=0.

(61b)

From
(
61a
)–(
61b
),
it follows that in geometric coordinates, the following identities hold:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t},

\displaystyle\breve{X}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial U},

(62)

\displaystyle[L,\breve{X}]

\displaystyle=0.

(63)

From (
44b
),
(
59
),
and the identities in (
62
),
we deduce the following expression for
\breve{\underline{L}}
relative to the geometric coordinates:

\displaystyle\breve{\underline{L}}

\displaystyle=\upmu\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+2\frac{\partial}{\partial U}.

(64)

We will often silently use
(
62
)–(
63
)
in the rest of Sect.
3
.

The following sets play a fundamental role in our analysis of solutions.

Definition 3.7

(Portions of submanifolds of geometric coordinate space
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{U}
)
.

Given intervals
I,J\subset\mathbb{R}
,
relative to the geometric coordinates, we define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{U^{\prime}}^{I}

\displaystyle:=\big\{(t,U)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ t\in I,\,U=U^{\prime}\big\},

(65a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t^{\prime}}^{J}

\displaystyle:=\big\{(t,U)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ t=t^{\prime},\,U\in J\big\}.

(65b)

We often write
\mathcal{P}_{U}
instead of
\mathcal{P}_{U}^{[0,\infty)}
and

\Sigma_{t}
instead of
\Sigma_{t}^{(-\infty,\infty)}
.
We often refer to the
\mathcal{P}_{U}
as
the characteristics
.

The following lemma shows that the geometric coordinates degenerate with respect to the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates precisely when

\upmu=0
.
However, Theorem
3.1
shows that for appropriate initial data,

\Upsilon
will still be a
homeomorphism
on the subset of the boundary of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD where
\upmu
vanishes.

Lemma 3.4

(The Jacobian determinant of
(t,U)\mapsto(t,x^{1})
)
.

Let:

\displaystyle\Upsilon(t,U)

\displaystyle:=(t,x^{1})

(66)

denote the change of variables map from geometric coordinates to Minkowski-rectangular coordinates.
Then the following identity holds:

\displaystyle\det\mathrm{d}\Upsilon

\displaystyle=-\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu.

(67)

Proof.

(
67
) follows from straightforward calculations based
on the identity
\frac{\partial}{\partial U}x^{1}=-\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu
,
which is implied by (
59
)–(
60
) and
(
62
).
∎

3.6.1.
Explicit solution formula in geometric coordinates

From (
62
), we see
that in simple isentropic plane-symmetry,
the transport equation (
49a
)
takes the following form in geometric coordinates:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(t,U)

\displaystyle=0.

(68)

From (
68
) and (
53
),
we see that in geometric coordinates, the solution to (
68
) is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(t,U)

\displaystyle=\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U).

(69)

3.6.2.
The evolution equation for
\upmu
, the non-degeneracy condition, and explicit expressions for various solution variables

In this section, we derive various identities involving
\upmu
. In particular,
we will later use the identities to show that suitable assumptions on the initial data of
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}

lead to the vanishing of
\upmu
in finite time, i.e., the formation of a shock.

We begin with the following lemma, which provides the evolution equation for
\upmu
.

Lemma 3.5

(Transport equation satisfied by
\upmu
)
.

For simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions,
the inverse foliation density, defined in (
57
),
satisfies the following transport equation,
where

c^{\prime}(H):=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}H}c(H)
:

\displaystyle L\left(\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu\right)

\displaystyle=G,

(70)

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1-c^{2}+\frac{c^{\prime}}{cH}}{2(\mathbf{u}^{0}+c\mathbf{u}^{1})^{2}}\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}.

(71)

Moreover,

\displaystyle LL\upmu

\displaystyle=LL\left(\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu\right)=0.

(72)

Proof.

First, using (
51a
),
(
52
),
(
60
),
the quotient rule,
the identity
\cosh^{2}(z)-\sinh^{2}(z)=1
,
and straightforward calculations, we deduce the following commutator identity, valid for simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions:

[L,\partial_{1}]=\frac{\mathfrak{n}}{c}\left\{\frac{1-c^{2}+\frac{c^{\prime}}{cH}}{2(\mathbf{u}^{0}+c\mathbf{u}^{1})^{2}}\right\}X\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\partial_{1}.

Equation (
70
) then follows from multiplying (
57
) by
c/\mathfrak{n}
