, differentiating both sides with respect to
L
, and using the following commutator identity,
which is a consequence the eikonal equation
LU=0
:

L\left(\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu\right)=\frac{1}{(\partial_{1}U)^{2}}L\partial_{1}U=\left(\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu\right)^{2}L\partial_{1}U=\left(\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu\right)^{2}[L,\partial_{1}]U.

Equation (
72
) then follows from differentiating (
70
) with respect to
L

and using (
49a
),
(
60
),
(
63
),
and the assumption of simple isentropic plane-symmetry.

∎

We find it convenient to work with an
anti-derivative
of the nonlinearity
G

defined in (
71
).
To this end,
viewing the quantity

-\frac{1-c^{2}+\frac{c^{\prime}}{cH}}{2(\mathbf{u}^{0}+c\mathbf{u}^{1})^{2}}
as a function of
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}

(see Convention
3.1
),
we define the function
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{A}[\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}]
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}[\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}]

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}\left\{-\frac{1-c^{2}+\frac{c^{\prime}}{cH}}{2(\mathbf{u}^{0}+c\mathbf{u}^{1})^{2}}\right\}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z.

(73)

From (
62
),
(
73
),
the fundamental theorem of calculus,
and the chain rule, it follows that:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\mathfrak{A}[\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}]

\displaystyle=\breve{X}\mathfrak{A}[\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}]=G,

(74a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}[0]

\displaystyle=0.

(74b)

We highlight the important fact that in simple isentropic plane-symmetry,
the non-degeneracy assumption (
43
),
the initial condition of (
47
),
(
73
),
and (
74b
)
collectively imply that:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}\mathfrak{A}[\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}]|_{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}=0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left\{1-(\overline{c})^{2}+\frac{\overline{c}^{\prime}}{\overline{c}\overline{H}}\right\}\neq 0.

(75)

From
(
73
)
and
(
75
),
it follows that the function

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\mapsto\mathfrak{A}[\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}]
is
a diffeomorphism from a neighborhood of the origin
(i.e., near
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}=0
) onto a neighborhood of the origin;
we will silently use this basic fact in the rest of Sect.
3
.
As we mentioned in Sect.
3.5
,
(
75
) is fundamental for ensuring the
formation of shocks, for it implies that near
(\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}},\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}})=(0,0)
,
equation (
49a
) is genuinely nonlinear.

Next, we note that in simple isentropic plane-symmetry,
in view of (
69
),
the quantities
G
,
c
, and
\mathfrak{n}
can be viewed as functions of
U
alone.
We sometimes emphasize this fact by using notation such as
G(U)
,
c(U)
, etc.
The following corollary is a straightforward application of this fact and the above discussion;
we omit the straightforward proof.

Corollary 3.6

(Explicit expressions for
\upmu
,
L\upmu
,
\breve{X}\upmu
, and
\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
)
.

Let
G
be the source term from (
71
), viewed as a function of
U
.
Let
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}[\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}]

be the function of
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
defined by
(
73
).
Then for simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions, the following identities hold
relative to the geometric coordinates,
where

\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}[z]:=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}\mathfrak{A}[z]
:

\displaystyle L\left\{\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu(t,U)\right\}

\displaystyle=G(U)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]=\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U),

(76)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu(t,U)&=1+tG(U)=1+t\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]\\
&=1+t\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U),\end{split}

(77)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\breve{X}\upmu(t,U)&=t\left(\frac{\mathfrak{n}}{c}G^{\prime}\right)(U)+\left\{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{n}}{c}\right)(U)\right\}\left\{1+tG(U)\right\}\\
&=\frac{\mathfrak{n}}{c}t\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}U^{2}}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]+\left\{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{n}}{c}\right)(U)\right\}\left\{1+t\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]\right\},\end{split}

(78)

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}](t,U)&=-\frac{1}{\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu(t,U)}\frac{\partial}{\partial U}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(t,U)=-\frac{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)}{1+tG(U)}\\
&=-\frac{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)}{1+t\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)}.\end{split}

(79)

3.7.
Initial data that lead to admissible shock-forming solutions

In this section, we construct examples of initial data that fall under the scope of
Theorem
3.1
,
i.e., initial data that launch shock-forming solutions
such that we can derive the structure of a portion of the maximal classical development that includes a neighborhood of its boundary.
Our construction could be substantially generalized to exhibit a much larger class of initial data for which the results of
Theorem
3.1
hold; here we have striven for simplicity.

3.7.1.
Assumptions on the “seed” profile

To start, we fix any scalar “seed profile”
\mathring{\varphi}
with the following properties
(it is straightforward to show that such functions exist):

•

\mathring{\varphi}=\mathring{\varphi}(U)
is a
6
6

6

Our proof of Theorem
3.1

does not actually require
\mathring{\varphi}
to be
C^{\infty}
;
C^{4}
would suffice.

C^{\infty}
function that is
compactly supported in
an interval
[-U_{1},U_{2}]
of
U
-values, where
U_{1},U_{2}>1
.

•

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathring{\varphi}(U)

has a unique, non-degenerate minimum at
U=0
(our choice
U=0
is only for convenience).
In particular,
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}}{\mathrm{d}U^{3}}\mathring{\varphi}(0)>0
.

•

Modifying
\mathring{\varphi}
by multiplying it by a constant
and composing it with a linear map of the form
U\rightarrow zU

for some constant
z\in\mathbb{R}
if necessary, we assume that
(for the modified
\mathring{\varphi}
), there is a constant
\mathring{\updelta}_{*}
satisfying:

\displaystyle\mathring{\updelta}_{*}

\displaystyle>0

(80)

such that:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathring{\varphi}(U)\bigg|_{U=0}

\displaystyle=-\mathring{\updelta}_{*},

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}U^{2}}\mathring{\varphi}(U)\bigg|_{U=0}=0;

(81)

that there is a constant
\mathfrak{b}
satisfying:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}

\displaystyle>0

(82)

such that:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}}{\mathrm{d}U^{3}}\mathring{\varphi}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{b},

(83)

and such that:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{b}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d^{3}}{\mathrm{d}U^{3}}\mathring{\varphi}(U)\leq 2\mathfrak{b},

\displaystyle\mbox{if }|U|\leq 1;

(84)

and that there is a constant
\mathfrak{p}
satisfying:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{p}<1

(85)

such that:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathring{\varphi}(U)

\displaystyle>-\mathfrak{p}\mathring{\updelta}_{*},

\displaystyle\mbox{if }|U|\geq 1.

(86)

See Fig.
1
for the graph
of a representative seed profile.

\begin{overpic}[graph_of_seed.pdf]
\put(9.0,15.0){$U=U_{2}$}
\put(26.0,15.0){$U=1$}
\put(38.0,15.0){$U=0$}
\put(51.0,15.0){$U=-1$}
\put(82.0,15.0){$U=-U_{1}$}
\put(36.0,7.0){$U$}
\put(65.0,32.0){$\mathring{\varphi}(U)$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 1.
The graph of a representative “seed profile,” with
U
increasing from right to left.

3.7.2.
The initial data for
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
and amplitude smallness assumption

We now construct the initial data for
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
.
Let
\mathring{\varphi}
be as in Sect.
3.7.1
.
We define the initial data function
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}:=\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}|_{t=0}
to be the following function of

U
:

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{A}^{-1}[\mathring{\varphi}(U)],

(87)

where
\mathfrak{A}^{-1}
is the inverse function of the map

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{A}[\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}]

defined by (
73
).

Amplitude smallness assumption on the initial data

By (
75
) and continuity,
we can further modify
\mathring{\varphi}
by multiplying it by a constant
to shrink the amplitude of
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
and ensure that
the following two conditions hold:

(1)

The isentropic plane-symmetric initial data array

\mathring{\mathbf{V}}:=(h,\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{u}^{1},0,0,0)|_{t=0}

determined by
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
via
(
50a
)–(
50b
)
(with
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}=0
in those formulas)
satisfies
\mathring{\mathbf{V}}\in\mbox{interior}(\mathcal{H})
,
where
\mathcal{H}
is the regime of hyperbolicity defined in (
27
).

(2)

The following
non-degeneracy condition holds:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}\mathfrak{A}[\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}]\bigg|_{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}=\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)}

\displaystyle\neq 0,

\displaystyle U\in[-U_{1},U_{2}].

(88)

In the rest of Sect.
3
, we will use the above two assumptions without always explicitly mentioning them.

Taking into account (
43
) and (
74b
),
we deduce from Taylor expansions and standard calculus
that the following conclusions
hold, where
C>0
depends on
\mathring{\varphi}
,
and for functions
f=f(U)
on
\Sigma_{0}:=\{t=0\}
,

\|f\|_{C^{0}(\Sigma_{0})}:=\max_{U\in\mathbb{R}}|f(U)|
:

•

\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
is compactly supported in
[-U_{1},U_{2}]

and satisfies the following bounds:

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{M}}{\mathrm{d}U^{M}}\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\right\|_{C^{0}(\Sigma_{0})}

\displaystyle\leq C,

\displaystyle M=0,1,2,3,4.

(89)

•

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]

has a unique, negative, non-degenerate minimum at
U=0
.
In particular,

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}}{\mathrm{d}U^{3}}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]|_{U=0}>0
.

•

There is a differentiable function
\mathscr{R}:[-U_{1},U_{2}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\left\|\mathscr{R}\right\|_{C^{1}(\Sigma_{0}^{[-U_{1},U_{2}]})}\leq C

and such that:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]

\displaystyle=-\mathring{\updelta}_{*}+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{b}U^{2}+\mathscr{R}(U)U^{3}.

(90)

•

For
j=1,2,3
, there exists a continuous function

\Lambda_{j}:[-U_{1},U_{2}]\rightarrow(0,\infty)

such that:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{j}(U)

\displaystyle\approx 1,

\displaystyle U\in[-U_{1},U_{2}],

(91)

and such that for
M=0,1,2
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{M}}{\mathrm{d}U^{M}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]+\mathring{\updelta}_{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{M+1}(U)U^{2-M},

\displaystyle\mbox{if }|U|\leq 1,

(92a)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]

\displaystyle\geq-\mathfrak{p}\mathring{\updelta}_{*},

\displaystyle\mbox{if }|U|\geq 1,

(92b)

where
\mathfrak{p}\in[0,1)
is the non-negative constant in (

),
and in (

) and throughout,

A\approx B
means that there is a

.

•

The constant

appearing in (

\displaystyle\mathring{\updelta}_{*}

\displaystyle=\max_{U\in[-U_{1},U_{2}]}[G(U)]_{-}=\max_{U\in[-U_{1},U_{2}]}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]\right]_{-},

(93)

where
[z]_{-}:=\max\{-z,0\}
is the negative part of

.

3.7.3.
The time of first blowup

Definition 3.8

(Time of first blowup)
.

Given any initial data function
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)
constructed above,
with
\mathring{\updelta}_{*}>0
as in (
81
),
we define:

\displaystyle T_{\textnormal{Shock}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\mathring{\updelta}_{*}}.

(

and
(

is the Minkowskian time
at which
\upmu

the positive time of first blowup for
|\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}|
.

3.7.4.
Sharp estimates for the inverse foliation density

Lemma 3.7

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}

\displaystyle:=\max_{U\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{c(U)}{\mathfrak{n}(U)}.

Note that by (

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<\mathfrak{c}\leq\mathfrak{C}<1.

Then there exists
7
7

7

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{c}^{-1}\mathring{\updelta}_{*}\leq\min_{\Sigma_{t}^{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}}L\upmu\leq\max_{\Sigma_{t}^{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}}L\upmu\leq-\mathfrak{C}^{-1}\mathring{\updelta}_{*}.

Moreover, for

\displaystyle\upmu(t,U)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)\frac{\mathfrak{b}}{2}U^{2}+\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)\mathring{\updelta}_{*}(T_{\textnormal{Shock}}-t),

\displaystyle L\upmu(t,U)

\displaystyle\{\breve{X}\upmu=0\}\cap\Sigma_{T_{\textnormal{Shock}}}^{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}

\displaystyle\min_{\Sigma_{T_{\textnormal{Shock}}}^{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}\backslash\Sigma_{T_{\textnormal{Shock}}}^{[-\frac{1}{2}U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},\frac{1}{2}U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}}|\breve{X}\upmu|

\upmu^{\textnormal{PS}}

\displaystyle\min_{\{(t,U)\ |\ t\in[0,T_{\textnormal{Shock}}],\,|u|\geq U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}\}}\upmu(t,U)

3.8.
The crease, singular curve, and Cauchy horizon in
1D
simple isentropic plane-symmetry

In this section,
we provide a description of a localized portion of the boundary of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD.
We emphasize the following key point, which follows from
(
69
),
(
77
),
and (
79
):

For the initial data from Sect.
3.7.2
,
the corresponding fluid solution
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
and the inverse foliation density

\upmu
are
explicit smooth functions
on all
9
9

9

In multi-dimensions, the best known estimates allow for the possibility of high order energy-blowup; see Sect.
6.9.3
.
However, even in multi-dimensions, the solution remains smooth at the low-to-mid order derivative levels.

of
\mathbb{R}^{2}

in the
(t,U)
differential structure
, even though
the Minkowskian partial derivative
\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
blows up in finite time.

Therefore, when we speak of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD, we are referring to the standard differential structure corresponding to the
(t,x^{1})
coordinates.
Nonetheless,
we prefer to carry out most our analysis of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD in
(t,U)
coordinates
,
thanks to the explicit structure of the equations and solutions in geometric coordinates revealed in
Cor.
3.6
. Hence, to reach conclusions about the behavior of solution in the
(t,x^{1})
-differential structure, we have to control the change of variables map
\Upsilon(t,U):=(t,x^{1})
; indeed,
in Theorem
3.1
, we reveal various diffeomorphism and homeomorphism properties of
\Upsilon
.

For the solutions and localized region under study,
when described in geometric coordinates,
the boundary of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD consists of two pieces, depicted in Fig.
2
:

(1)

A singular curve portion

\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}
, emanating from the first shock point – called the
crease
and denoted by
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}
–
such that
\upmu
vanishes on
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}
.

(2)

A Cauchy horizon portion, denoted by
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}
, which is a portion of
the integral curve of
\underline{L}
that
that emanates from
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}
, and along which
\upmu
is positive,
except at
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}
.

In particular, the image
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]})
,
depicted in Fig.
3
,
is a boundary of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD in the differential structure of the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates
(t,x^{1})
in the sense that
|\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}|
blows up when
\upmu
vanishes.
In contrast, even though
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
remains smooth all the way up to

\Upsilon(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}\backslash\partial_{-}\mathcal{B})
,

\Upsilon(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]})
is a boundary of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD
because the crease
\Upsilon(\partial_{-}\mathcal{B})
– a point at which
|\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}|
blows up –
lies in the
\mathbf{h}
-causal past of any point in
\Upsilon(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]})
.
We highlight again that the singular behavior is only visible relative to the differential structure of Minkowski space in
(t,x^{1})
coordinates. The discrepancy between the behavior of the solution in the two coordinate systems
is tied to the breakdown in the diffeomorphism property of
\Upsilon

when
\upmu
vanishes; see (
67
).

3.8.1.
Singular curve, crease, Cauchy horizon, and developments

We now define some subsets of geometric coordinate space
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{U}
that,
in Theorem
3.1
,
will play an important role in describing the shape of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD.
Many of our definitions are localized to the subset
\{|U|\leq U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}\}
,
where
U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}>0
is the constant from Lemma
3.7
.
This is mainly for convenience, since Lemma
3.7
provides sharp
control over the solution when
|U|\leq U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}
.

Definition 3.9

(Truncated portions of the singular curve and the crease)
.

For the solution launched by the compactly supported initial data
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}=\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)

constructed in Sect.

,
let
G
be the corresponding source term from (
71
),
viewed as a function of

.
We define:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{G(U)}.

(108)

Let
I\subset[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]
be a subset of
U
-values,
where
U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}>0
is the constant from Lemma
3.7
.
We define the
truncated singular curve
to be the
following subset of geometric coordinate space

\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{U}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{I}&:=\big\{(t,U)\in[0,\infty)\times I\ |\ \upmu(t,U)=0\big\}\cap\big\{(t,U)\in[0,\infty)\times I\ |\ \breve{X}\upmu(t,U)\leq 0\big\}\\
&=\big\{\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U),U\right)\ |\ U\in I\big\}\cap[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\cap\big\{(t,U)\in[0,\infty)\times I\ |\ \breve{X}\upmu(t,U)\leq 0\big\},\end{split}

(109)

where
\breve{X}

),

(
77
),
(
96
),
and
(
108

We define the

\displaystyle\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle:=\big\{(t,U)\in[0,\infty)\times[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]\ |\ \upmu(t,U)=0\big\}\cap\big\{(t,U)\in[0,\infty)\times[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]\ |\ \breve{X}\upmu(t,U)=0\big\}.

(110)

From
(

),
(
78
),
(

),
(

(

),

it follows that if

then

We therefore have the following alternate characterization of

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{I}&=\big\{\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U),U\right)\ |\ U\in I\cap[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]\big\}\cap[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}.\end{split}

(111)

We make the following remarks.

•

One might wonder why in the singular curve definition (
109
),
there is a restriction to regions in which
\breve{X}\upmu\leq 0
.
This is because the portion of
\{\upmu=0\}
on which we formally have
\breve{X}\upmu>0

never actually dynamically develops in the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD;
that “fictitious portion” is cut off by the Cauchy horizon before it has a chance to dynamically form; see also
Remark
3.2
.

•

Our terminology “crease” is motivated by picture of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD in multi-dimensions, where the
analog of
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}
is a co-dimension
2
hypersurface given by the intersection of the singular
boundary and the Cauchy horizon; see Fig.
6
.

•

From (
79
),
(
109
),
and (
111
),
we deduce the following key result:
in regions where we can
justify
the use of geometric coordinates
for reaching conclusions about the behavior of the solution in
(t,x^{1})
space,

\mathcal{B}_{I}
is the subset of points
(t,U)\in[0,\infty)\times\left(I\cap[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]\right)

such that
|\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(t,U)|
blows up at
\Upsilon(t,U)
.

In Theorem
3.1
, we will indeed justify the use of the geometric coordinates
when

.

•

(
77
),
(
93
),
and
(
96
)
imply that
T_{\textnormal{Shock}}
is the smallest value of

along
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}
.

•

To further flesh out the previous point, we note that for the solutions

,
the smallest value of

.

Definition 3.10

Then for an interval

We now define
\mathcal{M}_{*}

Definition 3.11

(Classical development regions)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}

11

11

With some additional effort, given the results of Theorem

, we could extend the region of spacetime on which
\Upsilon(t,U)=(t,x^{1})
is a homeomorphism to include the infinite region
\mathcal{M}_{*}^{\textnormal{Large}}:=\mathcal{M}_{*}\bigcup\left([0,T_{\textnormal{Shock}}]\times(-\infty,-U_{1})\cup(U_{2},\infty)\right)
.
We refrain from

\upmu
is uniformly positive on

\mathcal{M}_{*}^{\textnormal{Large}}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{*}

(see (

))
and thus no singularity occurs in that region.

3.8.2.
The main theorem

We now prove Theorem
3.1
, which is the main result of
Sect.
3
. On the region
\mathcal{M}_{*}
, the theorem provides a sharp description of the solution in the differential structure of the geometric coordinates
(t,U)
as well as the behavior of the solution on

\Upsilon(\mathcal{M}_{*})
in differential structure of the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates
(t,x^{1})
,
where we recall that
\Upsilon(t,U)=(t,x^{1})
is the change of variables map.
In particular, the theorem reveals various diffeomorphism and homeomorphism properties of
\Upsilon
and exhibits the blowup
of
|\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}|
as
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]})
is approached by points in
\Upsilon(\mathcal{M}_{*})
.
In Fig.
2
, we have depicted the region in geometric coordinate space

\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{U}
.
In Fig.
3
, we have depicted the
region in Minkowski-rectangular coordinate space
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{x^{1}}
, i.e., the image
under
\Upsilon
of the region from geometric coordinate space.

Remark 3.2

(Only a subset of
\{\upmu=0\}
is relevant for the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD)
.

The dotted curve in Fig.
2

labeled “
\{\upmu=0\}\cap\{\breve{X}\upmu>0\}
”
is not part of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD or its boundary. Formally,
\{\upmu=0\}\cap\{\breve{X}\upmu>0\}

is the portion of the level set
\{\upmu=0\}

that never has a chance to dynamically emerge because it is cut off by the Cauchy horizon portion

\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{(0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}
,
which lies below it

(as the proof of Theorem
3.1
shows).
For this reason, we do not display
\Upsilon\left(\{\upmu=0\}\cap\{\breve{X}\upmu>0\}\right)

in Fig.
3
.
One can use Taylor expansions to show that formally,

\Upsilon\left(\{\upmu=0\}\cap\{\breve{X}\upmu>0\}\right)
would
be a curve in
\Upsilon(\mathcal{M}_{\textnormal{Sing}})
that lies
below

\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]})

and thus formally,
\Upsilon
would not even be injective if its domain extended
up to
\{\upmu=0\}\cap\{\breve{X}\upmu>0\}
.

Theorem 3.1

(A large portion of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD, including a localized portion of its boundary)
.

Let
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
be initial data satisfying the assumptions of Sect.
3.7.2
,
and let
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
be the corresponding solution of the quasilinear initial value problem (
53
)
(with
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\equiv 0
).
Under the assumptions and conclusions of Lemma
3.7
,
perhaps shrinking the constant
U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}>0
if necessary, the following results hold,
where the notation
\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)
is defined in the statement of
Lemma
3.7
.

Classical existence with respect to the geometric coordinates.

With respect to the geometric coordinates
(t,U)
, the following results hold.

•

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
is a smooth function of
U
alone, i.e.,
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(t,U)=\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)
.

•

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
, the null vectorfields
L
and
\breve{\underline{L}}
,
the vectorfield
\breve{X}
,
and the inverse foliation density
\upmu
exist classically on

\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{U}
.

•

On
\{(t,U)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ U\notin[-U_{1},U_{2}]\}
,

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
vanishes,

L^{1}:=Lx^{1}=\overline{c}
,

\breve{\underline{L}}^{1}:=\breve{\underline{L}}x^{1}=-\overline{c}
,
and
\upmu=1/\overline{c}
,
where the constant
\overline{c}>0
denotes the speed of sound when the enthalpy is constantly
\overline{H}
,
where
\overline{H}>0
is the constant fixed in (
3
).

•

The following estimate holds:

\displaystyle L\upmu(t,U)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)\mathring{\updelta}_{*},

\displaystyle\mbox{for }(t,U)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}].

(119)

In particular, with
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}
denoting the closure of
the region
\mathcal{M}_{*}
defined in (
118
),
since Def.
3.11
implies that

\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]
,
it follows that the estimate (
119
) holds on

\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}
.

Description of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD in geometric coordinates.

•

Let
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}
be the singular curve portion
from (
111
),
and let
\mathcal{M}_{*}
be the subset of geometric coordinate space defined in (
118
).
Then the following disjoint union holds,
where
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}
is the closure of
\mathcal{M}_{*}
:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{*}\sqcup\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}.

(120)

•

The crease, which is defined in (
110
),
is a single point:

\displaystyle\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\{\left(T_{\textnormal{Shock}},0\right)\}.

(121)

•

The singular curve function
\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}=\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U)

from (
108
)
and
(
111
)
satisfies the following estimates:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)U,

\displaystyle U\in[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0],

(122a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U)

\displaystyle=T_{\textnormal{Shock}}+\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)U^{2},

\displaystyle U\in[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0].

(122b)

•

The rectangular spatial coordinate
x^{1}

satisfies the following estimate on
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}x^{1}\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)U,

\displaystyle U\in[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0].

(123)

•

The integral curve initial value problem for
\breve{\underline{\gamma}}(U)
given by
(
112a
)–(
112b
)
has a unique solution on the interval
U\in[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]
.
Hence, the truncated Cauchy horizon
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}
,
defined in (
115
),
can be parameterized as follows:

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}

\displaystyle=\{\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)\ |\ U\in[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]\},

(124)

as in (
115
).
Moreover,
\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U)
satisfies the following estimates:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)U^{2},

\displaystyle U\in[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}],

(125a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U)

\displaystyle=T_{\textnormal{Shock}}+\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)U^{3},

\displaystyle U\in[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}].

(125b)

•

The inverse foliation density
\upmu
,
defined in (
57
),
satisfies the following estimate along the Cauchy horizon portion
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}
:

\displaystyle\upmu\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)U^{2},

\displaystyle U\in[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}].

(126)

•

The rectangular spatial coordinate
x^{1}

satisfies the following estimate along the Cauchy horizon portion
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}x^{1}\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)U^{2},

\displaystyle U\in[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}].

(127)

•

\upmu
is positive on
\mathcal{M}_{*}
but vanishes on
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}
.
In particular, (
126
) implies
that
\upmu
is positive on
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{(0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}

but vanishes on the crease
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}\subset\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}
, which corresponds to
U=0
in
(
126
).

Description of the singularity formation and the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD in Minkowski-rectangular coordinates.

•

Let
\Upsilon(t,U):=(t,x^{1})
be the change of variables map from geometric coordinates to Minkowski-rectangular coordinates.
Then
\Upsilon
is a diffeomorphism from
\mathcal{M}_{*}
onto its image. Moreover,

\Upsilon
is a
homeomorphism
from
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}
onto its image.

•

The solution
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
exists classically relative to the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates
(t,x^{1})

on the subset
\Upsilon(\mathcal{M}_{*})
of
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{x^{1}}
.

•

The following estimate holds on
\Upsilon\left(\mathcal{M}_{*}\right)
:

\displaystyle\left|[\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}]\circ\Upsilon^{-1}(t,x^{1})\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\upmu}\left|\frac{\mathfrak{n}}{c}\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\right|\circ\Upsilon^{-1}(t,x^{1})=\frac{1}{\upmu}\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1),

\displaystyle(t,x^{1})\in\Upsilon\left(\mathcal{M}_{*}\right).

(128)

In particular, since
\upmu
vanishes along
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]})
,
given any point
(t_{*},x_{*}^{1})\in\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]})

and any sequence of points
\{(t_{m},x_{m}^{1})\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Upsilon\left(\mathcal{M}_{*}\right)

with
\lim_{m\to\infty}(t_{m},x_{m}^{1})=(t_{*},x_{*}^{1})
,
we have that
|\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}|\circ\Upsilon^{-1}\left((t_{m},x_{m}^{1})\right)\to\infty
as
m\to\infty
.

•

Recall that the vectorfield
L
is defined relative to the
(t,x^{1})
-coordinates in (
44a
)
(see also (
51a
)).
With respect to the
(t,x^{1})
-coordinates,
the solution
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
and all of its
L
-derivatives of any order, i.e.,
L^{M}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
for any
M
,
are continuous on
\Upsilon(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}})
,
including the singular curve portion
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]})
.
Moreover,
on
\Upsilon(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}})
,
relative to the
(t,x^{1})
-coordinates,
the
1D
acoustical metric
\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\beta}
from (
40a
)
is a continuous, non-degenerate,
2\times 2
matrix of signature
(-,+)
.

The causal structure of the Cauchy horizon.

•

\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}

is a
\mathbf{h}
-null curve portion, i.e., its tangent vector
is null with respect to
\mathbf{h}
.

The causal structure of the singular curve.

•

Relative to the geometric coordinates
(t,U)
,
the vectorfield
Q
defined by:

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle:=L-\frac{L(\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu)}{\breve{X}(\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu)}\breve{X}

(129)

is well-defined on the singular curve portion
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)}
, and
Q

is tangent to
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)}
.

•

On
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)}
, we have

Qt>0
and
QU<0
. In particular,

Q
is future-directed and transversal to the characteristics

\mathcal{P}_{U}
.

•

For every
q\in\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)}
,
the pushforward vectorfield
[\mathrm{d}\Upsilon(q)]\cdot Q(q)
, which is tangent to the curve

\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)})\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{x^{1}}
,
is equal to
L|_{\Upsilon(q)}=[L^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}]_{\Upsilon(q)}
.

•

For every
q\in\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)}
,
L|_{\Upsilon(q)}
is
\mathbf{h}
-orthogonal to the tangent space of

\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)})
.

Since there exist integral curves of the
\mathbf{h}
-null vectorfield
L^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}
that foliate and are
\mathbf{h}
-orthogonal to
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)})
, it follows that
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)})
is a
\mathbf{h}
-null curve in the Minkowskian coordinate differential structure.

Before proving the theorem, we make some remarks.

•

All the important results of the theorem are stable under perturbations of the initial data of

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
. That is, even though the theorem was stated only for initial data that satisfy
the assumptions of Sect.
3.7.2
, the proof shows that
if one perturbs any of those data by an arbitrary (small)
C^{4}
function, then the corresponding
perturbed simple isentropic plane-wave solution has an
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD/exhibits singularity formation
that is quantitatively and qualitatively close to the un-perturbed solution.

•

In a similar vein as the previous point, with only modest additional effort, the results of
Theorem
3.1
could be extended to
a large, open set of (non-simple) isentropic plane-symmetric solutions
in which
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
is sufficiently small compared to
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}

(or, alternatively, in which
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
is sufficiently small compared to
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
).

•

The emergence of the Cauchy horizon from the crease is fundamentally tied to the
fact that in
1D
, the isentropic compressible Euler equations have two characteristic directions,

L
and
\underline{L}
, as is evident from equations (
49a
)–(
49b
).
In particular, even within the class of simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions
in which
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
(which is transported by
\underline{L}
) vanishes,
when investigating the structure of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD,
one cannot “ignore” the
\underline{L}
direction.
This is in contrast to Burgers’ equation, in which there is only one characteristic direction.
Relatedly, for Burgers’ equation, there does not exist a notion of “globally hyperbolic”
that enjoys the same fundamental significance as the notion of “globally hyperbolic”
in the context of Lorentzian geometry, relativistic Euler flow, or wave equations.

•

From the point of view of determinism, the Cauchy horizon is an ally in that it “blocks” the non-uniqueness
of classical solutions (in
\mathbf{h}
-globally hyperbolic regions).
In particular, in Fig.
3
,
one can see that the Cauchy horizon prevents the characteristics in the left of the figure from entering into the region on the right,
where the fluid’s gradient blows up. That is, the Cauchy horizon prevents crossing of the characteristics

\Upsilon(\mathcal{M}_{*})
, which in turn prevents multi-valuedness/non-uniqueness of classical solutions in this region.
This should be contrasted against Burgers’ equation,
which exhibits non-uniqueness of classical solutions due to multi-valuedness stemming from the crossing of
characteristics emanating from widely separated regions in the initial data hypersurface.

•

In Fig.
3
,
along
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]})
,
one sees the interesting phenomenon of

non-uniqueness
of integral curves of
L
. In particular,
L
is tangent to

\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]})

and
the characteristics, which are the straight lines in the figure
that intersect
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]})
tangentially.
The non-uniqueness is tied to the blowup of
|\partial_{1}L^{1}|

along
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]})
, which in particular
shows that
L^{\alpha}
does not enjoy the Lipschitz regularity that lies
behind standard ODE uniqueness theorems for integral curves.

•

One can show that in
1D
, the acoustical metric takes the following form in geometric coordinates:

\mathbf{h}=-\upmu\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}U-\upmu\mathrm{d}U\otimes\mathrm{d}t+\upmu^{2}\mathrm{d}U\otimes\mathrm{d}U
.
In particular, in geometric coordinates,
\mathbf{h}

vanishes
at points such that

\upmu=0
. Hence,
\mathbf{h}
is not capable of “measuring” causality along the singular curve portion

\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}
, along which
\upmu
vanishes.
We note, however, that in more than one space dimension, even when
\upmu=0
,
there are some remaining non-degenerate components of
\mathbf{h}
in geometric coordinates.
Relatedly, in our
3D
paper
[
1
]
on the non-relativistic compressible Euler equations,
we prove that the
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}
is a co-dimension
2

\mathbf{h}
-spacelike submanifold of geometric coordinate space.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Throughout the proof, we will silently shrink the size of
U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}
as necessary;
this will in particular guarantee that some important terms arising in various
Taylor expansions have a sign.

Classical existence and properties of the solution relative to the geometric coordinates:
The fact that

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(t,U)=\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)
is smooth on all of
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{U}

was proved in (
69
).
In particular, in geometric coordinates,
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}

smoothly extends to the closure
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}
.
The fact that
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
vanishes on
the complement of
\{(t,U)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ U\in[-U_{1},U_{2}]\}
then follows from
our assumptions on the initial data, which in particular imply that

\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
is supported in
[-U_{1},U_{2}]
.

Next, using the identity (
77
),
the estimate (
96
),
and the fact that
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
is supported in
\{(t,U)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ U\in[-U_{1},U_{2}]\}
,
we conclude the asserted properties of
\upmu
in geometric coordinates,
except for the positivity of
\upmu
on
\mathcal{M}_{*}
, which we prove below.
The asserted properties of
L
,
\breve{\underline{L}}
,
Lx^{1}
, and
\underline{L}x^{1}
in geometric coordinates then follow from
(
51a
)–(
51b
),
(
62
),
(
63
),
and the fact that
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
is supported in
\{(t,U)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ U\in[-U_{1},U_{2}]\}
.

Proof of the properties of
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}
relative to the geometric coordinates:

The fact that the crease is the single point
(T_{\textnormal{Shock}},0)
is a consequence of
definition (
110
),
(
98
),
and (
103
).
We have therefore proved (
121
).

From
(
77
),
(
96
),
(
108
),
(
109
),
(
111
),
and definition (
116
),
we see that
\upmu>0
on
\mathcal{M}_{\textnormal{Sing}}
while
\upmu
vanishes on

\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}
.

The estimate (
119
) follows from the first equality in
(
76
) and the estimate (
107a
).

The estimates (
122a
)–(
122b
)
follow from definitions (
94
) and (
108
)
and the estimates
(
107b
)–(
107c
).

Proof of the properties of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}
relative to the geometric coordinates:

We first prove that the integral curve initial value problem for
\breve{\underline{\gamma}}(U)
given by
(
112a
)–(
112b
) (or equivalently, (
114
))
has a unique solution on the interval
U\in[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]
.
Since
\upmu
is a smooth function on all of
(t,U)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the standard existence and uniqueness theorem for ODEs
yields a unique solution to (
114
)
for
U\in[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]
, provided we shrink the size of
U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}
if necessary.

We now prove (
125a
)–(
125b
).
Differentiating (
114
) and using
(
62
) and the chain rule,
we deduce that:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}U^{2}}\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}[\upmu(L\upmu)]|_{(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U)}+\frac{1}{2}\breve{X}\upmu|_{(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U)},

(130)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}}{\mathrm{d}U^{3}}\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U)&=\frac{1}{8}[\upmu^{2}(LL\upmu)]_{\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)}+\frac{1}{8}[\upmu(L\upmu)^{2}]_{\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)}+\frac{1}{4}[(\breve{X}\upmu)(L\upmu)]_{\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)}\\
&\ \ +\frac{1}{2}[\upmu(\breve{X}L\upmu)]_{\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)}+\frac{1}{2}\breve{X}\breve{X}\upmu|_{\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)}.\end{split}

(131)

Using
(
62
),
(
114
),
(
130
)–(
131
),
and the Taylor expansions
(
98
)–(
102
),
we can Taylor expand
the solution
\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U)
to (
114
) around
U=0
,
thereby concluding (
125a
)–(
125b
).

Since (
114
) implies that

\upmu\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)=2\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U)

along
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{(0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}
, we can use the Taylor expansions
described in the previous paragraph to conclude the estimate (
126
)
for
\upmu
.

To prove (
127
),
we first use
(
51b
),
(
59
),
(
112a
),
our assumed smallness on the amplitude of the initial data
(which in particular implies that the ratio

\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)-c\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)}{\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)-c\sinh\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}{2}\right)}

from RHS (
44b
)
(with
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}\equiv 0
)
is
-\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)
),
and the chain rule
to deduce that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}x^{1}\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)=\frac{1}{2}\breve{\underline{L}}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)=\frac{1}{2}[\upmu\underline{L}^{1}]\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right)=-\upmu\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1)
. From this relation and (
126
), we conclude
(
127
).

Finally,
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}
is a
\mathbf{h}
-null curve portion because its tangent vector is everywhere proportional to
the vectorfield
\breve{\underline{L}}
, which is
\mathbf{h}
-null by (
45
)
and (
59
).

Proof of (
120
):

We have already shown that
\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U)

is smooth on
[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]
,
that
\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U)
is smooth on

[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]
, and that

\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(0)=\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(0)=T_{\textnormal{Shock}}
.
From these facts,
(
111
),
and
(
116
)–(
118
),
we conclude (
120
).

Proof that
\upmu>0
on
\mathcal{M}_{*}
:

We have already shown that
\upmu>0
on
\mathcal{M}_{\textnormal{Sing}}
.
Hence, in view of definition
118
,
we see that to prove
\upmu>0
on
\mathcal{M}_{*}
, it suffices to prove

\upmu>0
on
\mathcal{M}_{\textnormal{Reg}}
.
To this end, we first note that the same arguments given above imply that
the curve
U\rightarrow\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U),U\right)

is smooth for
U\in[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]

and that
\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U)
satisfies the estimate stated in (
122b
)
for
U\in[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]

(not just for
U\in[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]
). The portion of this curve corresponding to
U\in(0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]

is shown as a dotted curve in Fig.
2
;
for purposes of this proof,
we denote this dotted curve, which by definition does not contain the crease,
by “
\mathcal{B}_{\textnormal{Fictitious}}
”
(“fictitious” because the image of
\mathcal{B}_{\textnormal{Fictitious}}
under
\Upsilon
in
(t,x^{1})
-space is not part of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD).
The same arguments that we used to prove that
\upmu>0
on
\mathcal{M}_{\textnormal{Sing}}

(which relied on (
77
),
(
96
),
and
(
108
))
imply that
\upmu>0
on

\mathcal{D}:=\left\{(t,U)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ 0\leq t<\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U),\,U\in(0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]\right\}
,
where we note that the top boundary of this region is
\mathcal{B}_{\textnormal{Fictitious}}
.
The key point is that
(
113
),
(
115
),
and the estimates
(
122b
) (valid also for
U\in(0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]
)
and
(
125b
)
imply that
\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U)<\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U)

for
U\in(0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]
and thus
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{(0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]}
lies
below

\mathcal{B}_{\textnormal{Fictitious}}
.
In view of definition (
117
),
we see that
\mathcal{M}_{*}\subset\mathcal{D}

and hence
\upmu>0
on
\mathcal{M}_{\textnormal{Reg}}
, as desired.

Proof of the diffeomorphism and homeomorphism properties of
\Upsilon
:

From Def.
3.11
,
we see that the top boundary of
\mathcal{M}_{*}

is the following
U
-parameterized curve
(note that the curve is well-defined since
\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(0)=\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(0)=T_{\textnormal{Shock}}
):

\displaystyle\upgamma_{\textnormal{top}}(U)

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{top}}(U),U),

\displaystyle U\in[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}],

(132)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{top}}(U)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{Sing}}(U),U),&U\in[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0],\\
\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{CH}}(U),U\right),&U\in[0,U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}].\end{cases}

(133)

Using
(
123
),
(
127
),
and the mean value theorem,
we see that
x^{1}(\mathfrak{t}_{\textnormal{top}}(U),U)
is strictly decreasing
for
U\in[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}}]
, i.e., the
x^{1}

coordinate function strictly decreases as we move from right to left along
the top boundary of
\mathcal{M}_{*}
in Fig.
2
.
Next, we note that (
59
) and (
60
)
imply that
\frac{\partial}{\partial U}x^{1}=-\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu
.
Using this identity, the fact that
\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}>0
, and the fact that we have already shown that
\upmu>0
on
\mathcal{M}_{*}

and
\upmu
vanishes precisely along the curve portion
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]}
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}
,
we see that for any
(t,U)\in\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}
,
the map
U\rightarrow x^{1}(t,U)
is strictly decreasing.
From these two monotonicity properties of
x^{1}
, we conclude, given the shape of

\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}
(see Fig.
2
),
that the map
\Upsilon(t,U)=(t,x^{1}(t,U))
is
injective on the compact set
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}
and thus is a homeomorphism
from
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}
onto
\Upsilon(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}})
.
Also considering the identity

\det\mathrm{d}\Upsilon=-\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu
proved in
(
67
), we further conclude that

\Upsilon
is a diffeomorphism on
\mathcal{M}_{*}
, where
\upmu>0
.

Proof of (
128
) and the structure of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD in Minkowski-rectangular coordinates
(t,x^{1})
:

We deduce from
(
59
),
(
60
),
(
69
),
(
76
),
the chain rule,
the non-degeneracy assumption (
88
),
and (
119
)
that for
(t,U)\in\mathcal{M}_{*}
,
the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big|\upmu\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}\big|(t,U)&=\frac{\mathfrak{n}(U)}{c(U)}\left|\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)\right|=\frac{\mathfrak{n}(U)}{c(U)}\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)\right|=\frac{\mathfrak{n}(U)}{c(U)}\left|\frac{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}U}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]}\right|\\
&=\left|\frac{L\upmu(U)}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}}\mathfrak{A}[\mathring{\mathcal{R}}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(U)]}\right|=\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{pos}}(1).\end{split}

(134)

From (
134
), we conclude (
128
).

The continuity of
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}},\,L^{M}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}
and

\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\beta}=\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})

all the way up to
\Upsilon(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}})
in Minkowski-rectangular coordinates

(t,x^{1})
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}(t,U)
is smooth on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{U}

and the already proven fact that
\Upsilon
is a homeomorphism from
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}}

onto
\Upsilon(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{*}})
.
The non-degeneracy of the
2\times 2
matrix
\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\beta}
relative to the
(t,x^{1})
-coordinates
follows from the expression (
40a
) for
\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\beta}=\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}})
,
our assumed smallness on the amplitude of the initial data (which in particular implies that
0<c\leq 1
),
and the identities stated in Prop.
3.2
.

Proof of the causal structure of the singular curve in Minkowski-rectangular coordinates
(t,x^{1})
:

Using (
62
),
(
76
)–(
77
),
and
(
109
),
we deduce that along
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)}
,
the vectorfield
Q

defined by (
129
)
can be expressed as
follows, where

G
is the source term from (
71
), viewed as a function of
U
,
and
G^{\prime}
is its derivative:

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\frac{G(U)}{tG^{\prime}(U)}\frac{\partial}{\partial U}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\frac{G^{2}(U)}{G^{\prime}(U)}\frac{\partial}{\partial U},

\displaystyle\mbox{along }\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)}.

(135)

Hence, using (
107a
) and (
107c
),
we see that
Q
is well-defined on
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)}
,
i.e., the denominator term
G^{\prime}(U)
on RHS (
135
)
is non-zero for
U\in[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)
.
Next, we deduce from (
129
) that

Q\left(\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu\right)=0
, i.e., that
Q
is tangent to the level sets of
\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu
.
Using the fact that
\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}>0
(see (
96
)),
we see that the zero level set of
\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu

coincides with the zero level set of
\upmu
. Hence, in view of definition (
109
), we see that

Q
is tangent to
\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)}
.
The inequalities
Qt>0
and
QU<0

follow easily from
(
135
),
(
107a
),
and (
107c
).

Next, using (
59
),
(
60
),
and (
62
),
we see that the pushforward of
\frac{\partial}{\partial U}
by
\Upsilon
, i.e.,
\mathrm{d}\Upsilon\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial U}

is equal to
-\frac{c}{\mathfrak{n}}\upmu\partial_{1}
.
Hence,
\mathrm{d}\Upsilon\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial U}

vanishes along all of
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)})
, where
\upmu
is
0
.
Moreover, from
(
62
)
, we deduce that

\mathrm{d}\Upsilon\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}=L^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}
.
From these facts and (
135
), we deduce
that for every
q\in\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)}
,
we have
[\mathrm{d}\Upsilon(q)]\cdot Q(q)=L|_{\Upsilon(q)}
.
In particular,
since
L|_{\Upsilon(q)}
spans the tangent space of
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)})
and is
\mathbf{h}
-null
(see (
45
)),
it follows that
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0)})
is a
\mathbf{h}
-null curve.

∎

4.
The new formulation of the flow in three spatial dimensions

In Sect.
6
, we provide an overview of how to extend some aspects of
the simple isentropic plane-symmetric shock formation results yielded Theorem
3.1
to the
much harder case of three spatial dimensions in the presence of vorticity and entropy.
The strategy we present in Sect.
6
is based on the approach we used in studying shock formation
for the non-relativistic multi-dimensional compressible Euler equations
[
65
,
67
,
1
]
.
Fundamental to that strategy is the availability of a new formulation of the flow as
as system of geometric wave-transport-div-curl equations that exhibit remarkable regularity properties
and geometric null structures. As we explain in Sect.
6
,
such a formulation allows one to implement multi-dimensional nonlinear geometric optics,
which is important for the study of shocks
(as is already evident from Theorem
3.1
).
In the non-relativistic case, such a formulation was derived in
[
66
,
94
]
,
while in the relativistic case, such a formulation was derived in
[
41
]
.
In Sect.
4
, in the relativistic case,
we set up the machinery needed to state the formulation from
[
41
]
and present it in a simplified,
schematic form as Theorem
4.1
.
In Sect.
4.2.5
, we make some brief comments on how the new formulation can be extended to more general spacetimes
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
.

4.1.
Connection to quasilinear wave equations

Although our study of isentropic plane-symmetric solutions in Sect.
4
was based on
analyzing the first-order Riemann invariant system (
49a
)–(
49b
),
as we will see in Sect.
6
, in multi-dimensions, it is advantageous
to work with the formulation of the flow provided by Theorem
4.1
,
which features geometric wave equations.
The main advantage is that for geometric wave equations, there is an advanced geo-analytic machinery available,
tied to the vectorfield method, for studying the global behavior of solutions.
To motivate the relevance of wave equations for the study of relativistic fluids,
we now establish a simple connection between quasilinear wave equations and the Riemann invariant formulation
of isentropic plane-symmetric solutions that we provided in Sect.
4
.
More precisely, upon differentiating (
49a
)–(
49b
),
we deduce that for isentropic plane-symmetric solutions, the Riemann invariants

(\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}},\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}})
satisfy the following coupled system of quasilinear wave equations:

\displaystyle\underline{L}L\mathcal{R}_{(+)}^{\textnormal{PS}}

\displaystyle=0,

(136a)

\displaystyle L\underline{L}\mathcal{R}_{(-)}^{\textnormal{PS}}

\displaystyle=0.

(136b)

Equations (
136a
)–(
136b
) are quasilinear wave equations
because by Lemma
3.1
, the principal operator on the LHSs
is proportional to
(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}
,
where
\mathbf{h}
is the acoustical metric.

4.2.
Additional fluid variables

The remainder of Sect.
4
concerns solutions

\left(h,\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\mathbf{u}^{3},s\right)

to the relativistic Euler equations (
12a
)–(
12d
)
in three spatial dimensions without any symmetry, isentropicity, or irrotationality assumptions.

In Sect.
4.2
,
we introduce some additional fluid variables that complement the ones
we introduced in Sect.
2
. The new variables play a fundamental role
in the new formulation of relativistic Euler flow provided by Theorem
4.1
.

Definition 4.1

(The
\mathbf{u}
-orthogonal vorticity of a one-form)
.

Let
\bm{\upxi}
be a one-form.
We define its
\mathbf{u}
-orthogonal vorticity, denoted by

\mbox{vort}^{\alpha}(\bm{\upxi})
, to be the following vectorfield, where

\upepsilon
is the fully antisymmetric symbol normalized by
\upepsilon_{0123}=1
:

\displaystyle\mbox{vort}^{\alpha}(\bm{\upxi})

\displaystyle:=-\upepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\mathbf{u}_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}\bm{\upxi}_{\delta},

(137)

and we recall that throughout the paper,
we use the Minkowski metric
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
– as opposed to
\mathbf{h}
– to lower and raise indices.
In particular,
\upepsilon^{0123}=-1
.

Note that
\mathbf{u}_{\alpha}\mbox{vort}^{\alpha}(\bm{\upxi})=0
, which explains our
terminology “
\mathbf{u}
-orthogonal vorticity” of
\bm{\upxi}
.

Definition 4.2

(Vorticity vectorfield)
.

We define the vorticity
\varpi^{\alpha}
to be the following vectorfield:

\displaystyle\varpi^{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\mbox{vort}^{\alpha}(H\mathbf{u})=-\upepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\mathbf{u}_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}(H\mathbf{u}_{\delta}).

(138)

Definition 4.3

(Entropy gradient one-form)
.

We define
S_{\alpha}
to be the following one-form:

\displaystyle S_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\partial_{\alpha}s.

(139)

The fluid variables

\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
from the next definition play a fundamental
role in the setup of the new formulation of the flow. In particular,
Theorem
4.1

shows that they satisfy transport equations with source terms that
enjoy remarkable regularity and null structures.

Definition 4.4

(Modified fluid variables)
.

We define
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}

and

\mathcal{D}

to respectively be the following vectorfield and scalar function:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}&:=\mbox{vort}^{\alpha}(\varpi)+c^{-2}\upepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\mathbf{u}_{\beta}(\partial_{\gamma}h)\varpi_{\delta}\\
&\ \ +(\uptheta-\uptheta_{;h})S^{\alpha}(\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa})+(\uptheta-\uptheta_{;h})u^{\alpha}(S^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h)\\
&\ \ +(\uptheta_{;h}-\uptheta)S^{\kappa}((\bm{\mathfrak{m}})^{-1})^{\alpha\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\mathbf{u}_{\kappa}),\end{split}

(140a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n}(\partial_{\kappa}S^{\kappa})+\frac{1}{n}(S^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h)-\frac{1}{n}c^{-2}(S^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h).

(140b)

4.2.1.
Covariant wave operator

Covariant wave equations play a central role in Theorem
4.1
.
In Def.
4.5
, we recall the standard definition of a covariant wave operator acting on scalar functions;
in Theorem
4.1
, we apply the covariant wave operator
only

to quantities that we view to be scalar functions.

Definition 4.5

(Covariant wave operator acting on a scalar function)
.

The covariant wave operator
\square_{\mathbf{h}}
acts on scalar functions
\varphi
as follows:

\displaystyle\square_{\mathbf{h}}\varphi

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mbox{det}\mbox{$\mathbf{h}$}|}}\partial_{\alpha}\left\{\sqrt{|\mbox{det}\mathbf{h}|}(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\varphi\right\}.

(141)

4.2.2.
Null forms

In Def.
4.6
, we recall the definition of
standard null forms relative to
\mathbf{h}
.
The key point is that in Theorem
4.1
, all derivative-quadratic terms
on the RHS of the equations are null forms relative to the acoustical metric.
In Sect.
6.8.3
, we will provide a detailed explanation as to why, in the context of shock formation,
such null forms are harmless error terms. However, at this point in the paper,
we merely state that
\mathbf{h}
-null forms are derivative-quadratic nonlinearities
that, when a shock forms, are
strictly weaker than the
Riccati-type terms that drive the blowup
(see Sect.
3.5
).
As we will further explain in Remark
6.4
,
the dangerous Riccati-type terms are “hiding” in the definition of the covariant wave operator on LHS (
147a
).

Definition 4.6

(Standard null forms relative to
\mathbf{h}
)
.

We define the standard null forms relative to
\mathbf{h}
(which we refer to as “standard
\mathbf{h}
-null forms” for short)
to be the following derivative-quadratic terms,
where
\phi
and
\psi
are scalar functions and
0\leq\mu<\nu\leq 3
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}^{(\mathbf{h})}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi,\boldsymbol{\partial}\psi)

\displaystyle:=(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}\partial_{\kappa}\phi\partial_{\lambda}\psi,

(142a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{\mu\nu}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi,\boldsymbol{\partial}\psi)

\displaystyle:=\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\psi-\partial_{\nu}\phi\partial_{\mu}\psi.

(142b)

4.2.3.
Two vectorfields

The two vectorfields featured in the following definition will play a role in our forthcoming discussion.

Definition 4.7

(
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{N}
)
.

We define
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{N}
to respectively be the vectorfields
with the following components relative to the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\mathbf{u}^{0}}\mathbf{u}^{\alpha},

(143a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=-(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}t=-(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha 0}.

(143b)

Note that
\mathbf{B}t=1
, while by (
19
),

\mathbf{N}
is the future-directed
\mathbf{h}
-unit normal to
\Sigma_{t}
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{N})

\displaystyle=-1.

(144)

We also compute that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{N})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{B}^{0}=-1

(145)

and, with the help of (
12d
) and (
17a
), that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}(\mathbf{B},L)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{n}\bm{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{B},L).

(146)

Note that
\mbox{RHS~\eqref{E:GINNERPRODUCTOFTRANSPORTANDLUNIT}}<0

because
\mathfrak{n}>0
,

\mathbf{B}
is future-directed and
\mathbf{h}
-timelike,

L
is future-directed and
\mathbf{h}
-null,
and the
\mathbf{h}
-null cones are inside the
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}
-null cones (see Remark
2.3
).

4.2.4.
The geometric wave-transport-div-curl formulation of the flow

We now state – in a somewhat abbreviated form – the formulation of relativistic Euler flow derived in
[
41
]
.
The proof of theorem is based on differentiating the equations
(
12a
)–(
12d
) in well-chosen directions
and finding a myriad of cancellations and special structures.

Theorem 4.1
.

[
41
]
*The geometric wave-transport-div-curl formulation of the flow

Let
\vec{\Psi}=\left(h,\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\mathbf{u}^{3},s\right)
be a

C^{3}
solution to the relativistic Euler equations (
12a
)–(
12d
)
on the Minkowski spacetime background. Then for
\Psi\in\vec{\Psi}
, the following equations hold, where

\mathfrak{Q}
denotes a linear combination of standard
\mathbf{h}
-null forms (see Def.
4.6
)
and
\mathrm{f}
denotes a smooth function, both of which are
free to vary from line to line:

\displaystyle\square_{\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})}\Psi

\displaystyle=\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})+\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot\mathfrak{Q}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi},\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi})+\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi},S)\cdot(\varpi,S)\cdot\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi},

(147a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}(\varpi^{i},S^{i})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi},S)\cdot(\varpi,S)\cdot\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi},

(147b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{B}(\mathcal{C}^{i},\mathcal{D})&=\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot\mathfrak{Q}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\varpi,\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi})+\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot\mathfrak{Q}(\boldsymbol{\partial}S,\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi})\\
&\ \ +\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot(\varpi,S)\cdot\mathfrak{Q}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi},\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi})\\
&\ \ +\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi}\cdot(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})+\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot S\cdot\boldsymbol{\partial}S+\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi},\varpi,S)\cdot(\varpi,S)\cdot\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi},\end{split}

(147c)

\displaystyle\partial_{\kappa}\varpi^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot\varpi\cdot\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi},\qquad\mbox{vort}^{\alpha}(S)=0.

(147d)

4.2.5.
Comments on the case of more general ambient spacetimes
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})

Here we make some brief comments on some of the adjustments that would be needed to extend the results of
Sect.
4
to general
\mathbf{g}
-globally hyperbolic spacetimes
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
.
By
\mathbf{g}
-globally hyperbolic, we mean that there exists a
\mathbf{g}
-Cauchy hypersurface

\Sigma_{0}\subset\mathcal{M}
.
By a “
\mathbf{g}
-Cauchy hypersurface,”
we mean a co-dimension one submanifold
\Sigma_{0}
such that every inextendible

\mathbf{g}
-causal curve in
\mathcal{M}
intersects
\Sigma_{0}
exactly once. It is well known
[
45
,
17
]
that

\mathbf{g}
-globally hyperbolic spacetimes admit a smooth time function
t\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
.
By a “time function,” we mean that

t^{-1}(0)=\Sigma_{0}
,
that the level sets
\Sigma_{t^{\prime}}:=\{t\equiv t^{\prime}\}
are
\mathbf{g}
-spacelike Cauchy hypersurfaces,
that
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{t}\Sigma_{t}
, and
that
t
has a past-directed timelike gradient
\nabla^{\#}t:=\mathbf{g}^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}t
(which is everywhere normal to
\Sigma_{t}
).
Here,
\nabla^{\#}t
denotes the vectorfield equal to the dual of
\nabla t
with respect to
\mathbf{g}
.

First, we note that
the extra terms described in Remark
2.1
would
lead to the presence of extra terms in the system (
147a
)–(
147d
).
However, as we described in Remark
2.1
, in the context of
the study of shock formation, in which fluid gradients are large, the extra terms
would be small relative to the shock-driving Riccati-type fluid terms
(which are “hidden” in
\square_{\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})}\Psi
; see Remark
6.4
).

Next, we note that under the above assumptions, we can define a lapse function
\Phi
by
\Phi:=(-\mathbf{g}(\nabla t,\nabla t))^{-1/2}
.
We can then define the vectorfield
T:=-\Phi^{2}\nabla^{\#}t
, which is
\mathbf{g}
-normal to
\Sigma_{t}
and satisfies
Tt=1
.
Hence, given a coordinate system
(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
on
\Sigma_{0}
,
it can be propagated to any
\Sigma_{t}
by the flow of
T
,
thereby yielding a coordinate system
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
on spacetime.
In this setup, the ambient spacetime metric can be decomposed as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}

\displaystyle=-\Phi^{2}\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+g,

(148)

where
g=g(t)
is the Riemannian metric on
\Sigma_{t}
induced by
\mathbf{g}
, i.e.,
the first fundamental form of
\Sigma_{t}
with respect to
\mathbf{g}
.
Given
\mathbf{g}
and the relativistic fluid, the corresponding acoustical metric
\mathbf{h}
is defined by
(
21
).

The upshot of introducing a time function
t
is that it allows for many of the constructions from this section to easily be generalized.
For example, using (
2
) and (
148
), one sees that

\mathbf{u}^{0}:=\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla t:=\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}t>0

and hence one could naturally define the analog of (
143a
)
by
\mathbf{B}:=\frac{1}{\mathbf{u}^{0}}\mathbf{u}
and
the analog of (
143b
) by
\mathbf{N}:=-\mathbf{h}^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}t
.

5.
Some prior works on shocks

In this section, we discuss some prior works on shocks.
There is a vast literature, and we cannot hope to mention all of it here.
We have aimed to discuss works that provide further context and motivation for
Sects.
2
and
4

and to help prepare the reader for Sects.
6

and
7
.

5.1.
Comments on the
1D
theory

In Riemann’s foundational paper
[
83
]
, he developed the method of Riemann invariants and combined it with the method of
characteristics to prove that for the
1D
non-relativistic compressible Euler equations, there exist large sets of initial data such that a shock forms in the sense that the fluid’s gradient blows up in finite time, though the fluid variables themselves remain bounded.
Once one has formulated the flow in terms of Riemann invariants, the proof of the blowup is based on differentiating the equations
to obtain a coupled system of Riccati-type equations along the characteristics. This can be viewed as a
multi-directional (i.e., there are two characteristic directions in
1D
isentropic compressible Euler flow)
version of the blowup of
\partial_{x}\Psi

that often occurs in solutions to Burgers’ equation
\partial_{t}\Psi(t,x)+\Psi\partial_{x}\Psi(t,x)=0
;
in Sect.
3.5
, we discussed this approach in more detail in the case of
simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions to the relativistic Euler equations.
Lax later generalized
[
62
]
Riemann’s results to
2\times 2
genuinely nonlinear hyperbolic systems in
1D
.
John later extended
[
53
]
Lax’s blowup-results to apply to some systems in
1D
with more than two unknowns.
Christodoulou and Raoul-Perez used a sharpened, more geometric version
(in the style of our proof of Theorem
3.1
)
of Lax’s approach to give a detailed proof of shock formation in
1D
for electromagnetic waves in nonlinear crystals
[
31
]
.
Related results have recently been proved for plane-symmetric solutions to the equations of elasticity

[
12
]
and the equations of non-relativistic magnetohydrodynamics
[
13
]
,
and in these works, the plane-symmetric blowup was also used to prove ill-posedness results in
3D
;
see also the survey article
[
14
]
.
For the relativistic Euler equations in
1D
, stable blowup-results
have been proved for large sets of initial data
(which are allowed to be large) for isentropic flows
[
16
]
as well as flows with dynamic entropy

[
15
]
.
Theorem
3.1
provides a sharpened version of these kind of results for
simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions to the relativistic Euler equations; by “sharpened,” we mean that,
different from the works mentioned above,
Theorem
3.1
follows the solution all the way up to the singular boundary
and Cauchy horizon, rather than just up to the time of first blowup.

Even though shocks can form for large classes of hyperbolic PDEs, signifying the end of the classical evolution,
ideally, one would like to develop a theory of weak
solutions that can accommodate the formation of shocks and their subsequent interactions. In
1D
,
a robust such theory exists, at least for strictly hyperbolic systems and initial data that are small in a suitable
bounded variation (BV) function space. We refer to
[
39
]
for a comprehensive discussion of the
1D
theory.
In multi-dimensions, much less is known. A key obstacle is that it is known,
thanks to the important paper
[
80
]
by Rauch,
that quasilinear hyperbolic PDEs are typically ill-posed in BV spaces. In fact, for most
multi-dimensional quasilinear hyperbolic systems
of interest, even local well-posedness is known
only
in
L^{2}
-type Sobolev spaces. In practice, this means that to prove
shock formation in multi-dimensions, one must commute the equations and derive energy estimates up the singularity;
this turns out to be a difficult task, for reasons we discuss in detail in Sect.
6
.

5.2.
Blowup-results in multi-dimensions without symmetry: proofs by contradiction

In light of the previous paragraph,
it is perhaps not surprising that the first blowup-results without symmetry assumptions
in fluid mechanics were non-constructive.
Specifically, in
[
85
]
, Sideris studied the
3D
compressible Euler equations under equations of state that satisfy a convexity assumption. For open sets of initial data (including near-constant-state-data) without symmetry or irrotationality assumptions,
he used arguments based on integrated quantities to give a proof by contradiction that a smooth solution cannot exist for all time.
For the
3D
relativistic Euler equations and relativistic Euler–Maxwell equations, Guo and Tahvildar-Zadeh proved similar results

[
48
]
for open sets of initial data that are
large
perturbations of constant-states with positive density.
In contrast to the present work,
[
85
,
48
]
relied on convexity assumptions on the equation of state.

5.3.
Proofs of shock formation in multi-dimensions via nonlinear geometric optics

Alinhac
[
9
,
8
,
10
,
11
]

was the first to prove stable shock formation without symmetry assumptions
for scalar quasilinear wave equations in
2D
and
3D

of the form:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\Phi)\partial_{\kappa}\partial_{\lambda}\Phi

\displaystyle=0

(149)

whenever the nonlinearities in (
149
) fail to satisfy the null condition.
In (
149
),
\mathbf{h}
is a Lorentzian metric whose components

\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\beta}
in the standard coordinate system are prescribed functions of
\boldsymbol{\partial}\Phi
,
i.e., functions of the spacetime-gradient of
\Phi
in the standard coordinate system.
Alinhac’s proof applied to open sets of initial data that satisfy a non-degeneracy assumption that guaranteed
that within the constant-time hypersurface of first blowup, the singularity is an isolated point.
His non-degeneracy assumption guaranteed that the singular boundary
\mathcal{B}
and its past boundary
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}

are strictly convex, as in Fig.
4
, and in the context of the figure, his approach allowed
him to follow the solution up to the flat constant-time hypersurface that contains the point
b_{\textnormal{lowest}}
;
in Sect.
5.5
, we discuss
\mathcal{B}
and
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}
in more detail.
Alinhac’s main results showed that at
b_{\textnormal{lowest}}
, a shock forms in the sense that

\boldsymbol{\partial}^{2}\Phi
blows up while
\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\leq 1}\Phi
remains bounded.
His proof relied on nonlinear geometric optics
(i.e., eikonal functions and the method of characteristics,
as in Sects.
3.6
and
6.3
),
and his proof showed that relative to a
geometric coordinate system
constructed out of the eikonal function,

\Phi
and its partial derivatives remain bounded, except possibly at the high derivative levels.
To close the energy estimates in the geometric coordinate system without derivative loss,
he relied on a Nash–Moser iteration scheme that necessarily terminated at the time of first blowup.

\begin{overpic}[crease_convex_cartesian.pdf]
\put(51.0,8.0){$\Sigma_{0}$}
\put(4.0,20.0){$(x^{2},x^{3})$}
\put(-2.0,21.0){$t$}
\put(3.0,11.0){$x^{1}$}
\put(38.0,23.0){$\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}$}
\put(55.5,63.0){$\mathcal{B}$}
\put(51.0,30.0){$b_{\textnormal{lowest}}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4.
Strictly convex crease and singular boundary

In Christodoulou’s breakthrough monograph
[
34
]
,
he proved a substantially sharper result for the
3D
relativistic Euler equations
in regions where the solution is irrotational and isentropic. The dynamics in such regions
is described by a scalar quasilinear wave equation for a potential function
\Phi

(i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\partial}\Phi
determines the four-velocity and proper energy density)
that is of the form (
149
)
and that is invariant under the Poincaré group.
Like Alinhac, Christodoulou used an eikonal function in his proof, that is, a solution
U

to the eikonal equation:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\Phi)\partial_{\kappa}U\partial_{\lambda}U

\displaystyle=0.

(150)

The level sets of
U
are characteristic for equation (
149
), i.e.,
hypersurfaces that are null with respect to
\mathbf{h}
.
Also like Alinhac, Christodoulou
constructed a geometric coordinate system tied to
U
and proved that relative to the geometric coordinates,
the solution remains smooth, except possibly at the high derivative levels.
He showed that for open sets of initial data, the shock singularity
–
i.e., the blowing-up of the second-order Minkowski-rectangular derivatives

\boldsymbol{\partial}^{2}\Phi
while
\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\leq 1}\Phi
remains bounded
–
is tied to the vanishing of the
inverse foliation density

\upmu
,
defined by:

\displaystyle\upmu

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\Phi)\partial_{\kappa}U\partial_{\lambda}t}=-\frac{1}{(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa 0}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\Phi)\partial_{\kappa}U},

(151)

where
t
is the standard time coordinate on Minkowski spacetime.
The vanishing of
\upmu
signifies the infinite density of the level-sets of
U
in Minkowski-spacetime, i.e.,
the infinite density of the characteristics and the blowup of
\boldsymbol{\partial}U
,
as in Theorem
3.1
.
Motivated by techniques used in his joint proof of the stability of Minkowski spacetime with Klainerman
[
28
]
,
Christodoulou was able to avoid Nash–Moser estimates. Rather, to avoid derivative loss in the eikonal function
and to close the proof, he used a
geometric energy method
for wave equations and
U
;
see Sect.
6

for further discussion of a related geometric energy method
in the context of the
3D
relativistic Euler equations with vorticity and entropy.

The results of
[
34
]
go beyond Alinhac’s in several crucial ways.
First, for smooth compactly supported perturbations of non-vacuum constant-state initial data, Christodoulou proved that the solution
remains global unless
\upmu
vanishes, i.e., he proved a conditional global existence result. Second, his geometric energy method allowed
for commutator and multiplier vectorfields with time and radial weights, which allowed him to simultaneously study the
interaction between the dispersive tendency of waves and the fact that nonlinearities can focus waves and cause singularities.
Third, for open sets of initial data that do not have to satisfy Alinhac’s non-degeneracy condition, Christodoulou proved that

\upmu
vanishes in finite time, leading to the blowup of
\boldsymbol{\partial}^{2}\Phi
. In particular, even
without

strict convexity of the type shown in Fig.
4
, Christodoulou was able show that there is at least one singular point.
Finally, for open sets of initial data that
satisfy a non-degeneracy assumption that is weaker than Alinhac’s, Christodoulou was able to follow the solution beyond the time
of first blowup and to reveal a portion of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD,
up to the boundary. The portion of the boundary of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD revealed by
[
34
]

was not described explicitly and thus we cannot make straightforward comparisons
with Theorem
3.1
.
The weaker notion of non-degeneracy used by Christodoulou coincides with our notion of
transversal convexity, which we describe in Sect.
5.5
.

Christodoulou’s results
[
34
]
have been extended and applied to other equations, including:

•

Under the assumption of an irrotational and isentropic flow,
the (non-relativistic)
3D
compressible Euler equations
were handled in
[
30
]
.

•

A larger class of wave equations (e.g., without the assumption of Poincaré invariance)
was treated in
[
74
,
92
]
. See also the survey article
[
49
]
, which is centered around
the works
[
34
,
92
]
.

•

Solution regimes that are different than the small, compactly supported data regime were treated in

[
49
,
74
]
.

•

Solutions that exist classically precisely on a past-infinite half-slab were studied in
[
75
]
.

•

Some systems in
1D
involving multiple speeds of propagation were treated in
[
31
,
12
,
13
]
.

•

Other multiple speed systems in multi-dimensions were treated in
[
93
,
95
]
.

•

2D
compressible Euler solutions with vorticity were handled in
[
65
]
with the help of
the new formulation of the flow derived in
[
66
]
,
which yields a non-relativistic analog of Theorem
4.1
for barotropic equations
of state, which by definition are such that the pressure is a function of the density.

•

3D
compressible Euler solutions with vorticity and entropy were treated in
[
67
]
