with the help of the new formulation of the flow derived in
[
94
]
,
which extends the results of
[
66
]
to general equations of state
in which the pressure is a function of the density and entropy.

•

For the
3D
compressible Euler equations with vorticity and entropy,
a complete description of open sets of solutions up to a neighborhood of the boundary of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD
is provided by our series
[
4
,
1
,
3
]
, which collectively yield a
multi-dimensional version of Theorem
3.1
for the non-relativistic equations.
See also Sect.
5.5
.

5.4.
A different approach to multi-dimensional singularity formation

In the works
[
22
,
23
]
, the authors developed a new approach for proving gradient-blowup for
for solutions the
3D
compressible Euler equations under adiabatic equations of state with vorticity
(and entropy in
[
23
]
). Instead of using nonlinear geometric optics, the authors used
modulation parameters to show that for open sets of initial data with large gradients, the
solution forms a gradient singularity in finite time that is a perturbation of a self-similar
Burgers-type shock. The approach
allows one to follow the solution to the time of first blowup, but not further.
It applies to non-degenerate initial data such that within the constant-time hypersurface of first blowup, the singularity occurs
at an isolated point. That is, the approach applies when the singular boundary
\mathcal{B}
and its past boundary
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}

are strictly convex, as in Fig.
4
, and in the context of the figure, it allows one
to follow the solution up to the flat constant-time hypersurface that contains the point

b_{\textnormal{lowest}}
, where the singularity occurs.
Such singularities can be viewed as fluid analogs of the ones studied by Alinhac in his
aforementioned works
[
9
,
8
,
10
,
11
]
on quasilinear wave equations.
See also the precursor work
[
24
]
, in which the authors studied the same problem
for the
2D
compressible Euler equations in azimuthal symmetry, and the work
[
21
]
,
which, in the same symmetry class, constructs unstable self-similar solutions whose cusp-like
behavior at the singularity is non-generic.

Self-similar blowup is a phenomenon that occurs in many other PDEs besides those of fluid mechanics.
In particular, singularity formation modeled on a self-similar
Burgers’-type shock has been proved for various

non-hyperbolic PDEs

[
79
,
38
,
101
,
61
,
76
,
37
,
36
]
.

5.5.
Maximal classical
\mathbf{h}
-globally hyperbolic developments

It is of mathematical and physical interest to study shock-forming solutions in a region of classical existence
that goes beyond the constant-time hypersurface of first blowup, e.g., in a region larger than the one studied by
Alinhac in
[
9
,
8
,
10
,
11
]
,
as we described in Sect.
5.3
.
The holy grail object in this vein is the
maximal (classical)
\mathbf{h}
-globally hyperbolic development
(
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD), which is what we studied in Theorem
3.1

within the class of simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions to the relativistic Euler equations.
Recall that, roughly speaking, the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD the largest possible classical solution +
\mathbf{h}
-globally hyperbolic region
that is uniquely determined by the initial data.
In particular, the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD can have a complicated boundary
that includes points lying to the future of the constant-time hypersurface of first blowup,
as in Figs.
3

and
6
.
Here, “
\mathbf{h}
-globally hyperbolic” means that the development contains a Cauchy hypersurface,
i.e., a surface such that every inextendible
\mathbf{h}
-causal curve
(where
\mathbf{h}
is the acoustical metric) in the development intersects it.
In the important work
[
42
]
, the authors showed that for general quasilinear hyperbolic PDEs,

one cannot ensure uniqueness of the MGHD

until one constructs it and shows that it enjoys some crucial structural properties,
which are global in nature.

In our series
[
4
,
1
,
3
]
, for open sets of initial data for the
3D
compressible Euler equations
leading to shock-forming solutions, we constructed a large (though bounded) portion of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD,
up to the boundary, i.e., in the non-relativistic case without symmetry assumptions,
we proved an analog of Theorem
3.1
.
In particular, these are the first results to fully justify
Fig.
6
for open sets of solutions to the
3D
compressible Euler equations
with vorticity and dynamic entropy.
Although these works are of mathematical interest in themselves, they are also fundamental for setting up
the shock development problem, as we describe in Sect.
5.6
.

Our results
[
4
,
1
,
3
]
exhibit a localized version of the crucial property that the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD
“lies on one side of its boundary,” as
is shown in Fig.
6
.
In
[
42
]
, the authors showed, roughly,
that if this “one-sidedness” property holds globally, then the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD is unique.
They also showed, for a specific hyperbolic PDE and specific initial data, that
uniqueness of the MGHD fails
and the property fails!
As in Theorem
3.1
,
the boundary portion we construct in
[
4
,
1
,
3
]
has two kinds of components:

•

A singular boundary
\mathcal{B}
, constructed in
[
4
]
,
along which the fluid’s gradient blows up, the past boundary of which
is a
2
-dimensional acoustically spacelike
12
12

12

Acoustically spacelike means spacelike with respect to
the acoustical metric of the
3D
compressible Euler equations. Acoustically null means null with respect to the
acoustical metric.

submanifold known as
the crease
and denoted by “
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}
” in Fig.
6
.

•

A Cauchy horizon, constructed in
[
3
]

and denoted by “
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
” in Fig.
6
,
which is an acoustically null
hypersurface that emanates from the crease and along which the solution extends smoothly (except at the crease).
The crease plays the role of the
true initial singularity
in shock-forming solutions.

Our analysis in
[
1
,
3
]
relies on an assumption of
transversal convexity
,
which is a weak, stable form of convexity
that can be read off the initial data and propagated by the flow.
Roughly, transversal convexity means that when
the inverse foliation density

\upmu

(which in the context of
[
1
,
3
]

is an analog of (
151
) or (
157
) for the
3D
compressible Euler equations)
is small,
\upmu
has a positive second derivative in a direction transversal to the level sets of the eikonal function
U
.
For the simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions
we studied in Sect.
3
, transversal convexity is ensured by the estimate
(
102
) and is manifested in
Fig.
3
by the upwards-bending
nature of
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]})
. We highlight the importance of transversal
convexity with the following remarks.

Even for simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions, in the absence of transversal convexity, the
qualitative structure of the singularity can be radically altered.
For example, if instead of the estimate (
102
)
there held
\breve{X}\breve{X}\upmu\equiv 0
at the first singularity,
then in the
(t,x^{1})
-coordinates picture,
an entire continuum of characteristics would fold into a single point,
and the change of variables map
\Upsilon
from Theorem
3.1

would dramatically fail to be an injection. This would be a serious obstacle to even the local
well-posedness of the shock development problem, which we describe in Sect.
5.6
.
This is in contrast to the “favorable” situation shown in
Fig.
3
, in which, thanks to transversal convexity,
the characteristics
graze
the singular boundary
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]})
,
but distinct characteristics do not actually intersect along
\Upsilon(\mathcal{B}_{[-U_{\mbox{\tiny\Radioactivity}},0]})
.

In the context of the
3D
solutions depicted in
Fig.
6
, the transversal convexity is manifested by

\mathcal{B}
being “convex in the
x^{1}
-direction” (i.e., upwards bending in the
x^{1}
direction)
but
not necessarily convex in the
(x^{2},x^{3})
-directions
. Note that if we view plane-symmetric solutions as solutions in
3D

with symmetry, then the corresponding singular boundary
\mathcal{B}
is not strictly convex because of the “symmetric directions”

(x^{2},x^{3})
.

Of the works on shock formation cited in Sect.
5.3
, Christodoulou’s
monograph
[
34
]
on
3D
irrotational and isentropic relativistic fluids
is the only one that follows the solution beyond the time of first blowup.
He used eikonal functions and foliations of spacetime by
\mathbf{h}
-spacelike planes
\Sigma

(more precisely, the
\Sigma
were coordinate planes with respect to the standard Minkowski-rectangular coordinates)
to reveal an implicit portion of the boundary of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD, where, for example,
the portion of the crease
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}
that was revealed is such that the
crease had to lie to the future of any
\Sigma
that is tangent to it.
For initial data such that the crease is a strictly convex subset of Minkowski-rectangular coordinate space
(as in Fig.
4
),
his approach can be used to study the entire crease and a neighborhood of the singular boundary
that emerges from it. However, strict convexity does not hold for all shock-forming solutions. In particular,
as we noted in the previous paragraph,
strict convexity fails for symmetric solutions (roughly, strict convexity will fail in the directions of symmetry)
and general small perturbations of them.
In
[
1
]
, under the weaker assumption of transversal convexity, we construct the entire crease
and a neighborhood of the singular boundary that contains it for solutions with vorticity and dynamic entropy.
To handle the lack of strict convexity, we dynamically construct a new foliation of spacetime
by “rough acoustically spacelike hypersurfaces,”
depicted in Fig.
5
,
that are precisely adapted to the shape of the crease and singular boundary.
We refer to the foliations as “rough” because they are less differentiable than the fluid solution,
which is a key difficulty that has to be overcome in the PDE analysis.
To handle the presence of vorticity and dynamic entropy, we combine the special structure of the
geometric wave-transport-div-curl formulation of the flow derived in
[
94
]
with upgraded versions of the integral
identities derived in
[
4
]
, which in total yield a regularity theory for the vorticity and entropy that
is adapted to the rough foliations
(and hence the shape of the singular boundary)
and that allows one to treat the fluid equations as a perturbation of a wave equation system.
In particular, as in
[
65
,
67
]
, the framework yields (and requires) one extra degree of
differentiability for the vorticity and entropy compared to standard estimates.
See also Sect.
6.9.2
, in which we discuss
the extra differentiability of the vorticity and entropy in the context of the

3D
relativistic Euler equations.

\begin{overpic}[MinterestingCartesiancoord.pdf]
\put(46.0,13.0){$\Sigma_{0}$}
\put(5.0,20.0){$(x^{2},x^{3})$}
\put(1.0,20.0){$t$}
\put(4.5,13.0){$x^{1}$}
\put(45.0,81.0){$\Upsilon(\partial_{-}\mathcal{B})$}
\put(60.0,92.5){$\Upsilon(\mathcal{B})$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 5.
Rough foliations and the singular boundary from
[
1
]
, depicted in Cartesian coordinate space

5.6.
Shock development problem

In Majda’s celebrated works
[
70
,
71
]
, he solved the
shock front problem
for
open sets of solutions to the non-relativistic
3D
compressible Euler equations. Roughly, this means that he considered
piecewise smooth initial data that have a jump discontinuity across a smooth hypersurface, and he then constructed a local weak solution
to the equations and a shock hypersurface such that the solution is piecewise smooth on either side of
shock hypersurface but has a jump discontinuity across it and satisfies the Rankine–Hugoniot condition
and an entropy-type condition.

The
shock development problem
is closely related to the shock front problem
but is laden with additional technical difficulties.
It is the problem of describing how initially smooth solutions form a “first singularity”
(which is the crease, as described in Sect.
5.5
),
from which emerges a shock hypersurface
(which is not known in advance, as in the shock front problem),
and describing the transition of the solution from classical to weak
such that in the “weak solution region,” the solution jumps across the shock hypersurface
and satisfies the Rankine–Hugoniot jump condition and an entropy-type condition. In particular,
in the shock development problem, there is no jump discontinuity in the initial data; rather, the jump
emerges dynamically, starting from the crease.
In problems that are effectively one-dimensional
(and thus energy estimates can be avoided), the shock development
problem has been solved for various hyperbolic PDEs

[
63
,
27
,
52
,
29
,
51
,
20
]
.

The only solution to a multi-dimensional shock development problem without symmetry assumptions was provided by
Christodoulou’s recent breakthrough monograph
[
35
]
,
in which he used nonlinear geometric optics
(i.e., a pair of acoustic eikonal functions, one ingoing and one outgoing)
to solve the
restricted shock development problem

in an arbitrary number of spatial dimensions for the compressible Euler equations and the
relativistic Euler equations. Roughly, the word “restricted” means that he solved an idealized problem in which he
ignored the jump in entropy and vorticity across the shock hypersurface,
thereby producing a weak solution to a hyperbolic PDE system that approximates the true one;
in the real problem, which remains unsolved, the vorticity and entropy must jump across the shock hypersurface.
Compared to the
1D
case, the main new difficulty in multiple spatial dimensions is that one must derive
energy estimates, which are degenerate near the first singularity for reasons related to the energy degeneracies
that occur in the shock formation problem, as we discuss in Sect.
6.9.3
.

We highlight the following key issue, which is a primary motivating factor for our construction
of large portions of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD in
[
4
,
1
,
3
]
:

The setup of the shock development problem
(see
[
29
,
35
]
) is such that the data for
it are an
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD
13
13

13

The shock development problem is local, so to locally study the solution, one only needs
as initial data a portion of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD,
specifically a portion that contains a crease and a portion of the singular boundary and Cauchy horizon that emerge from
it, which we constructed in
[
4
,
1
,
3
]
.

of a shock-forming solution launched by smooth initial data, with a precise description of the
gradient-blowup along a singular boundary, a precise description of the classical solution’s regular behavior
along a Cauchy horizon
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
, and a sharp description of the structure of the “first singularity,” which
in Sect.
5.5
(also in Theorem
3.1
),
we referred to as “the crease” and which we denote by “
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}
” in Fig.
6
.
Crucially,
under the
“transversal convexity” assumption described in Sect.
5.5
,

\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}
is a co-dimension
2
,

\mathbf{h}
-spacelike submanifold equal to the intersection
of the Cauchy horizon and the singular boundary; without this structure, it is not even clear whether the
shock development problem is well-posed. These structures,
as well as the full state of the fluid up to
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B},\,\underline{\mathcal{C}}
,
were
posited
as data in
[
29
,
35
]
.
In
1D
, Theorem
3.1
provides the first rigorous
construction
of these qualitative and quantitative aspects of the data for the shock development problem, starting from smooth initial conditions on a spacelike
Cauchy hypersurface.
For the non-relativistic Euler equations in
3D
and without symmetry assumptions,
our works
[
4
,
1
,
3
]
collectively provide an analog of Theorem
3.1
.

5.7.
Rarefaction waves

In his foundational papers
[
7
,
6
]
, for a large class of
multi-dimensional hyperbolic systems that includes scalar
conservation laws and the compressible Euler equations as special cases,
Alinhac proved local existence and uniqueness for rarefaction wave solutions.
Multi-dimensional rarefaction waves are analogs of a class of solutions to the well-known
Riemann problem in
1D
,
in which the initial data are piecewise smooth and discontinuous,
and the initial discontinuity is immediately smoothed out by the flow.
His approach relied on Nash–Moser estimates to overcome derivative loss
in linearized versions of the equations.
Alinhac proved
[
7
,
6
]
that,
as in the
1D
case, in the corresponding multi-dimensional
region + solution, the initial discontinuity is immediately smoothed out by the flow.
In the important recent works
[
68
,
69
]
,
the authors study the isentropic
2D
compressible
Euler equations with a family of irrotational discontinuous initial data that are (asymmetric) perturbations of plane-symmetric
data for a corresponding
1D
Riemann problem.
For irrotational data,
their main result provides a sharpened version of the local existence and uniqueness results of Alinhac
[
7
,
6
]
.
Their work shows in particular that the
2D
irrotational rarefaction solution is a perturbation of the standard
1D
rarefaction wave solution to the Riemann problem. Compared to
[
7
,
6
]
, the works
[
68
,
69
]
yield two important advances.
First, the techniques of
[
68
,
69
]
avoid a loss of derivatives in a corresponding linearized problem and consequently,
the authors were able to close the energy estimates without Nash–Moser estimates.
Second,
[
68
,
69
]
provide a complete description of the characteristic geometry in the problem.

6.
Some ideas behind the proof of shock formation in
3D
without symmetry

In this section, we provide an outline (without complete proofs) of how to extend some aspects of
Theorem
3.1
to apply to open sets of

3D
relativistic Euler solutions without symmetry, isentropicity, or irrotationality assumptions.
We anticipate that
all
aspects of Theorem
3.1

can be extended.
A key reason behind our expectation is the availability of the new formulation of the flow provided by
Theorem
4.1
,
which is qualitatively similar to the formulation of the non-relativistic flow from
[
94
]
,
which was used in prior related works

[
65
,
67
,
4
,
1
,
3
]

on shocks for the (non-relativistic)
3D
compressible Euler equations.
To keep the discussion short, we only
sketch some key ideas behind the proof of Conjecture
1
,
which essentially concerns studying
3D
solutions until their time of first blowup.
Our discussion here mirrors the strategy we used in our work
[
67
]
,
in which we proved an analog of Conjecture
1

for the non-relativistic
3D
compressible Euler equations.

6.1.
Conjectures for the
3D
relativistic Euler equations

To set the stage, we state three conjectures for the
3D
relativistic Euler equations that are tied to the following figure:

\begin{overpic}[MinterestingCartesian.pdf]
\put(50.0,7.0){$\Sigma_{0}$}
\put(5.5,18.5){$(x^{2},x^{3})$}
\put(0.5,15.0){$t$}
\put(5.0,6.5){$x^{1}$}
\put(43.0,54.0){$\mbox{Crease}$}
\put(49.0,49.5){\rotatebox{90.0}{$=$}}
\put(45.0,45.5){$\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}$}
\put(67.5,52.5){$L$}
\put(62.5,67.5){$\mathcal{B}$}
\put(29.5,68.5){$\underline{\mathcal{C}}$}
\put(10.0,62.0){$\mbox{null}$}
\put(5.0,58.5){$\mbox{generator}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 6.
Conjectured local picture of the
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD for general small perturbations of simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions
to the
3D
relativistic Euler equations in Minkowski-rectangular coordinate space,
with the map
\Upsilon(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})=(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})
suppressed

For the
3D
compressible Euler equations, Fig.
6
has been justified

[
4
,
1
,
3
]
for open sets of initial data.

Conjecture 1

(Time of first blowup for the
3D
relativistic Euler equations with vorticity and entropy)
.

Consider the simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions of Theorem
3.1

as “background solutions” to the
3D
relativistic Euler equations with symmetry. Consider smooth
initial data in
3D
– without symmetry, irrotationality, or isentropicity assumptions –
that are close (in a sufficiently high-order Sobolev space) to the data of one of the background solutions.
Then the perturbed solution forms a shock at a time that is a perturbation of
T_{\textnormal{Shock}}
, i.e.,
a perturbation of the shock-formation-time of the background solution;
the conjectured perturbed first blowup-time is the smallest value of
t
along the crease
\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}

depicted in Fig.
6
.

Conjecture 2

(The structure of the crease and singular boundary for the
3D
relativistic Euler equations with vorticity and entropy)
.

Under the assumptions of Conjecture
1
,
the perturbed solution has a crease and singular boundary that are
perturbations of the crease singular boundary of the
background solution; the background crease and singular boundary are depicted in
Fig.
3
,
while the conjectured perturbed singular boundary and crease are depicted in
Fig.
6
.

Conjecture 3

(The structure of the Cauchy horizon with vorticity and entropy)
.

Under the assumptions of Conjecture
1
,
the perturbed solution has a Cauchy horizon that is a perturbation of the Cauchy horizon of the
background solution; the background Cauchy horizon is depicted in
Fig.
3
,
while the conjectured perturbed Cauchy horizon is depicted in
Fig.
6
.

Conjecture
2
is strictly harder than
Conjecture
1
,
while Conjecture
3
is independent (though closely related).

6.2.
Almost Riemann invariants

To study general (asymmetric) perturbations of simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions, it is convenient
to use analogs of the Riemann invariants from Def.
3.2
.

Definition 6.1

(Almost Riemann invariants)
.

Let
F=F(H,s)
be the solution to
the following transport equation initial value problem:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial H}F(H,s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Hc(H,s)},

\displaystyle F(\overline{H},s)=0,

(152)

where
\overline{H}>0
is the constant
(
3
),
and in (
152
), we are viewing the speed of
sound
c
as a function of
H
and
s
.

We define the
almost Riemann invariants

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}
and
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{(+)}

\displaystyle:=F(H,s)+\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1+\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\sqrt{1+(\mathbf{u}^{1})^{2}}}}{1-\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\sqrt{1+(\mathbf{u}^{1})^{2}}}}\right),

(153a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{(-)}

\displaystyle:=F(H,s)-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1+\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\sqrt{1+(\mathbf{u}^{1})^{2}}}}{1-\frac{\mathbf{u}^{1}}{\sqrt{1+(\mathbf{u}^{1})^{2}}}}\right).

(153b)

Note that for plane-symmetric solutions with
s\equiv 0
, the quantities
defined by (
153a
)–(
153b
)
coincide with the ones defined in (
48a
)–(
48b
).

We view
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}
and
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}
as replacements for
H
and
\mathbf{u}^{1}

that are convenient for studying perturbations of simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions.
In particular, for values of

(H,s)

near

(\overline{H},0)
,
the factor
\frac{1}{Hc(H,s)}
in
(
152
) is positive.
The implicit function theorem then allows one to solve for
H
as a smooth function of

F
and
s
.
We can then use the relations (
153a
)–(
153b
)
to express
(H,\mathbf{u}^{1})
as smooth functions of
(\mathcal{R}_{(+)},\mathcal{R}_{(-)},s)
.
Then, under the algebraic relation (
12d
),
a complete set of state-space variables for the
3D
relativistic Euler equations is given by:

\displaystyle\vec{\Psi}

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{R}_{(+)},\mathcal{R}_{(-)},\mathbf{u}^{2},\mathbf{u}^{3},s).

(154)

Although we have previously used the symbol
\vec{\Psi}
to denote the array

\left(h,\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\mathbf{u}^{3},s\right)
,
in the rest of Sect.
6
,
we use
\vec{\Psi}
to denote the array in (
154
).

Away from isentropic plane-symmetry,

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}

and

\mathcal{R}_{(-)}

no longer solve the homogeneous transport equations
(
49a
)–(
49b
).
However, from (
147a
),
the chain rule,
and Def.
6.1
,
it follows that for general solutions,

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}

and

\mathcal{R}_{(-)}

satisfy geometric wave equations that have the same schematic form
as (
147a
). In the rest of Sect.
6
, we use this fact without
always explicitly mentioning it.

6.3.
Nonlinear geometric optics

As in the
1D
case treated in Sect.
3
,
the study of shock formation in
3D
relies on nonlinear geometric optics,
i.e., multi-dimensional
analogs of the constructions from Sect.
3.6
.
The main object behind the construction is an eikonal function.
The use of eikonal functions to study the
global
properties of multi-dimensional quasilinear hyperbolic
PDE solutions originated Christodoulou–Klainerman’s proof

[
28
]
of the stability of Minkowski spacetime.
See also Sect.
5.3
for a discussion of nonlinear geometric optics in the context
of proofs of multi-dimensional shock formation.

As we discussed at the beginning of Sect.
3.6
,
for simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions, the role of nonlinear geometric optics/geometric coordinates
is to yield a framework/differential structure in which the solution remains smooth all the way up to the shock,
thereby allowing one to reduce the problem of shock formation
to a more traditional problem in which one studies long-time existence.
The same remarks hold in the present context of three spatial dimensions,
although many additional geometric constructions are required. In particular,
all of the geometric quantities we introduce in Sects.
6.3
and
6.8
,
including the acoustic
eikonal function, the geometric coordinates, various geometric vectorfields,
and various geometric energies
are introduced in order
regularize the problem. However, unlike in the simple isentropic plane-symmetric case,
in the present multi-dimensional context, one needs to derive energy estimates, and it turns out that
a remnant of the shock singularity survives, even in the “good” geometric coordinates. The remnant manifests
as singular estimates for the high-order geometric energies, though it is crucial for our approach
that the mid-order-and-below geometric energies remain bounded; see Sect.
6.9.3

for further discussion.

Definition 6.2

(Acoustic eikonal function)
.

An acoustic eikonal function (eikonal function for short) is a solution
to the eikonal equation, which is the following fully nonlinear hyperbolic PDE, where

\mathbf{h}
is the acoustical metric from Def.
2.4
:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}(\vec{\Psi})\partial_{\kappa}U\partial_{\lambda}U

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}

\displaystyle U>0.

(155)

When studying shocks close to plane-symmetry, it is convenient to assume the following
initial condition for
U
, which is the exact same initial condition (
55b
)
that we used in our study of isentropic plane-symmetric solutions:

\displaystyle U|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=-x^{1}.

(156)

We refer to the level sets of
U
as
the characteristics
.
One can show that for isentropic plane-symmetric solutions, the solution “
U
” to
(
55a
)–(
55b
)
coincides with the solution to (
155
)–(
156
).

Definition 6.3

(Inverse foliation density)
.

We define the inverse foliation density of the characteristics to be the following scalar function:

\displaystyle\upmu

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}(\vec{\Psi})\partial_{\kappa}t\partial_{\lambda}U}=\frac{1}{\mathbf{N}U},

(157)

where the second equality in (
157
) follows from (
143b
).

One can show that for isentropic plane-symmetric solutions, the
quantity “
\upmu
” defined by (
57
)
coincides with the one defined in (
157
).
As in Sect.
3
, the formation of a shock occurs when the density of the characteristics becomes
infinite, that is, when
\upmu\downarrow 0
.

6.4.
Geometric coordinates and vectorfield frames tied the eikonal function

In this section, we introduce geometric coordinates and related vectorfields
that are analogs of quantities we used in our study isentropic plane-symmetric solutions.

6.4.1.
Geometric coordinates

Definition 6.4

(Geometric coordinates and the corresponding partial derivative vectorfields)
.

We refer to
\{t,U,x^{2},x^{3}\}
as the
geometric coordinates
on spacetime,
where
U
is the eikonal function and

t
,
x^{2}
, and
x^{3}
are the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates, with
t:=x^{0}
.
We denote the corresponding partial derivatives by
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\frac{\partial}{\partial U},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{2}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{3}}\}

(which are not to be confused with the partial derivatives
\partial_{\alpha}
in the
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
-coordinate system).

Definition 6.5

(Some important submanifolds of geometric coordinate space)
.

•

We denote the level set
\{U=U^{\prime}\}
by
\mathcal{P}_{U^{\prime}}
, and we refer to these
level sets as
the characteristics
; see Fig.
7
.

•

We define
\Sigma_{t^{\prime}}
to be the hypersurface

\{t=t^{\prime}\}
, where
t
is the Minkowski time function.

•

We define the two-dimensional surfaces
\ell_{t^{\prime},U^{\prime}}:=\Sigma_{t^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{P}_{U^{\prime}}
;
see Fig.
7
.

The surfaces
\mathcal{P}_{U}
are characteristic for the wave operator

\square_{\mathbf{h}}
on LHS (
147a
), or equivalently,
null with respect to the acoustical metric
\mathbf{h}
(
\mathbf{h}
-null for short).

As we mentioned in Sect.
3
and just above Def.
6.2
,
a key strategy behind the proof of shock formation is to show that the solution remains
smooth with respect to the geometric coordinates. The blowup of the solution’s gradient in
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
-coordinates,
i.e., the blowup of
\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi}
, occurs when
\upmu
vanishes.
One can prove (see e.g.
[
67
]
*Lemmas 2.22 and 2.23)
the following relationship between the partial derivatives in
the two coordinate systems, where the
a_{\alpha}^{\beta}
are smooth (solution-dependent) functions of the geometric coordinates:

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=a_{\alpha}^{0}\frac{1}{\upmu}\frac{\partial}{\partial U}+a_{\alpha}^{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+a_{\alpha}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{2}}+a_{\alpha}^{3}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{3}}.

(158)

The first product on RHS (
158
) shows in particular why

\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi}
can blow up as
\upmu\downarrow 0
, even when the derivatives of
\vec{\Psi}

with respect to elements of
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\frac{\partial}{\partial U},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{2}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{3}}\}

remain bounded. The use of geometric coordinates allows one to study the problem of shock formation
by using ideas that have traditionally been used in long-time existence problems.
However, different from the
1D
case, in
3D
, some difficult degeneracies can occur
in the energy high-order energy estimates; we discuss this difficulty in more detail in
Sect.
6.9.3
.

6.4.2.
Vectorfield frames

Experience has shown (e.g.,
[
34
]
and the related works cited in Sect.
5.3
)
that in the study of shocks,
to avoid loss of derivatives in commutator estimates and other difficulties,
it is advantageous to use vectorfield frames adapted to the eikonal function.
In particular, once one has constructed
U
,
one can construct the following vectorfield frames, depicted in Fig.
7
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{Z}

\displaystyle:=\{L,\breve{X},Y_{(2)},Y_{(3)}\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}

\displaystyle:=\{L,Y_{(2)},Y_{(3)}\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{Y}

\displaystyle:=\{Y_{(2)},Y_{(3)}\},

(159)

which we describe below.
The frame
\mathscr{Z}
spans the tangent space of geometric coordinate space,
the subset
\mathscr{P}
spans the tangent space of the characteristics
\mathcal{P}_{U}
,
and the subset
\mathscr{Y}
spans the tangent space of the two-dimensional surfaces
\ell_{t,U}
,
i.e., the span of
\mathscr{Y}
is equal to that of
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{2}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{3}}\}
.

\begin{overpic}[Frame.pdf]
\put(82.7,49.7){\large$\displaystyle L$}
\put(72.0,40.2){\large$\displaystyle\breve{X}$}
\put(70.5,48.0){\large$\displaystyle Y_{(2)}$}
\put(54.0,25.0){\large$\displaystyle L$}
\put(38.5,17.0){\large$\displaystyle\breve{X}$}
\put(45.0,27.0){\large$\displaystyle Y_{(2)}$}
\put(49.5,10.0){\large$\mathcal{P}_{-U_{1}}$}
\put(35.0,10.0){\large$\mathcal{P}_{U}$}
\put(5.0,10.0){\large$\mathcal{P}_{U_{2}}$}
\put(22.0,26.0){\large$\displaystyle\upmu\approx 1$}
\put(66.0,67.0){\large$\displaystyle\upmu\ \mbox{is small}$}
\put(55.0,60.5){\large$\displaystyle\ell_{t,U_{2}}$}
\put(-4.0,15.0){\large$\displaystyle\ell_{0,U_{2}}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 7.
The frame
\mathscr{Z}
at two distinct points on
\mathcal{P}_{U}

with the
x^{3}
-direction suppressed

We now briefly describe how to construct the vectorfields. First,
L
is defined by

L^{\alpha}:=-\upmu(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\kappa}(\vec{\Psi})\partial_{\kappa}U
.

L
is clearly
\mathbf{h}
-orthogonal to the characteristics
\mathcal{P}_{U}
,
and by (
155
) and (
157
),
we see that
\mathbf{h}(L,L)=0
and
Lt=1
.
In particular, since
L
is
\mathbf{h}
-orthogonal to
\mathcal{P}_{U}
and
\mathbf{h}
-null,

\mathcal{P}_{U}
is a
\mathbf{h}
-null hypersurface.
One can show that for isentropic plane-symmetric solutions, the
quantity “
L
” defined in this fashion
coincides with the one defined in (
44a
).
However, for general solutions in
3D
, a big difference with the plane-symmetric case is that
there does not exist any explicit formula for
L
in the spirit of (
44a
). That is, in general,

L
depends on the gradient of
U
, which in turns depends implicitly on the fluid solution
via the coefficients in the eikonal equation (
155
).

Next, we define
X
to be the
\Sigma_{t}
-tangent vectorfield that is

\mathbf{h}
-orthogonal to the
\ell_{t,U}
, normalized by
\mathbf{h}(X,X)=1
,
and is such that
U
increases along the integral curves of
X
, i.e.,
XU>0
. We then define

\breve{X}:=\upmu X
, where
\upmu
is defined in (
157
).
It follows that
\mathbf{h}(\breve{X},\breve{X})=\upmu^{2}
. With the help of
(
19
), one can show that
\breve{X}U=1
.
That is,
\breve{X}
is a geometric replacement
14
14

14

(
63
)
shows that in plane-symmetry,
\breve{X}=\frac{\partial}{\partial U}
. However,
in the general
3D
case,
\breve{X}
is equal to
\frac{\partial}{\partial U}
plus corrections belonging to

\mbox{span}\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{2}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{3}}\right\}
.

for
\frac{\partial}{\partial U}
, and among the elements of

\mathscr{Z}
, it is the one vectorfield that is transversal to the characteristics.
One can show that for isentropic plane-symmetric solutions, the
quantities “
X
” and “
\breve{X}
” defined in this fashion
coincide with the ones defined in (
60
).

Next, for
A=2,3
, we define
Y_{(A)}
to be the
\mathbf{h}
-orthogonal projection of

\partial_{A}
onto
\ell_{t,U}
, where
\partial_{A}
is the standard Minkowski-rectangular
partial derivative vectorfield. In our study of isentropic plane-symmetric solutions,
we did not rely on any analogs of the
Y_{(A)}
.

6.4.3.
The first fundamental form of
\ell_{t,U}
and related decompositions

Standard calculations (see, e.g.,
[
87
]
*Equation (2.40b))
imply that the inverse acoustical metric from (
17b
) can be decomposed as follows:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-L^{\alpha}L^{\beta}-L^{\alpha}X^{\beta}-X^{\alpha}L^{\beta}+(g\mkern-8.0mu/^{-1})^{\alpha\beta},

(160)

where
g\mkern-8.0mu/
is the Riemannian metric on
\ell_{t,U}
induced by
\mathbf{h}
, i.e., the first fundamental form of
\ell_{t,U}
.
We view
g\mkern-8.0mu/
to be a spacetime tensor that vanishes on contraction with
L
or
X
and that agrees with
\mathbf{h}
on

\ell_{t,U}
-tangent vectors. Moreover, in (
160
),

(g\mkern-8.0mu/^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}:=(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\gamma}(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\beta\delta}g\mkern-8.0mu/_{\gamma\delta}

is the corresponding inverse first fundamental form.

In what follows,
{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}
denotes the Levi-Civita connection of
g\mkern-8.0mu/
and
|\cdot|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}
denotes the pointwise norms of tensors
with respect to
g\mkern-8.0mu/
, e.g., for scalar functions
f
, we have

|{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}f|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2}=(g\mkern-8.0mu/^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}{{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}_{\mkern-3.0mu\alpha}}f{{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}_{\mkern-3.0mu\beta}}f
.

For future use, we note that for scalar functions
f
, we have the following decomposition
of
\upmu\square_{\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})}f
(see, e.g.,
[
87
]
*Proposition 2.16),
where
{\Delta\mkern-12.0mu/\,}
denotes the covariant Laplacian associated to
g\mkern-8.0mu/
:

\displaystyle\upmu\square_{\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})}f

\displaystyle=-L(\upmu Lf+2\breve{X}f)+\upmu{\Delta\mkern-12.0mu/\,}f+\cdots.

(161)

On RHS (
161
),

\cdots
denotes terms that depend on the first derivatives of
f

and that we will not discuss in detail here.

Remark 6.1

(Comments on the case of more general ambient spacetimes
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
)
.

Under the setup described in Sect.
4.2.5
,
almost all the constructions from this section have natural analogs
for the study of the relativistic Euler equations
on a
\mathbf{g}
-globally hyperbolic spacetime
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
.
That is, consider the time function
t\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
and the foliations
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{t}\Sigma_{t}
.
Here are three concrete examples:

I)

The definition of the eikonal function (
17a
) would remain unchanged

II)

With
\mathbf{N}:=-\mathbf{h}^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}t
,
(
157
) would again yield a suitable definition of the
inverse foliation density (of the characteristics, with respect to
\Sigma_{t}
).

III)

X
could be defined to be a
\Sigma_{t}
-tangent vectorfield that is
\mathbf{h}
-orthogonal to

\ell_{t,U}
and normalized by
\mathbf{h}(X,X)=1
(this would define
X
up to an overall minus sign,
where in Sect.
3
, we defined
X
such that is is “left-pointing”).

We do, however, highlight the following point: general spacetime manifolds have no symmetries and thus
do not generally allow for the existence of the simple plane-symmetric solutions constructed in Sect.
3
.
Nonetheless, we expect the robust geometric framework described above and many of the key ideas presented in the subsequent sections to be useful for proving shock formation results for initial data with large gradients.
We also note that the initial data (
156
)
for the eikonal function generally needs to be
adjusted to fit the regime one is studying.

6.5.
Description of the compactly supported initial data of perturbations of simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions

We now describe the initial data in Conjecture
1
in more detail.
One simply takes any of the “background” simple isentropic plane-symmetric shock-forming solutions from
Theorem
3.1

and then considers a general small (asymmetric) perturbation of its initial data on
\Sigma_{0}
.
For the proof to close, the perturbed initial data need to belong to a sufficiently high order Sobolev space;
we will discuss the issue of regularity in more detail later on.
For convenience, it is easiest to consider perturbed initial data that, like the background data, are
compactly supported in
\Sigma_{0}^{[-U_{1},U_{2}]}:=\Sigma_{0}\cap\{(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})\ |\ -U_{1}\leq U\leq U_{2}\}
.
Then by finite speed of propagation, the corresponding perturbed solution remains spatially compactly supported for all time
and in particular vanishes along the characteristic
\mathcal{P}_{-U_{1}}

(which therefore appears flat in Fig.
6
).
The compact support allows one to avoid difficult boundary terms in the elliptic estimates for the vorticity and entropy; see
Sect.
6.9.2
.

For such initial data, all of the wave variables in the array
\vec{\Psi}
defined in (
154
)
– except for
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}
– will initially have a negligible effect on the dynamics.
Similarly, the fluid variables
\varpi^{\alpha}
,
S_{\alpha}
,

\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}
, and
\mathcal{D}
from Sect.
4.2

will also be small initially. The challenge is to propagate suitable versions of this smallness
all the way up to the shock.

6.6.
Bootstrap assumptions and the regions
\mathcal{M}_{[0,t^{\prime}),[-U_{1},U^{\prime}]}

To prove that a shock forms, one commutes the equations up to
N_{\textnormal{top}}
times
and derives energy estimates up to top-order and
L^{\infty}
estimates at the lower orders.
For reasons described later on, the proof requires
N_{\textnormal{top}}
to be rather large
(i.e., substantially larger than a proof of local well-posedness requires)
and thus the initial data need to belong to a high order Sobolev space.
To close the proof, it is convenient to make
L^{\infty}
bootstrap assumptions
that capture the expectation that at the lower derivative levels,
the solution behaves like a perturbation of a simple
isentropic plane-symmetric solution (in which only
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}
is non-vanishing).
That is, one aims to propagate various aspects of the smallness enjoyed by the
initial data described in Sect.
6.5
.
The bootstrap assumptions are convenient because explicit formulae in the spirit of Cor.
3.6

are not available in
3D
. They capture the expectation that nonetheless, the
3D
solution should obey
estimates that are similar to the ones
proved in Lemma
3.7
and
Theorem
3.1
in the simple isentropic plane-symmetric case.

Before introducing the bootstrap assumptions, we first introduce some notation.
In what follows,
\mathscr{P}^{N}
denotes an arbitrary
order
N
string of elements of the
\mathcal{P}_{U}
-tangent vectorfields
\mathscr{P}
defined in (
159
).
We refer to such vectorfields as “tangential” (to the characteristics
\mathcal{P}_{U}
).
In contrast, the vectorfield
\breve{X}
from (
159
) is transversal
(because it satisfies
\breve{X}U=1
).

The bootstrap assumptions are made on a region

\mathcal{M}_{[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}}),[-U_{1},U_{3}]}

for some
T_{\textnormal{Boot}}>0
and
U_{3}>U_{2}

(discussed further below, where
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are as in Sect.
6.5
),
where in the rest of Sect.
6
,
given any
t^{\prime}>0
and
U^{\prime}\in[-U_{1},U_{2}]
,

\mathcal{M}_{[0,t^{\prime}),[-U_{1},U^{\prime}]}
denotes the following open-at-the-top
slab in geometric coordinate space:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{[0,t^{\prime}),[-U_{1},U^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle:=\{(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})\ |\ t\in[0,t^{\prime})\}\cap\{(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})\ |\ -U_{1}\leq U\leq U^{\prime}\}.

(162)

The proof will show that
\upmu
first vanishes at a time that is a perturbation of
the plane-symmetric blowup-time
T_{\textnormal{Shock}}
from Theorem
3.1
.
That is, in all estimates and arguments, one can safely assume that
0<T_{\textnormal{Boot}}\leq 2T_{\textnormal{Shock}}
.
From this assumption, the assumption that the data are supported in
\Sigma_{0}^{[-U_{1},U_{2}]}
,
and finite speed of propagation, one can compute/choose
U_{3}>U_{2}

such that for
t\in[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}})
, the solution is trivial
15
15

15

For general perturbations of the background solutions,
the perturbed solution will not be spatially supported in
\Sigma_{t}\cap\{U\in[-U_{1},U_{2}]\}
; this is
why we have introduced the parameter
U_{3}
.

at points in
\Sigma_{t}

belonging to the complement of
\Sigma_{t}\cap\{U\in[-U_{1},U_{3}]\}
.

Specifically, for
N
up to mid-order, i.e., roughly, for
N\leq\frac{N_{\textnormal{top}}}{2}
,
one assumes
L^{\infty}

smallness
on
\mathcal{M}_{[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}}),[-U_{1},U_{3}]}
for the following quantities,
which, aside from
L\upmu
, vanish in the case of simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions:

•

The pure
\mathcal{P}_{u}
-tangential derivatives

\mathscr{P}^{N}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}
,
\mathscr{P}^{N}\varpi^{\alpha}
,
\mathscr{P}^{N}S_{\alpha}
,
\mathscr{P}^{N}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}
,

\mathscr{P}^{N}\mathcal{D}
,

\mathscr{P}^{N}L^{i}
, and
\mathscr{P}^{N}\upmu
, except
L\upmu
does not have to be small
(cf. (
119
)).

•

Mixed tangential-transversal derivatives of all these quantities except
\upmu
,
e.g.,
L\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}
.

•

Pure transversal derivatives of all fluid variables excluding
\mathcal{R}_{(+)}
, e.g.,
for
\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(-)}
,
\breve{X}v^{2}
,
\breve{X}\varpi^{\alpha}
,
\breve{X}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}
.

One also assumes
L^{\infty}

boundedness
(not smallness) on
\mathcal{M}_{[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}}),[-U_{1},U_{3}]}

for the following quantities, which are non-zero for the simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions
we studied in Theorem
3.1
:

•

\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}

•

\breve{X}L^{i}
,
L\upmu
.

One also assumes that:

\displaystyle\upmu

\displaystyle>0,

\displaystyle\mbox{on }\mathcal{M}_{[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}}),[-U_{1},U_{3}]}.

(163)

The assumption (
163
) captures that no shock has occurred on
\mathcal{M}_{[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}}),[-U_{1},U_{3}]}
, though
it leaves open the possibility that the shock happens exactly at time
T_{\textnormal{Boot}}
.

The difficult part of the proof is proving energy estimates that allow one to derive, through a combination of Sobolev embedding
and transport estimates, improvements of the bootstrap assumptions.
Then through a standard continuation argument, one can extend the solution
all the way up until the first time that
\upmu
vanishes.

6.7.
The behavior of
\upmu
and the formation of a shock

Given the framework established above and the bootstrap assumptions,
it is not difficult to prove that shocks can form for open sets of initial data that
are perturbations of simple isentropic plane-symmetric data.
We now sketch the argument.
Using the eikonal equation (
155
)
and the bootstrap assumptions of Sect.
6.6

one can show that for perturbations of simple isentropic plane-symmetric solutions,
the inverse foliation density defined in (
157
)
satisfies an evolution equation of the following schematic form
(cf. Lemma
3.5
) on
\mathcal{M}_{[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}}),[-U_{1},U_{3}]}
:

\displaystyle L\upmu(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})

\displaystyle=\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})+\cdots,

(164)

where
\cdots
denotes small error terms.
In addition, using that
L\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}
is small by the bootstrap assumptions,
one can show that on
\mathcal{M}_{[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}}),[-U_{1},U_{3}]}
, we have:

\displaystyle\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})

\displaystyle=\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}(0,U,x^{2},x^{3})+\cdots,

(165)

that is,
\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}
stays close to its initial condition
(cf. (
69
), which shows that in simple isentropic plane-symmetry

\mathcal{R}_{(+)}
depends only on
U
).
Combining (
164
)–(
165
),
we see that on
\mathcal{M}_{[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}}),[-U_{1},U_{3}]}
, we have:

\displaystyle L\upmu(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})

\displaystyle=\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}(0,U,x^{2},x^{3})+\cdots.

(166)

Since
Lt=1
(i.e.,
L=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}
along the integral curves of
L
),
and since the
\ell_{t,U}
-tangential derivative
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{A}}\upmu
is small by the bootstrap assumptions,
we deduce from (
166
) and the fundamental theorem of calculus that
on
\mathcal{M}_{[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}}),[-U_{1},U_{3}]}
, we have:

\displaystyle\upmu(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})

\displaystyle=\upmu(0,U,x^{2},x^{3})+t\left\{\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}(0,U,x^{2},x^{3})+\cdots\right\}.

(167)

Thus, for initial data such that there are points where

\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}(0,U,x^{2},x^{3})
is negative and large enough to dominate the terms
\cdots

on RHS (
167
), we infer from (
167
) that

\min_{\Sigma_{t}}\upmu
will vanish in finite time, and that the time of first vanishing can be controlled
by the initial data.
Moreover, since this argument implies that
|\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}|\neq 0
at the points where
\upmu
vanishes,
and since:

\displaystyle|X\mathcal{R}_{(+)}|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\upmu}|\breve{X}\mathcal{R}_{(+)}|,

(168)

it follows that
|X\mathcal{R}_{(+)}|
blows up like
\frac{C}{\upmu}
at the points where
\upmu
vanishes
(cf.
128
and (
158
)).
In total, these arguments show why
\upmu
can vanish in finite time and reveal how the vanishing is connected to
the blowup of
|X\mathcal{R}_{(+)}|
.

We also note that the above arguments yield the following:

\displaystyle L\upmu(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})<0,

\displaystyle\mbox{at points where }\upmu(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})\mbox{ is close to }0,

(169)

which is an analog of the estimate (
119
) in simple
isentropic plane-symmetry. In particular, at fixed
(U,x^{2},x^{3})
,

\upmu
vanishes linearly in
t
. These facts have important implications for the energy estimates, described below;
see in particular Remark
6.3
.

6.8.
Vectorfield multiplier method and energies

In this section, we describe some basic ingredients that are needed to set up the energy estimates.
To control the solution up to the shock, one needs geometric machinery that is much more advanced than
that of Sect.
2.3.4

and that takes advantage of the structure of the equations provided by Theorem
4.1
.

6.8.1.
Energies and null fluxes for wave equations

In this section, we set up the vectorfield multiplier method for the wave equations (
147a
).

Given a scalar function
f
, we define the energy-momentum tensor associated to it to be the
following symmetric type
\binom{0}{2}
tensor:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}_{\alpha\beta}[f]:=\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}f\mathbf{D}_{\beta}f-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}f\mathbf{D}_{\lambda}f.

(170)

In (
170
) and throughout,
\mathbf{D}
denotes the Levi-Civita connection of
\mathbf{h}
.

Straightforward calculations, based on the symmetry property

\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}\mathbf{D}_{\beta}f=\mathbf{D}_{\beta}\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}f

and the Leibniz rule,
yield that given any
multiplier vectorfield

Z
,
we have the following identity:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}\mathbf{Q}_{\beta\kappa}[f]Z^{\kappa})

\displaystyle=(\square_{\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})}f)Zf+\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}\mathbf{Q}_{\alpha\kappa}{{}^{(Z)}{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\beta\lambda}}},

(171)

where:

\displaystyle{{}^{(Z)}{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\alpha\beta}}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{h}_{\beta\kappa}\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}Z^{\kappa}+\mathbf{h}_{\alpha\kappa}\mathbf{D}_{\beta}Z^{\kappa}

(172)

is the deformation tensor of
Z
(with respect to
\mathbf{h}
).
The identity (
171
) is convenient for bookkeeping in the divergence theorem
when one integrates by parts.

To derive energy-null flux identities for wave equations, we will use (
171
)
with the multiplier vectorfield
Z
equal to
\breve{T}
, which is defined as
follows, where
L
and
\breve{X}
are as in Sect.
6.4.2
:

\displaystyle\breve{T}

\displaystyle:=(1+2\upmu)L+2\breve{X}.

(173)

One can compute that
\mathbf{h}(\breve{T},\breve{T})=-4\upmu(1+\upmu)

and thus
\breve{T}
is
\mathbf{h}
-timelike whenever
\upmu>0
. The
\mathbf{h}
-timelike
nature of
\breve{T}
is crucial for generating coercive energies.
The
\upmu
-weights in (
173
) have been carefully placed.
Recall that
\breve{X}=\upmu X
, where
\mathbf{h}(X,X)=1
. Thus,
\breve{X}
effectively contains a
\upmu
-weight.

To close the
L^{2}
estimates, one needs energies on:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t^{\prime}}^{[-U_{1},U^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle:=\{(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})\ |\ t=t^{\prime}\}\cap\{(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})\ |\ -U_{1}\leq U\leq U^{\prime}\}

(174)

and null fluxes on:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{U^{\prime}}^{[0,t^{\prime})}

\displaystyle:=\{(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})\ |\ 0\leq t<t^{\prime}\}\cap\{(t,U,x^{2},x^{3})\ |\ U=U^{\prime}\}.

(175)

Recall that
\mathbf{N}
is the future-directed
\mathbf{h}
-unit normal to
\Sigma_{t}
defined in
(
143b
). The strength of our energies on the
\Sigma_{t}^{[-U_{1},U]}
is determined by the following
identity (see, e.g.,
[
87
]
*Lemma 3.4):

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}[f](\breve{T},\mathbf{N})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+2\upmu)\upmu(Lf)^{2}+2\upmu(\breve{X}f)Lf+2(\breve{X}f)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(1+2\upmu)\upmu|{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}f|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2}.

(176)

Similarly, the strength of our null fluxes on the
\mathcal{P}_{U}^{[0,t)}
is determined by the following identity
(again, see, e.g.,
[
87
]
*Lemma 3.4):

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}[f](\breve{T},L)

\displaystyle:=(1+\upmu)(Lf)^{2}+\upmu|{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}f|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2}.

(177)

These identities (
176
)–(
177
)
motivate the following definition.

Definition 6.6

(Wave equation energies and null-fluxes)
.

Given a scalar function
f
, we define the associated energy
\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[f]

and null flux
\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[f]
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[f](t,U)\\
&:=\int_{\Sigma_{t}^{[-U_{1},U]}}\left\{\frac{1}{2}(1+2\upmu)\upmu(Lf)^{2}+2\upmu(\breve{X}f)Lf+2(\breve{X}f)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(1+2\upmu)\upmu|{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}f|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2}\right\}\,{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}U^{\prime},\end{split}

(178a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[f](t,U)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{U}^{[0,t)}}\left\{(1+\upmu)(Lf)^{2}+\upmu|{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}f|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2}\right\}\,{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime},

(178b)

where
g\mkern-8.0mu/
and
|{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}f|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2}
are as in Sect.
6.4.3
,
and on each
\ell_{t^{\prime},U^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{\mbox{det}g\mkern-8.0mu/(t^{\prime},U^{\prime},x^{2},x^{3})}\,\mathrm{d}x^{2}\mathrm{d}x^{3},

(179)

is the canonical volume form induced by
g\mkern-8.0mu/
.
In particular, the determinant in (
179
) is taken relative to the coordinates

(x^{2},x^{3})
on
\ell_{t^{\prime},U^{\prime}}
.

Remark 6.2

(
\upmu
weights and error terms)
.

Note that one
L
-derivative involving term on RHS (
178b
)
lacks
a
\upmu
-weight. This means that

\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[f](t,U)
is strongly coercive in the
L
-derivatives even in regions where

\upmu
is small. This is crucial for controlling
L
-derivative-involving energy estimate error terms that lack
\upmu
-weights.
In contrast, all terms on RHS (
178a
)–(
178b
) involving
the
{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}
-derivatives of
f
have a
\upmu
-weight.
Hence, to control dangerous

{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}
-derivative-involving energy estimate error terms that lack
\upmu
-weights,
one must rely on the special structure afforded by the coercive spacetime integral described in
Remark
6.3
.

Using (
171
) with
Z:=\breve{T}
,
one can prove the following lemma (see, e.g.,
[
87
]
*Proposition 3.5), which forms
the starting point for the
L^{2}
-analysis of solutions to the wave equations (
147a
).

Lemma 6.1

(Energy-null flux identity for wave equations)
.

For scalar functions
f
on
\mathcal{M}_{[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}}),[-U_{1},U_{3}]}

that vanish along
16
16

16

This vanishing assumption holds under the compact support assumptions on the
initial data stated in Sect.
6.5
.

\mathcal{P}_{-U_{1}}
,
the following energy-null flux identity holds for
(t,U)\in[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}})\times[-U_{1},U_{3}]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[f](t,U)+\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[f](t,U)&=\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[f](0,U)\\
&\ \ -\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{[0,t),[-U_{1},U]}}\upmu(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{h}^{-1})^{\kappa\lambda}\mathbf{Q}_{\alpha\kappa}{{}^{(\breve{T})}{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\beta\lambda}}}\,{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}U^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\\
&\ \ -\int_{\mathcal{M}_{[0,t),[-U_{1},U]}}(\breve{T}f)(\upmu\square_{\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})}f)\,{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}U^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}.\end{split}

(180)

Remark 6.3

(The coercive spacetime integral
\mathbb{K}[f](t,U)
)
.

Upon decomposing the
\mathbf{Q}
-involving integral on RHS (
180
)
relative to the vectorfields in
\mathscr{Z}
,
one finds that it contains the following term, which, after moving it to LHS (
180
),
takes the following form:

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}[f](t,U)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{[0,t),[-U_{1},U]}}[L\upmu]_{-}|{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}f|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}U^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}.

(181)

In (
181
),
[z]_{-}:=\max\{0,-z\}
denotes the negative part of
z
.
Importantly, the discussion in Sect.
6.7
shows that

L\upmu
is negative in regions close to the shock (i.e., in regions where
\upmu
is small)
and thus in such regions,
\mathbb{K}[f](t,U)
,
which we again emphasize has the favorable sign (
181
)
when moved to LHS (
180
),
yields
non-
\upmu
-weighted spacetime
L^{2}
control

of
|{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}f|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2}
. As we mentioned above,
the control afforded by this term is crucial for controlling error terms in the energy estimates
that depend on
{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}f
but lack
\upmu
-weights.

6.8.2.
Energies and null fluxes for transport equations

We now define energies and null fluxes for transport equations of the form
\mathbf{B}f=\cdots
,
where the vectorfield
\mathbf{B}
is defined in (
143a
).
The energies and null fluxes are relevant for controlling solutions to the transport
equations from Theorem
4.1
.

Specifically, motivated by the identities
(
145
)–(
146
),
we define the following energies and null fluxes.

Definition 6.7

(Transport equation energies and null-fluxes)
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Transport})}[f](t,U)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Sigma_{t}^{U}}\upmu f^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}U^{\prime},

(182a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Transport})}[f](t,U)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{U}^{[0,t)}}[-\mathfrak{n}\bm{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{B},L)]f^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}.

(182b)

Note that the discussion below (
146
) implies that
the factor
-\mathfrak{n}\bm{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{B},L)

on RHS (
182b
) is positive, which of course is important
for the coercivity of
\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Transport})}[f](t,U)
.
For the same reasons given in Sect.
6.8.1
,
it is crucially important that RHS (
182b
) lacks a
\upmu
-weight.

By applying the divergence theorem to the vectorfield

f^{2}\mathbf{B}
on
\mathcal{M}_{[0,t),[-U_{1},U]}
,
using (
145
)–(
146
),
and using that in geometric coordinates we have (see, e.g.,
[
92
]
*Corollary 3.47):

\displaystyle\sqrt{|\mbox{det}\mathbf{h}|}\mathrm{d}x^{2}\mathrm{d}x^{3}\mathrm{d}U^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\upmu{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}U^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime},

(183)

one can prove the following lemma, which forms
the starting point for the
L^{2}
-analysis of solutions to
the transport equations (
147b
)–(
147c
).

Lemma 6.2

(Energy-null flux identity for transport equations)
.

For scalar functions
f
on
\mathcal{M}_{[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}}),[-U_{1},U_{3}]}

that vanish along
\mathcal{P}_{-U_{1}}
(Footnote
16
applies here as well),
the following energy-null flux identity holds for
(t,U)\in[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}})\times[-U_{1},U_{3}]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Transport})}[f](t,U)+\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Transport})}[f](t,U)&=\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Transport})}[f](0,U)\\
&\ \ +\int_{\mathcal{M}_{[0,t),[-U_{1},U]}}\upmu(\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}\mathbf{B}^{\alpha})f^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}U^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\\
&\ \ +\int_{\mathcal{M}_{[0,t),[-U_{1},U]}}2f(\upmu\mathbf{B}f)\,{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}U^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}.\end{split}

(184)

6.8.3.
Commutator method and the shock-driving terms

To derive suitable energy estimates,
one commutes
\upmu
-weighted
17
17

17

The
\upmu
-weights allow one to avoid problematic error terms,
and they are compatible with the
\upmu
-weights in Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2
.

versions
of the equations of Theorem
4.1
with
the elements of the
\mathcal{P}_{U}
-tangent subset
\mathscr{P}
defined in (
159
)
and then applies the energy-null flux identities of Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2

and the elliptic estimates described in Sect.
6.9.2
.
In particular, it has been understood since
[
67
]
that to close the energy estimates in a shock formation problem,
one does
not
need to commute the equations
with the vectorfield
\breve{X}
, which is transversal to the characteristics.
We stress that it does not seem possible to close the estimates by commuting the equations with
the Minkowski-rectangular partial derivatives
\partial_{\alpha}
;
such an approach would generate error terms that we do not know how to control.

Although one must commute with all the elements of
\mathscr{P}
to close the energy estimates,
the most difficult terms arise from commuting the wave equations (
147a
)
with a string of elements of the
\ell_{t,U}
subset
\mathscr{Y}
defined in (
159
).
Schematically, for
\Psi\in\vec{\Psi}
,
with
\mathscr{Y}^{N}
denoting an order
N
string of elements of
\mathscr{Y}
,
we have:

\displaystyle\upmu\square_{\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})}\mathscr{Y}^{N}\Psi

\displaystyle=(\breve{X}\Psi)\cdot\mathscr{Y}^{N}{\mbox{tr}}_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\upchi+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\mathscr{Y}^{N}\mathcal{C}+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\mathscr{Y}^{N}\mathcal{D}+\cdots,

(185)

where

\upchi
is the null second fundamental form of
\mathcal{P}_{U}
,

{\mbox{tr}}_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\upchi
denotes its trace with respect to
g\mkern-8.0mu/
, and

\cdots
denotes easier error terms.

The
\mathscr{Y}^{N}\mathcal{C}
- and
\mathscr{Y}^{N}\mathcal{D}
-involving terms
on RHS (
185
) clearly arise from the first term on RHS (
147a
),
and we will discuss how to control them in Sect.
6.9.2
.
We refer to the proofs of
[
87
]
*Lemmas 2.18 and 4.2
for details on the origin of the commutator term
(\breve{X}\Psi)\cdot\mathscr{Y}^{N}{\mbox{tr}}_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\upchi
,
which we describe how to control just below; this term would lead to the loss of a derivative at the top order
if handled in a naive fashion.

Before proceeding, we first highlight that we have relegated all null-form-involving terms
on RHS (
147a
) to the terms
\cdots
on RHS (
185
).
The reason is that one can show (see
[
65
]
*Lemma 2.56)
that
\mathbf{h}
-null forms (see Def.
4.6
) enjoy the following good decomposition,
expressed schematically, relative to the frame

\mathscr{Z}
(
159
), where
P
schematically denotes elements of
\mathscr{P}
:

\displaystyle\upmu\mathfrak{Q}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi,\boldsymbol{\partial}\psi)

\displaystyle=\upmu P\phi\cdot P\psi+\breve{X}\phi\cdot P\psi+\breve{X}\psi\cdot P\phi.

(186)

The key point is that there are not any terms on RHS (
186
)
that are proportional to
\breve{X}\psi\cdot\breve{X}\phi
; signature considerations imply that
such terms, if present, would be multiplied by a dangerous factor of
\frac{1}{\upmu}
.
Such a factor would become singular as
\upmu\downarrow 0
, which would spoil the philosophy
of proving that the solution remains quite regular in geometric coordinates, even as
\upmu
vanishes.
A decomposition similar to (
186
) also holds
for the up-to-top-order derivatives of the
\upmu
-weighted
\mathbf{h}
-null forms.
We stress that the absence of terms proportional to
\breve{X}\psi\cdot\breve{X}\phi

on RHS (
186
) is a statement about the

full nonlinear structure
of the
\mathbf{h}
-null forms from Def.
4.6
.
In particular, this notion of null form is tied to the acoustical metric
\mathbf{h}

and is stronger than the “classic null condition” established by Klainerman
in
[
60
]
, which is tied to the Minkowski metric and is indifferent to the
structure of most cubic nonlinearities.

Remark 6.4

(The shock-driving terms are hidden in
\square_{\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})}\Psi
)
.

The upshot is that all terms in (
147a
) that drive the formation of the shock
are hidden in the covariant wave operator term
\square_{\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})}\Psi
on the LHS.
More precisely, the shock-driving terms are semilinear Riccati-type terms of type
X\Psi\cdot X\Psi

(cf. Sect.
3.5
)
that become visible if one first expands LHS (
147a
) relative
to the Minkowski-rectangular coordinates
and then decomposes the semilinear terms with respect to the frame
\mathscr{Z}
from (
159
).

In view of the above discussion, it follows that the goal is to show that
all terms on RHS (
185
) are error terms that
do not interfere with the shock formation processes.
As we have alluded to earlier, this is indeed possible except at the high derivative levels,
where the difficult regularity theory of the eikonal function
(in particular, the difficult regularity theory of
\upchi
)
leads to singular high-order estimates;
see Sect.
6.9.3
.

We now explain how to control the first product on RHS (
185
).
One starts with the Raychaudhuri equation
[
81
]
,
which plays an important role in mathematical General Relativity,
and which takes the following form in the present context:

\displaystyle L{\mbox{tr}}_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\upchi

\displaystyle=\frac{L\upmu}{\upmu}{\mbox{tr}}_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\upchi-\mathbf{Ric}_{LL}-|\upchi|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2}.

(187)

In (
187
),
\mathbf{Ric}
is the Ricci curvature of
the acoustical metric
\mathbf{h}
and
\mathbf{Ric}_{LL}:=\mathbf{Ric}_{\alpha\beta}L^{\alpha}L^{\beta}
.
The difficulty is that since
\mathbf{h}=\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})
,

\mathbf{Ric}
depends on the second derivatives of
\vec{\Psi}
. This suggests that
control of the term
\mathscr{Y}^{N}{\mbox{tr}}_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\upchi
on
RHS (
185
) requires control over
N+2
derivatives of
\vec{\Psi}
,
which is one too many derivatives to be compatible with the regularity for
\vec{\Psi}

afforded by energy estimates for the wave equation (
185
),
i.e, one has to worry about losing a derivative at the top-order.

Fortunately, based on ideas originating in
[
28
,
54
]
,
there is a way to avoid the loss of derivatives by combining the special structure of

\mathbf{Ric}_{LL}

with the special structures of the equations of Theorem
4.1
.
We will briefly explain the main ideas of the argument.
First, one decomposes
\mathbf{Ric}_{LL}
to deduce an
algebraic identity that can schematically be expressed as:

\displaystyle\upmu\mathbf{Ric}_{LL}

\displaystyle=L\left\{\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot L\vec{\Psi}+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot{\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}\vec{\Psi}\right\}+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot{\Delta\mkern-12.0mu/\,}\vec{\Psi}+\cdots,

(188)

where
\mathrm{f}
schematically denotes a smooth function that is allowed to vary from line to line
and
\cdots
denotes lower order terms that depend on the first derivatives of
\vec{\Psi}
.
Next, one uses (
161
) and (
164
)
to replace the term

\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\cdot{\Delta\mkern-12.0mu/\,}\vec{\Psi}

on RHS (
188
) with
a perfect
L
-derivative term plus
\upmu\square_{\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})}\vec{\Psi}
plus lower-order terms.
One then uses the relativistic Euler wave equation (
147a
) to replace

\upmu\square_{\mathbf{h}(\vec{\Psi})}\vec{\Psi}
with
the inhomogeneous terms on RHS (
147a
).
Combining with (
187
) and bringing all perfect
L
-derivative terms to the left,
we deduce a transport equation that can schematically be expressed as:

\displaystyle L\left\{\upmu{\mbox{tr}}_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\upchi+\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\breve{X}\vec{\Psi}+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})L\Psi+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi}){\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}\Psi\right\}

\displaystyle=-\upmu|\upchi|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2}+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\mathcal{C}+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\mathcal{D}+\cdots,

(189)

where
\cdots
denotes easier error terms that involve

\varpi
,
S
, and the first derivatives of
\vec{\Psi}
.
In particular,
RHS (
189
) does not involve the second derivatives of

\vec{\Psi}
, which is its main advantage compared to equation (
187
).

Similar results hold for the commuted equations,
i.e., given any order
N
string of
\ell_{t,U}
-tangent vectorfields
\mathscr{Y}^{N}
,
with
{{}^{(\mathscr{Y}^{N})}\mkern-3.0mu\mathscr{X}}
denoting the “fully modified” order
N

version of
{\mbox{tr}}_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\upchi
defined by:

\displaystyle{{}^{(\mathscr{Y}^{N})}\mkern-3.0mu\mathscr{X}}

\displaystyle:=\upmu\mathscr{Y}^{N}{\mbox{tr}}_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\upchi+\mathscr{Y}^{N}\left\{\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\breve{X}\vec{\Psi}+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})L\Psi+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi}){\nabla\mkern-14.0mu/\,}\Psi\right\},

(190)

one can derive a transport equation of the following schematic form
(see, e.g., the proof of
[
92
]
*Proposition 11.10 for the main ideas behind the proof):

\displaystyle L{{}^{(\mathscr{Y}^{N})}\mkern-3.0mu\mathscr{X}}

\displaystyle=-\upmu\mathscr{Y}^{N}(|\upchi|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2})+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\mathscr{Y}^{N}\mathcal{C}+\upmu\mathrm{f}(\vec{\Psi})\mathscr{Y}^{N}\mathcal{D}+\cdots.

(191)

Equation (
191
) is an order
N
version of equation (
189
)
that allows one to control
{{}^{(\mathscr{Y}^{N})}\mkern-3.0mu\mathscr{X}}
without derivative loss,
assuming that one can adequately control RHS (
191
).
In Sect.
6.9.2
, we will explain how to control the

\mathscr{Y}^{N}\mathcal{C}
- and
\mathscr{Y}^{N}\mathcal{D}
-involving terms
on RHS (
191
).
One remaining difficulty is that the term

\upmu\mathscr{Y}^{N}(|\upchi|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2})

on RHS (
191
)
involves the full tensor
\upchi
, rather than just the trace part featured on LHS (
191
).
However, one can control this term using
a strategy that goes back to
[
28
,
54
]
. Specifically, one can use elliptic estimates
on the two-dimensional surfaces
\ell_{t,U}
, based on the Codazzi equations from geometry,
to control the
\ell_{t,U}
-tangent derivatives of

|\upchi|_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}^{2}
in terms of the
\ell_{t,U}
-tangent derivatives of

{\mbox{tr}}_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\upchi
plus error terms with an admissible amount of regularity;
similar results hold up to top-order, and we refer, for example,
to the proof
[
92
]
*Lemma 20.20 for the main ideas behind the analysis.
In total, the approach described above allows one to control the product
(\breve{X}\Psi)\cdot\mathscr{Y}^{N}{\mbox{tr}}_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\upchi

on RHS (
185
) in appropriate
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces.
However, the argument introduces a difficult factor of
\frac{1}{\upmu}
into the top-order estimates,
as we explain in Sect.
6.9
.

6.9.
Energy estimates

In this section, we will describe some features of the proof of the energy estimates,
which are the most technical and difficult aspect of studying shock formation.

6.9.1.
Transport variable energy estimates

We now explain how to derive energy estimates for

(\varpi,S)
, which solve the transport equation
(
147b
). These estimates are relatively easy to derive.
Let
\mathscr{P}^{N}
denote an arbitrary
order
N
string of elements of the
\mathcal{P}_{U}
-tangent vectorfields
\mathscr{P}
defined in (
159
).
One multiplies equation (
147b
) by
\upmu
, commutes with

\mathscr{P}^{N}
for
N\leq N_{\textnormal{top}}
, and uses the bootstrap assumptions and the transport equation energy
identity (
184
) to deduce
that for
(t,U)\in[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}})\times[-U_{1},U_{3}]
, we have:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Transport})}[\mathscr{P}^{\leq N}(\varpi,S)](t,U)+\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Transport})}[\mathscr{P}^{\leq N}(\varpi,S)](t,U)\\
&\lesssim\mbox{data}+\int_{\mathcal{M}_{[0,t),[-U_{1},U]}}\mbox{$\upmu$-regular $\vec{\Psi}$ terms}\,{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}U^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}+\int_{-U_{1}}^{U}\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Transport})}[\mathscr{P}^{\leq N}(\varpi,S)](t,U^{\prime})\mathrm{d}U^{\prime}.\end{split}

(192)

From (
192
) and Grönwall’s inequality in
U
, we deduce that:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Transport})}[\mathscr{P}^{\leq N_{\textnormal{top}}}(\varpi,S)](t,U)+\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Transport})}[\mathscr{P}^{\leq N_{\textnormal{top}}}(\varpi,S)](t,U)\\
&\lesssim\mbox{data}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{M}_{[0,t),[-U_{1},U]}}\mbox{$\upmu$-regular $\vec{\Psi}$ terms}\,{\mathrm{d}\varpi_{g\mkern-8.0mu/}\,}\mathrm{d}U^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime},\end{split}

(193)

where here and throughout, “
data
” denotes a small term that depends on the initial data and that measures the perturbation
of the data away from simple isentropic plane-symmetric data.
The terms “
\upmu
-regular
\vec{\Psi}
terms
” in (
193
)
are easily controllable by the wave energies and null fluxes, and in particular,
no singular factor of
\frac{1}{\upmu}
is present in these terms.
Hence, the estimate (
193
) shows that the behavior of

\mathscr{P}^{\leq N_{\textnormal{top}}}(\varpi,S)
is effectively determined by the behavior of
the wave variables
\vec{\Psi}
(defined in (
154
)).
We clarify that even though there is no singular factor
of
\frac{1}{\upmu}
present in the
\upmu
-regular
\vec{\Psi}
terms
, these terms
can still blow up at the high derivative levels as
\upmu\downarrow 0
, for reasons we
describe in Sect.
6.9.3
.
Hence, in view of the coupling between the transport and wave energies shown by
(
193
), we see that the blowup of the
\vec{\Psi}
-energies can cause
the blowup of the
(\varpi,S)
-energies.

6.9.2.
Elliptic-hyperbolic estimates for the vorticity and entropy

Recall that the terms
\mathscr{Y}^{N}\mathcal{C}
and
\mathscr{Y}^{N}\mathcal{D}

appear on RHSs (
185
) and (
191
).
To close the energy estimates, we need to control
\mathscr{P}^{\leq N_{\textnormal{top}}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
in
L^{2}
.
These terms would lead to a loss of derivatives at the top-order if handled in a naive fashion.
There are several reasons why one cannot control these terms
by using only the same transport equation energy methods
we used to derive (
193
).
One reason is that Def.
4.4

shows that from the point of view of regularity, we have

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\sim\boldsymbol{\partial}(\varpi,S)+\cdots
;
since the inhomogeneous terms in the transport equation (
147b
)
satisfied by
(\varpi,S)
suggest (incorrectly, as it fortunately turns out)
that
\boldsymbol{\partial}(\varpi,S)

have at best the same regularity as
\boldsymbol{\partial}^{2}\vec{\Psi}
,
this is formally inconsistent (from the point of view of regularity)
with having
\boldsymbol{\partial}(\varpi,S)
as a source
term in the wave equations (
147a
) for
\vec{\Psi}
.
A second reason is that
the transport equations (
147c
) satisfied by

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
feature the inhomogeneous terms

\mathfrak{Q}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\varpi,\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi})

and

\mathfrak{Q}(\boldsymbol{\partial}S,\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi})
,
which depend on the
general
first derivatives
of
(\varpi,S)
, rather than the special combinations of first derivatives of

(\varpi,S)
present in the definitions
(
140a
)–(
140b
)
of
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
.
That is, due to these inhomogeneous terms,
(
147c
) cannot be treated as a pure transport equation in

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
.

To overcome the difficulties highlighted in the previous paragraph,
one can treat (
147b
)–(
147d
) as a coupled transport-div-curl system that yields

L^{2}
-control of

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})

and

(\boldsymbol{\partial}\varpi,\boldsymbol{\partial}S)
,
where
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
are controlled with hyperbolic transport energy estimates in the spirit of
(
193
), and the elliptic estimates are used only
to handle the inhomogeneous terms

\mathfrak{Q}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\varpi,\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi})

and

\mathfrak{Q}(\boldsymbol{\partial}S,\boldsymbol{\partial}\vec{\Psi})

on RHS (
147c
).
Similar results hold up to top-order and yield
L^{2}
control over

\boldsymbol{\partial}\mathscr{P}^{\leq N_{\textnormal{top}}}(\varpi,S)

and

\mathscr{P}^{\leq N_{\textnormal{top}}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
.
One key difficulty in this argument is that to obtain elliptic estimates on the spacelike hypersurfaces

\Sigma_{t}
, one needs to extract a
spatial
div-curl subsystem from
(
147b
)–(
147d
); the difficulty is that
equations (
147b
)–(
147d
)
appear to involve
spacetime
div-curl equations.
Nevertheless, by splitting various derivative operators into a
\mathbf{B}
-parallel part
and a
\Sigma_{t}
-tangent part, one can extract the desired
spatial div-curl subsystem; we refer to the proof of
[
41
]
*Lemma 9.21 for details on this extraction
in the context of a local well-posedness argument.
We also refer to
[
67
]
*Sections 11.2 and 11.3 for similar but simpler elliptic estimates
in the context of shock formation for the non-relativistic
3D
compressible Euler equations.

The argument described in the previous paragraph applies when
the initial data of the vorticity and entropy are compactly supported, which is the case for the initial data described in
Sect.
6.5
.
The key point is that under compact support,
one can avoid
boundary terms
in the elliptic estimates (the boundary terms vanish thanks to the compact support).
To treat solutions that are not (spatially) compactly supported,
one would need to handle the boundary terms that arise in the elliptic estimates.
In the case of non-relativistic
3D
compressible Euler equations,
based on the special structures found in the formulation of the flow derived in
[
94
]
,
localized spacetime “elliptic-hyperbolic” integral identities for the vorticity and entropy were derived
in
[
4
]
. The identities of
[
4
]
allow one,
in the non-relativistic case, to handle the boundary terms. In particular, in
[
1
,
3
]
,
we used specialized versions of those identities to study the structure of
\mathbf{h}
-MGHD for the
non-relativistic
3D
compressible Euler equations.
To extend these results to
3D
relativistic Euler solutions without compact support,
one would need to derive relativistic analogs of the integral identities from
[
4
]
.

6.9.3.
The wave energy estimate hierarchy

We now discuss the energy estimates for
the wave variables
\vec{\Psi}
, defined in (
154
).
As before, let
\mathscr{P}^{N}
denote an arbitrary
order
N
string of elements of the
\mathcal{P}_{U}
-tangent vectorfields
\mathscr{P}
defined in (
159
).
The main difficulty in closing the energy estimates in multi-dimensions is that the best estimates we know
how to derive allow for the possibility that the top-order energies
blow up as
\upmu\downarrow 0
.
Before proceeding, for notational convenience, we define:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{N}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)&:=\max_{\Psi\in\vec{\Psi}}\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{N}\Psi](t,U),\\
\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{N}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)&:=\max_{\Psi\in\vec{\Psi}}\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{N}\Psi](t,U),\\
\mathbb{K}[\mathscr{P}^{N}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)&:=\max_{\Psi\in\vec{\Psi}}\mathbb{K}[\mathscr{P}^{N}\Psi](t,U),\end{split}

(194)

where
\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}
and
\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}
are as in Def.
6.6

and
\mathbb{K}
is as in Remark
6.3
.

The estimates for the wave energies and null fluxes take the following hierarchical form
on
(t,U)\in[0,T_{\textnormal{Boot}})\times[-U_{1},U_{3}]

(see, e.g.,
[
65
]
*Proposition 14.1 for complete proofs in the case of
the
2D
non-relativistic compressible Euler equations with vorticity):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{N_{\textnormal{top}}}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)+\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{N_{\textnormal{top}}}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)+\mathbb{K}[\mathscr{P}^{N_{\textnormal{top}}}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)\\
&\leq\mbox{data}\cdot\upmu_{\star}^{-A}(t,U),\end{split}

(195a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{N_{\textnormal{top}}-1}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)+\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{N_{\textnormal{top}}-1}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)+\mathbb{K}[\mathscr{P}^{N_{\textnormal{top}}-1}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)\\
&\leq\mbox{data}\cdot\upmu_{\star}^{-(A-2)}(t,U),\end{split}

(195b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{N_{\textnormal{top}}-2}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)+\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{N_{\textnormal{top}}-2}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)+\mathbb{K}[\mathscr{P}^{N_{\textnormal{top}}-2}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)\\
&\leq\mbox{data}\cdot\upmu_{\star}^{-(A-4)}(t,U),\end{split}

(195c)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{[1,\frac{A}{2}]}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)+\mathbb{F}_{(\textnormal{Wave})}[\mathscr{P}^{[1,\frac{A}{2}]}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)+\mathbb{K}[\mathscr{P}^{[1,\frac{A}{2}]}\vec{\Psi}](t,U)\\
&\leq\mbox{data},\end{split}

(195d)

where as before,
“
